non-terminal starting vertices, but each unordered graph is counted twice).

Under the uniform distribution, the single-step reward is

{\mathcal{R}}_{\text{single}}({\mathcal{D}}^{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathrm{unif}}},\theta)=\frac{1}{|\mathrm{supp}({\mathcal{D}}^{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathrm{unif}}})|}\sum_{s\in\mathrm{supp}({\mathcal{D}}^{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathrm{unif}}})}\Pr\big(\mathrm{TF}(s;\theta)=y^{*}(s)\big).

If greedy decoding fails on input
s^{*}
, then
\arg\max_{y}\mathrm{TF}(s^{*};\theta)_{y}\neq y^{*}(s^{*})
, which implies
\Pr(\mathrm{TF}(s^{*};\theta)=y^{*}(s^{*}))\leq 1/2
(since the correct answer does not have the maximal logit). Thus at least one term in the sum is at most
1/2
, yielding

{\mathcal{R}}_{\text{single}}({\mathcal{D}}^{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathrm{unif}}},\theta)\leq 1-\frac{1}{2\cdot(2n)!\cdot(n-1)}=1-\frac{1}{(2n)!\cdot 2(n-1)}.

∎

We also utilize the decision version of the two-chain endpoint problem (which returns true/false for a pair of vertices depending on whether the latter is the endpoint of the chain containing the former); this will be expanded upon in
Section

D.7
.

D.2
Complexity-Theoretic Backgrounnd

Our hardness result relies on standard assumptions from circuit complexity theory regarding the computational power of constant-depth versus logarithmic-depth circuits. Circuit complexity characterizes computational problems by the resources required by boolean circuits to solve them. A circuit family consists of a circuit for each input length
n
, where each circuit is a directed acyclic graph with input nodes, internal gates (computing boolean functions), and output nodes. The key resources are the
size
(number of gates) and
depth
(longest path from input to output).

Definition 3
0
\mathsf{L}
).

A language
L\subseteq\{0,1\}^{*}
is in
\mathsf{L}
if there exists a deterministic Turing machine
M
and a constant
c\in\mathbb{N}
such that for every input
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
:

•

M
halts on all inputs, and accepts
x
if and only if
x\in L
,

•

On input
x
, the total number of work-tape cells that
M
ever scans is at most
c\log n
(i.e.,
M
uses
O(\log n)
space).

We next define the circuit classes central to our analysis.

Definition 4
0
\mathrm{AC}^{0}
).

A language
L\subseteq\{0,1\}^{*}
is in
\mathrm{AC}^{0}
if there exists a constant
d\in\mathbb{N}
, a polynomial
p(\cdot)
, and a family of boolean circuits
\{C_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
:

•

C_{n}
has depth at most
d
and size at most
p(n)
,

•

C_{n}
uses unbounded fan-in AND, OR, and NOT gates,

•

For all
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
,
C_{n}(x)=\mathbbm{1}[x\in L]
.

It is known that simple problems like PARITY (computing the XOR of all input bits) provably lie outside
\mathrm{AC}^{0}

(
26
;
3
)
.

Definition 5
0
\mathrm{TC}^{0}
).

A language
L\subseteq\{0,1\}^{*}
is in
\mathrm{TC}^{0}
if there exists a constant
d\in\mathbb{N}
, a polynomial
p(\cdot)
, and a family of boolean circuits
\{C_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
:

•

C_{n}
has depth at most
d
and size at most
p(n)
,

•

C_{n}
uses unbounded fan-in AND, OR, NOT, and MAJORITY gates, where a MAJORITY gate outputs 1 if and only if the majority of its inputs are 1,

•

For all
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
,
C_{n}(x)=\mathbbm{1}[x\in L]
.

The additional power of MAJORITY gates allows
\mathrm{TC}^{0}
to compute functions like PARITY which lie outside
\mathrm{AC}^{0}
, and thus
\mathrm{AC}^{0}\subsetneq\mathrm{TC}^{0}

(
7
)
.

Definition 6
0
\mathrm{NC}^{1}
).

A language
L\subseteq\{0,1\}^{*}
is in
\mathrm{NC}^{1}
if there exists a polynomial
p(\cdot)
and a family of boolean circuits
\{C_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
:

•

C_{n}
has depth
O(\log n)
and size at most
p(n)
,

•

C_{n}
uses bounded fan-in (typically fan-in 2) AND, OR, and NOT gates,

•

For all
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
,
C_{n}(x)=\mathbbm{1}[x\in L]
.

This class corresponds to problems efficiently parallelizable with a logarithmic number of parallel steps. Examples include evaluating boolean formulas and computing certain regular languages
(
18
)
.

Definition 7
0
\mathsf{X}
-uniformity).

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a circuit class defined via families
\{C_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
(e.g.,
\mathrm{AC}^{0},\mathrm{TC}^{0},\mathrm{NC}^{1}
).
A family
\{C_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is
\mathsf{X}
-uniform
if there exists a function
U\in\mathsf{X}
such that, on input
1^{n}
,
U
outputs a description of
C_{n}
.

A language
L\subseteq\{0,1\}^{*}
is in the
\mathsf{X}
-uniform version of
\mathcal{C}
if it is decided by some
\mathsf{X}
-uniform family
\{C_{n}\}\in\mathcal{C}
.

Throughout, all circuit classes (
\mathrm{AC}^{0}
,
\mathrm{TC}^{0}
,
\mathrm{NC}^{1}
) are
non–uniform unless explicitly marked as uniform.

Conjecture 1
0
\mathrm{TC}^{0}\subsetneq\mathrm{NC}^{1}
).

There exist problems in
\mathrm{NC}^{1}
that are not in
\mathrm{TC}^{0}
.

This separation- which is widely believed to hold- remains unproven and is considered a fundamental open problem in complexity theory
(
63
)
. Several natural problems are candidates for witnessing this separation, including certain composition operations on non-solvable groups and the iterated multiplication problem
(
38
)
.

D.3
A Complexity–Theoretic Obstruction for No–CoT Transformers

In this section we formalize a conditional lower bound showing that,
under the standard conjecture
\mathrm{TC}^{0}\neq\mathrm{NC}^{1}
, a constant–depth
transformer without chain–of–thought (CoT) steps cannot compute a
simple “two–chain endpoint” function. The argument proceeds via a
chain of reductions starting from an
\mathrm{NC}^{1}
–complete word problem,
passing through the logspace–complete problem
\mathrm{ORD}
, and ending at a
decision version of the two–chain endpoint problem.

D.4
Problems and Classes

We begin by fixing the problems we will use.

Definition 8
0Word problem for
S_{5}
).

Let
S_{5}
be the Symmetric group over 5 elements. The
word problem for
S_{5}
,
denoted
\mathrm{WP}_{S_{5}}
, is the language

\mathrm{WP}_{S_{5}}\;=\;\{\,w\in\Sigma^{*}:\text{the product of the letters of $w$ equals the identity of $S_{5}$}\,\}.

Definition 9
0Ordering / path reachability problem
\mathrm{ORD}
).

Fix
n\in\mathbb{N}
. An
\mathrm{ORD}
instance
on
[n]=\{1,\dots,n\}
consists of:

•

a
successor function

\mathsf{succ}:[n]\to[n]\cup\{\bot\}
, where
\mathsf{succ}(i)=j
encodes a directed edge

i\to j
and
\mathsf{succ}(i)=\bot
means
i
has no outgoing
edge;

•

two distinguished vertices
s,t\in[n]
.

Equivalently, an
\mathrm{ORD}
instance is a directed line graph (i.e a single chain),
P=([n],E)
with edges
E=\{(i,\mathsf{succ}(i)):\mathsf{succ}(i)\neq\bot\}
and two distinguished vertices
s,t\in[n]
.

The language
\mathrm{ORD}
is:

\mathrm{ORD}=\left\{(\mathsf{succ},s,t):t\text{ is reachable from $s$
along the unique directed path on }[n]\right\}.

Definition 10
0Two–chain endpoint function and decision problem).

A directed graph
G=([2n],E)
is a
pair of equal-length directed chains
if
G
consists of exactly two connected components, each being a directed chain on
n
vertices.

•

The
two–chain endpoint function

F_{\mathrm{TwoChain}}:\{\,G,v\,\}\longrightarrow[n]

outputs the unique terminal vertex of the (unique) directed chain
of
G
that contains
v
. (If
v
is itself terminal, then

F_{\mathrm{TwoChain}}(G,v)=v
.)

•

The associated
decision problem
is

\mathrm{TwoChainEndEq}=\{\,(G,v,q):G\text{ is a pair of equal-length directed chains and }F_{\mathrm{TwoChain}}(G,v)=q\,\}.

Definition 11
0No–CoT transformer class).

Let
\mathcal{T}_{0}
denote the class of functions computed (with no COT) by
transformers with:

•

Constant depth.

•

Width, embedding dimension and number of attention heads at most

\mathrm{poly}(n)
on length-
n
inputs.

•

Each parameter is represented with
O(\log n)
bits of precision in each scalar.

We view each such transformer family as a non–uniform circuit family
(one transformer per input length).

D.5
Complexity–theoretic results

We collect the complexity–theoretic ingredients we need.

Lemma 2
0Word problems complete for
\mathrm{NC}^{1}
).

\mathrm{WP}_{S_{5}}\in\mathrm{NC}^{1}
and

\mathrm{WP}_{S_{5}}
is
\mathrm{NC}^{1}
–complete under (non–uniform)
\mathrm{AC}^{0}
many–one
reductions. That is, for every language
L\in\mathrm{NC}^{1}
there is a
family of
\mathrm{AC}^{0}
circuits
(f_{n})
such that for all
x
,

x\in L\iff f_{|x|}(x)\in\mathrm{WP}_{S_{5}}.

Proof.

See
(
38
)
.
∎

The next lemma shows that this completeness extends to the uniform setting.

Lemma 3
0Word problems complete for
DLOGTIME-uniform\mathrm{NC}^{1}
).

\mathrm{WP}_{S_{5}}\in\mathrm{NC}^{1}
and

\mathrm{WP}_{S_{5}}
is
DLOGTIME-uniform\mathrm{NC}^{1}
–complete.

Proof.

See
(
8
)
.
∎

This lemma establishes a fundamental containment between uniform circuit classes and space complexity.

Lemma 4
0Uniform
\mathrm{NC}^{1}
is contained in logspace).

Let
\mathrm{NC}^{1}_{\mathsf{u}}
denote DLOGTIME–uniform
\mathrm{NC}^{1}
, and let

\mathsf{L}
denote deterministic logspace. Then

\mathrm{NC}^{1}_{\mathsf{u}}\subseteq\mathsf{L}.

Proof.

See
(
12
)
.
∎

The Ordering/Path Reachability Problem serves as a canonical complete problem for logspace.

Lemma 5
0
\mathrm{ORD}
is
\mathsf{L}
–complete under
\mathrm{AC}^{0}_{\mathsf{u}}
).

\mathrm{ORD}\in\mathsf{L}
and

\mathrm{ORD}
is
\mathsf{L}
–complete under DLOGTIME–uniform
\mathrm{AC}^{0}

reductions (equivalently, first–order interpretations). That is, for
every
A\in\mathsf{L}
there is a DLOGTIME–uniform
\mathrm{AC}^{0}
function

f
with

x\in A\iff f(x)\in\mathrm{ORD}.

Proof.

See
(
22
)
.
∎

Combining the previous lemmas, we obtain an
\mathrm{AC}^{0}
reduction from the
word problem to
\mathrm{ORD}
.

Lemma 6
0
\mathrm{WP}_{G}
reduces to
\mathrm{ORD}
in
\mathrm{AC}^{0}
).

Fix a group
G
as in Lemma
2
. Then there
exists a DLOGTIME–uniform
\mathrm{AC}^{0}
many–one reduction
f
such that

w\in\mathrm{WP}_{G}\iff f(w)\in\mathrm{ORD}.

Equivalently,
\mathrm{WP}_{G}\leq^{\mathrm{AC}^{0}}_{m}\mathrm{ORD}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
2
and Lemma
4
, the (uniform) language
\mathrm{WP}_{G}
is in

\mathsf{L}
. By Lemma
5
, there is a
DLOGTIME–uniform
\mathrm{AC}^{0}
reduction
f
such that

w\in\mathrm{WP}_{G}\iff f(w)\in\mathrm{ORD}
. This is precisely an

\mathrm{AC}^{0}_{\mathsf{u}}
(hence
\mathrm{AC}^{0}
) many–one reduction as claimed.
∎

We next state the transformer-to-
\mathrm{TC}^{0}
upper bound and a basic
closure property.
We start with some lemmas from Merrill–Sabharwal
(
43
)

Lemma 7
0Any
O(\log N)
-input function has depth-3 implementation).

Let
c>0
and
f:\{0,1\}^{c\log N}\to\{0,1\}^{m}
.
There is a depth-3
\{\wedge,\vee,\neg\}
-circuit of size
O(N^{c}+\log N+m)

computing
f
.

Proof.

see
(
43
)
(Lemma 1)
∎

The next lemma extends this to iterated addition with finite precision.

Lemma 8
0Iterated
p
-precision addition).

Let
p=c\log N
.
There exists a constant
d_{\oplus}
such that for each
n\leq N
the
p
-precision
sum of
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in\{0,1\}^{p}
is computed by a depth-
d_{\oplus}
,
polynomial-size threshold circuit.

Proof.

see
(
43
)
(Appendix A)
∎

Directly from
7
we can obtain the following lemma:

Lemma 9
0p-bit multiplication).

Let
p=c\log N
.
The truncated/rounded product
(u,v)\in\{0,1\}^{p}\times\{0,1\}^{p}\mapsto u\cdot v\in\{0,1\}^{p}

,and has a depth-
d_{\times}
implementation with
d_{\times}=3
and polynomial size.

Proof.

This follows directly from
7
.
∎

The following lemma is the key connection between no-CoT Transformers and circuit complexity.

Lemma 10
0No–CoT transformers are contained in
\mathrm{TC}^{0}
).

For every function family
F
computed by a family of no–CoT
transformers in
\mathcal{T}_{0}
, there is a non–uniform
\mathrm{TC}^{0}

circuit family computing
F
. In other words,

\mathcal{T}_{0}\subseteq\mathrm{TC}^{0}.

Proof.

We define a family of models
\{T_{n}\}
, where a model
T_{n}
accepts input from vocabulary
\Sigma_{n}
of size
|\Sigma_{n}|=n^{2}
.

Fix
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
The input is a sequence of 1-hot vectors of length
l=n-2
,

(h^{(0)}_{1},\dots,h^{(0)}_{l})
with
h^{(0)}_{i}\in\{e_{1},...,e_{|\Sigma_{n}|},0\}
.
Hence the total input bit-length is

N\;=\;l\cdot|\Sigma_{n}|\;=\;n^{2}*(n-2).

(7)

Let
L\geq 1
be a fixed constant.
For each layer
\ell\in\{0,\dots,L-1\}
there are shared matrices

Q^{(\ell)},\;K^{(\ell)},\;V^{(\ell)}\;\in\;\mathbb{R}^{|\Sigma_{n}|\times|\Sigma_{n}|}

(8)

with
p
-bit entries, where

p\;=\;c\log N

(9)

for a fixed constant
c>0
.
Hidden vectors at layer
\ell
are
h^{(\ell)}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{|\Sigma_{n}|}

(with
p
-bit coordinates); for
\ell=0
,
h^{(0)}_{i}
is the 1-hot input.

For all positions
i,j
the layer computes

\displaystyle q^{(\ell)}_{i}

\displaystyle=Q^{(\ell)}\,h^{(\ell)}_{i},

(10)

\displaystyle k^{(\ell)}_{j}

\displaystyle=K^{(\ell)}\,h^{(\ell)}_{j},

(11)

\displaystyle v^{(\ell)}_{j}

\displaystyle=V^{(\ell)}\,h^{(\ell)}_{j},

(12)

\displaystyle r^{(\ell)}_{i,j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{|\Sigma_{n}|}q^{(\ell)}_{i}[t]\;k^{(\ell)}_{j}[t],

(13)

\displaystyle s^{(\ell)}_{i,j}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}r^{(\ell)}_{i,j}&\text{(Linear)},\\[3.0pt]
\dfrac{\exp\!\bigl(r^{(\ell)}_{i,j}\bigr)}{\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{l}\exp\!\bigl(r^{(\ell)}_{i,j^{\prime}}\bigr)}&\text{(Softmax)},\end{cases}

(14)

\displaystyle a^{(\ell)}_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{l}s^{(\ell)}_{i,j}\,v^{(\ell)}_{j},\qquad h^{(\ell+1)}_{i}:=a^{(\ell)}_{i}.

(15)

Every scalar multiplication returns a
p
-bit result under a fixed
truncation/rounding rule; all sums use the
p
-precision addition operator
from Appendix A of Merrill–Sabharwal
(
44
)
.

We will now show that the Forward Pass of the described model is in
\mathrm{TC}^{0}
.

All steps below are performed in parallel over the relevant indices and coordinates.

For each possible i,j, equations
10
,
11
, and
12
are computed in the following way:

\displaystyle q^{(\ell)}_{i}[t]

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=1}^{|\Sigma_{n}|}Q^{(\ell)}[t,r]\;h^{(\ell)}_{i}[r],

(16)

\displaystyle k^{(\ell)}_{j}[t]

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=1}^{|\Sigma_{n}|}K^{(\ell)}[t,r]\;h^{(\ell)}_{j}[r],

(17)

\displaystyle v^{(\ell)}_{j}[t]

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=1}^{|\Sigma_{n}|}V^{(\ell)}[t,r]\;h^{(\ell)}_{j}[r].

(18)

Each product is a
p
-bit multiply (Lemma
9
, depth
d_{\times}
);
each sum is an iterated
p
-precision sum on
|\Sigma_{n}|=O(N)
elements (Lemma
8
, depth
d_{\oplus}
).
All three MV products are computed in parallel, so this stage costs

d_{\times}+d_{\oplus}
depth.

We now analyze softmax and linear attention separately.

For linear attention, we have the following Similarities computation:

s^{(\ell)}_{i,j}\;=\;\sum_{t=1}^{|\Sigma_{n}|}q^{(\ell)}_{i}[t]\;k^{(\ell)}_{j}[t],

(19)

computed by
p
-bit multiplies (depth
d_{\times}
) followed by a
p
-precision
sum (depth
d_{\oplus}
) for a total of
d_{\times}+d_{\oplus}
.

For softmax attention, we have the following computation:

Given the scores
r^{(\ell)}_{i,j}
from the model definition, compute

u^{(\ell)}_{i,j}:=\exp_{p}(r^{(\ell)}_{i,j})
.
By Lemma
7
,
\exp_{p}:\{0,1\}^{p}\to\{0,1\}^{p}
has a depth-
3
,
polynomial-size implementation.
Next, compute
Z^{(\ell)}_{i}:=\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{l}u^{(\ell)}_{i,j^{\prime}}
using
Lemma
8
(depth
d_{\oplus}
).
Finally output

s^{(\ell)}_{i,j}:=\div_{p}(u^{(\ell)}_{i,j},Z^{(\ell)}_{i})
(where
\div_{p}
is p-precision division);
since
\div_{p}
depends on
2p=O(\log N)
input bits, Lemma
7

gives depth
3
and polynomial size for a total of
d_{\oplus}+6
.

Finally, we analyze the remaining step in
15
:

For each
(i,j)
and coordinate
k
, we compute the sum element

(s^{(\ell)}_{i,j}\,v^{(\ell)}_{j})[k]\;=\;s^{(\ell)}_{i,j}\;v^{(\ell)}_{j}[k],

(20)

with depth
d_{\times}
and polynomial size via Lemma
9
.

We then sum for each
i
and coordinate
k
,

a^{(\ell)}_{i}[k]\;=\;\sum_{j=1}^{l}\bigl(s^{(\ell)}_{i,j}\;v^{(\ell)}_{j}[k]\bigr),

(21)

which is an iterated
p
-precision sum of
l

p
-bit values (Lemma
8
) for a total of
d_{\oplus}+d_{\times}
.

We have shown that each layer has constant depth,
and we parallelize multiple fixed-depth polynomial threshold circuits over a polynomial number of indices which yealds us a polynomial total size.
Therefore, Stacking
L=O(1)
layers gives overall constant depth and polynomial size. Parameters are hard-wired, so the
family is non-uniform. Hence
{T_{n}}\in\mathrm{TC}^{0}
.
∎

Remarks:

This model represents the model described in the main text by setting
L=1
, the number of heads as 1, and
|\Sigma|_{n}
to be the vocabulary of the model for graphs with
n
nodes.
The input size to the model is consistent as any 2 chain graph on
n
nodes can be represente with exactly
n-2
edges.

Lemma 11
0
\mathrm{TC}^{0}
is closed under
\mathrm{AC}^{0}
many–one reductions).

Let
L_{1},L_{2}
be languages with
L_{1}\leq^{\mathrm{AC}^{0}}_{m}L_{2}
. If

L_{2}\in\mathrm{TC}^{0}
, then
L_{1}\in\mathrm{TC}^{0}
as well.

Proof sketch.

Let
(f_{n})
be the
\mathrm{AC}^{0}
reduction and
(C_{n})
a
\mathrm{TC}^{0}
circuit
family for
L_{2}
. For each input length
n
, define a circuit

D_{n}(x)=C_{|f_{n}(x)|}(f_{n}(x))
. The
D_{n}
have constant depth,
polynomial size, and threshold gates: they are obtained by composing an

\mathrm{AC}^{0}
circuit with a
\mathrm{TC}^{0}
circuit. Thus
(D_{n})
is a
\mathrm{TC}^{0}

family deciding
L_{1}
.
∎

D.6
From
\mathrm{ORD}
to Two–Chain Endpoint

We now formalize the reduction from
\mathrm{ORD}
to the decision version of
the two–chain endpoint problem.

We fix the following encodings.

Definition 12
0Encoding graphs for
\mathrm{ORD}
and
\mathrm{TwoChainEndEq}
).

An
\mathrm{ORD}
instance
(\mathsf{succ},s,t)
on
[n]
is encoded as:

•

For each
i\in[n]
, the value
\mathsf{succ}(i)\in[n+1]
,
where we identify
\bot
with
0
, written in binary using

\lceil\log(n+1)\rceil
bits;

•

the vertices
s,t\in[n]
, written in binary using

\lceil\log n\rceil
bits each.

The encoding of a
\mathrm{TwoChainEndEq}
instance
(G,v,q)
uses the same
representation for
G
via a successor function
\mathsf{succ}_{G}
on

[n]
, together with
v,q\in[n]
in binary.

Lemma 12
0
\mathrm{ORD}
reduces to
\mathrm{TwoChainEndEq}
in
\mathrm{AC}^{0}
).

There is a DLOGTIME–uniform
\mathrm{AC}^{0}
many–one reduction

(\mathsf{succ},s,t)\mapsto(\widetilde{\mathsf{succ}},\tilde{v},\tilde{q})

such that

(\mathsf{succ},s,t)\in\mathrm{ORD}\iff(\widetilde{\mathsf{succ}},\tilde{v},\tilde{q})\in\mathrm{TwoChainEndEq},

and moreover the directed graph induced by
\widetilde{\mathsf{succ}}

is a disjoint union of
exactly two
directed chains of the same
length.

Proof.

Let
(\mathsf{succ},s,t)
be an
\mathrm{ORD}
instance on
[n]
satisfying the
promise that
P=([n],E)
is a single directed path.

Encoding convention.

We will build an output instance on
2n
vertices, which we identify
with the set
[n]\times\{0,1\}
. We encode a vertex
(i,b)
by writing
the copy bit
b
(one bit) followed by the binary encoding of

i\in[n]
using
\lceil\log n\rceil
bits. The special value
\bot
is
encoded as all zeros. This is just a fixed representation choice for
this reduction.

(1) Construction of
(\widetilde{\mathsf{succ}},\tilde{v},\tilde{q})
.

Define
\widetilde{\mathsf{succ}}:[n]\times\{0,1\}\to([n]\times\{0,1\})\cup\{\bot\}

as follows. For every
i\in[n]
and
b\in\{0,1\}
,

\widetilde{\mathsf{succ}}(i,b)\;=\;\begin{cases}\bot,&\text{if }\mathsf{succ}(i)=\bot,\\[2.0pt]
(\mathsf{succ}(i),\,b),&\text{if }\mathsf{succ}(i)\neq\bot\text{ and }i\neq t,\\[2.0pt]
(\mathsf{succ}(t),\,1-b),&\text{if }\mathsf{succ}(t)\neq\bot\text{ and }i=t.\end{cases}

Set
\tilde{v}:=(s,0)
.

Let
\mathrm{end}\in[n]
be the (unique) terminal vertex of the input
path, i.e. the unique vertex with
\mathsf{succ}(\mathrm{end})=\bot
.
Define the target vertex
\tilde{q}
by

\tilde{q}:=\begin{cases}(\mathrm{end},1),&\text{if }\mathsf{succ}(t)\neq\bot,\\
(\mathrm{end},0),&\text{if }\mathsf{succ}(t)=\bot.\end{cases}

(2) Structure: two equal–length chains.

Consider the input path
P
. For each copy bit
b
, the edges

(i,b)\to(\mathsf{succ}(i),b)
follow
P
within copy
b
except at the
single vertex
(t,b)
, where (when
\mathsf{succ}(t)\neq\bot
) the edge
is redirected to the other copy. Therefore the graph on

[n]\times\{0,1\}
induced by
\widetilde{\mathsf{succ}}
has out-degree
at most one everywhere.

If
\mathsf{succ}(t)=\bot
, then
\widetilde{\mathsf{succ}}(t,b)=\bot

and the output graph is simply two disjoint copies of the same directed
path, hence exactly two directed chains, each on
n
vertices.

If
\mathsf{succ}(t)\neq\bot
, let
u:=\mathsf{succ}(t)
. Cutting the
input path at the edge
t\to u
decomposes it into a prefix ending at

t
and a suffix starting at
u
and ending at
\mathrm{end}
. The
redirections
(t,0)\to(u,1)
and
(t,1)\to(u,0)
“swap” the suffixes
between the two copies. Hence the output graph is the disjoint union
of exactly the two chains:

\bigl(\text{prefix in copy }0\bigr)\ \cdot\ \bigl(\text{suffix in copy }1\bigr),\qquad\bigl(\text{prefix in copy }1\bigr)\ \cdot\ \bigl(\text{suffix in copy }0\bigr).

Each chain contains exactly one copy of every original vertex, so each
has exactly
n
vertices. Thus the output is a union of exactly two
directed chains of the same length.

(3) Correctness.

We show that

(\mathsf{succ},s,t)\in\mathrm{ORD}\iff(\widetilde{\mathsf{succ}},\tilde{v},\tilde{q})\in\mathrm{TwoChainEndEq}
.

•

If
\mathsf{succ}(t)=\bot
, then
t=\mathrm{end}
is the terminal
of the unique directed path on
[n]
. Since the input graph is a single
directed path,
t
is reachable from every
s
, so
(\mathsf{succ},s,t)\in\mathrm{ORD}
.
In the output graph, the chain containing
\tilde{v}=(s,0)
is exactly
copy
0
of the path, whose terminal is
(\mathrm{end},0)=\tilde{q}
.
Hence
(\widetilde{\mathsf{succ}},\tilde{v},\tilde{q})\in\mathrm{TwoChainEndEq}
.

•

Suppose
\mathsf{succ}(t)\neq\bot
. Consider the unique directed
walk from
s
in the input path.
If
t
is reachable from
s
, then the walk reaches
t
, so in the
output the walk from
(s,0)
reaches
(t,0)
and then crosses to copy

1
, after which it follows the suffix in copy
1
until terminating at

(\mathrm{end},1)=\tilde{q}
. Thus
F_{\mathrm{TwoChain}}(\widetilde{G},\tilde{v})=\tilde{q}
.
If
t
is not reachable from
s
, then the walk from
(s,0)
never
reaches
(t,0)
, hence never crosses copies, and it terminates at

(\mathrm{end},0)\neq(\mathrm{end},1)=\tilde{q}
. Therefore

F_{\mathrm{TwoChain}}(\widetilde{G},\tilde{v})\neq\tilde{q}
.

This proves the desired equivalence.

(4)
\mathrm{AC}^{0}
and uniformity.

We argue that the mapping is computable by a DLOGTIME–uniform
\mathrm{AC}^{0}

circuit family.

First,
\tilde{v}=(s,0)
is obtained by copying the bits of
s
and
setting the leading copy bit to
0
.

Next, the bits of
\mathrm{end}
are selectable in
\mathrm{AC}^{0}
under the
promise that there is a unique terminal vertex. For each
i\in[n]
let

\mathrm{IsEnd}(i)
be the predicate “
\mathsf{succ}(i)=\bot
”, which
is a depth–2
\mathrm{AC}^{0}
check that all bits of the encoding of

\mathsf{succ}(i)
are
0
. Then each bit of
\mathrm{end}
can be
written as an unbounded OR over
i\in[n]
:

\mathrm{end}_{r}\;=\;\bigvee_{i=1}^{n}\Bigl(\mathrm{IsEnd}(i)\ \wedge\ \mathrm{bit}_{r}(i)\Bigr),

where
\mathrm{bit}_{r}(i)
is the
r
-th bit of the (fixed) binary
encoding of the constant index
i
. The copy bit of
\tilde{q}
is

1
iff
\mathsf{succ}(t)\neq\bot
, which is again a depth–2
\mathrm{AC}^{0}

predicate on the bits of
\mathsf{succ}(t)
. Thus
\tilde{q}
is
computable in constant depth.

Finally, each output entry
\widetilde{\mathsf{succ}}(i,b)
is obtained
from the input bits of
\mathsf{succ}(i)
and a
depth–2
\mathrm{AC}^{0}
equality test between the constant index
i
and the
binary input
t
, together with the predicate
\mathsf{succ}(t)\neq\bot
.
The rule above is a constant-depth case distinction implemented by
AND/OR/NOT gates. DLOGTIME uniformity follows since the circuit only
uses fixed wiring patterns plus the standard uniform equality gadgets.

This completes the proof.
∎

D.7
From the Function Problem to the Decision Problem

We now relate the function
F_{\mathrm{TwoChain}}
to the decision problem

\mathrm{TwoChainEndEq}
.

Lemma 13
0From endpoint function to endpoint equality).

Let
F_{\mathrm{TwoChain}}
be the two–chain endpoint function. Define the
associated decision language

L_{F_{\mathrm{TwoChain}}}=\{\,(x,q):F_{\mathrm{TwoChain}}(x)=q\,\},

where
x
encodes
(G,v)
and
q
encodes a vertex in
[n]
. Then

\mathrm{TwoChainEndEq}=L_{F_{\mathrm{TwoChain}}}
, and the mapping

(G,v,q)\mapsto((G,v),q)
is computable in DLOGTIME–uniform

\mathrm{AC}^{0}
.

Proof.

By definition,
\mathrm{TwoChainEndEq}
is precisely the set of triples

(G,v,q)
such that
F_{\mathrm{TwoChain}}(G,v)=q
. The encoding of
instances in
L_{F_{\mathrm{TwoChain}}}
is just the concatenation of the
encoding of
(G,v)
and the encoding of
q
, which matches the encoding
of
(G,v,q)
used above, up to a trivial re-ordering of fields. The
re-ordering is implemented by wiring and does not require any gates, so
it is computable in depth 1. Thus
\mathrm{TwoChainEndEq}=L_{F_{\mathrm{TwoChain}}}

and the identity mapping is a DLOGTIME–uniform
\mathrm{AC}^{0}
transformation
between the two encodings.
∎

The next lemma shows that computing the function in
\mathrm{TC}^{0}
implies deciding the equality problem in
\mathrm{TC}^{0}
.

Lemma 14
0Computing
F_{\mathrm{TwoChain}}
implies deciding
\mathrm{TwoChainEndEq}
in
\mathrm{TC}^{0}
).

Suppose
F_{\mathrm{TwoChain}}
is computable by a non–uniform
\mathrm{TC}^{0}
circuit
family. Then
\mathrm{TwoChainEndEq}\in\mathrm{TC}^{0}
.

Proof.

Let
(C_{n})
be
\mathrm{TC}^{0}
circuits computing
F_{\mathrm{TwoChain}}
on inputs of
length
n
. To decide whether
F_{\mathrm{TwoChain}}(x)=q
, we can:

•

feed
x
into
C_{|x|}
to obtain an output
y
encoding a
vertex in
[n]
;

•

compare
y
and
q
bitwise using an
\mathrm{AC}^{0}
equality circuit:

y=q
iff each bit matches.

Equality on
O(\log n)
bits is in
\mathrm{AC}^{0}
, and the composition of a

\mathrm{TC}^{0}
circuit with an
\mathrm{AC}^{0}
post–processing is still a
\mathrm{TC}^{0}

circuit by the closure Lemma
11
. Thus

\mathrm{TwoChainEndEq}=L_{F_{\mathrm{TwoChain}}}\in\mathrm{TC}^{0}
.
∎

D.8
Main Theorem: No–CoT Transformers Cannot Solve Two–Chain Endpoint

We are now ready to state and prove our main result.

Theorem 5
0No–CoT transformers cannot compute the two–chain endpoint function, unless
\mathrm{TC}^{0}=\mathrm{NC}^{1}
).

Assume
\mathrm{TC}^{0}\neq\mathrm{NC}^{1}
. Then there is no family of no–CoT
transformers in
\mathcal{T}_{0}
that computes
F_{\mathrm{TwoChain}}
on all
valid inputs. Equivalently,
F_{\mathrm{TwoChain}}\notin\mathcal{T}_{0}
.

Proof.

Suppose, for contradiction, that there is a family of no–CoT
transformers computing
F_{\mathrm{TwoChain}}
exactly on all valid inputs
(graphs that are unions of at most two chains).

By Lemma
10
, we have

\mathcal{T}_{0}\subseteq\mathrm{TC}^{0}
, so
F_{\mathrm{TwoChain}}\in\mathrm{TC}^{0}
. By
Lemma
14
, this implies

\mathrm{TwoChainEndEq}\in\mathrm{TC}^{0}
.

By Lemma
12
, we have

\mathrm{ORD}\leq^{\mathrm{AC}^{0}}_{m}\mathrm{TwoChainEndEq}
. By the closure
Lemma
11
, it follows that

\mathrm{ORD}\in\mathrm{TC}^{0}
.

By Lemma
6
, we also have

\mathrm{WP}_{G}\leq^{\mathrm{AC}^{0}}_{m}\mathrm{ORD}
for some fixed finite group
G
. Combining
this with
\mathrm{ORD}\in\mathrm{TC}^{0}
and applying
Lemma
11
again, we obtain

\mathrm{WP}_{G}\in\mathrm{TC}^{0}
.

Finally, by Lemma
2
,
\mathrm{WP}_{G}
is

\mathrm{NC}^{1}
–complete under
\mathrm{AC}^{0}
reductions. Hence for every language

L\in\mathrm{NC}^{1}
, there is an
\mathrm{AC}^{0}
reduction
L\leq^{\mathrm{AC}^{0}}_{m}\mathrm{WP}_{G}
.
By Lemma
11
, this implies
L\in\mathrm{TC}^{0}
.
Therefore
\mathrm{NC}^{1}\subseteq\mathrm{TC}^{0}
.

Since we always have
\mathrm{TC}^{0}\subseteq\mathrm{NC}^{1}
, we conclude

\mathrm{TC}^{0}=\mathrm{NC}^{1}
, contradicting the assumption that these classes are
different. Thus our initial assumption that a no–CoT transformer can
compute
F_{\mathrm{TwoChain}}
must be false.
∎

Appendix E
Proof of
Theorem

2

This section proves
Theorem

2
and is organized as follows. In
Section

E.1
, we show that the loss can be expressed as the same-side absorption probability of a Markov chain, which we call the canonical chain.
Section

E.2
introduces the key definitions (depth, long jumps) and two surrogate chains—the long-jump-absorbing chain and the no-long-jump chain—along with the source function representation that relates parameter derivatives to expected sources along trajectories.
Section

E.3
and
Section

E.4
compute the source functions and derivative estimates for these two chains respectively.
Section

E.6
combines these estimates to establish that
\partial_{\alpha}S\geq 0
,
\partial_{\beta}S\leq 0
, and
\partial_{\gamma}S\leq 0
for easy examples, implying monotonic parameter evolution under gradient flow.
Section

E.7
shows that hard examples contribute negligibly to the gradient. Finally,
Section

E.9
assembles these components to complete the proof.

We establish the derivative estimates at initialization, but they in fact hold throughout the learning process as long as the forward transition probability remains non-decreasing and the long-jump probability stays bounded away from zero. Since our analysis shows that both conditions are maintained for all
t\leq t_{0}
, these estimates in fact hold for all
t\leq t_{0}
.

Furthermore, under the assumptions of
Theorem

2
, the training distribution decomposes into
easy examples
—starting vertices within distance
s
of the terminal—and
hard examples
—starting vertices at distance at least
\lceil an\rceil
from the terminal. We analyze the gradient contributions from easy and hard examples separately, showing that easy examples drive learning while hard examples contribute negligibly.

E.1
Loss can be written as absorption probability of a Markov chain

Lemma

68
implies that the loss from
Section

4
, under the assumptions of
Section

6.1
, is equivalent to the probability of same-side absorption in a Markov chain on the state space of two identical directed chains
A
and
B
of equal length
n
, with absorbing endpoints
a_{n},b_{n}
, and nonterminal states
a_{1},\dots,a_{n-1},b_{1},\dots,b_{n-1}
. We will therefore analyze this absorption probability throughout the subsequent sections, which we term the
canonical Markov chain
.

The transition probabilities of the canonical chain are determined by a logit structure with parameters
\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Without loss of generality, we subtract
\gamma(t)
from all logits (which does not affect the softmax probabilities).

If
v
is in the middle of the chain, we have that:

\displaystyle o(v,C)_{k}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\alpha(t),&\text{$k$ is one ahead of $v$ in the chain}\\
\beta(t),&\text{$k$ is one back of $v$ in the chain}\\
0,&\text{$k$ start of some chain (not necessary that with $v$)}\\
0,&\text{$k$ end of some chain (not necessary that with $v$)}\\
\gamma(t),&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

If
v
is at the penultimate vertex of the chain, we have that:

\displaystyle o(v,C)_{k}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}(\alpha(t)-\gamma(t)),&\text{$k$ is one ahead of $v$ in the chain}\\
\beta(t),&\text{$k$ is one back of $v$ in the chain}\\
0,&\text{$k$ start of some chain (not necessary that with $v$)}\\
0,&\text{$k$ is the end of chain without $v$}\\
\gamma(t),&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

If
v
is at the second vertex of the chain, we have that:

\displaystyle o(v,C)_{k}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\alpha(t),&\text{$k$ is one ahead of $v$ in the chain}\\
(\beta(t)-\gamma(t)),&\text{$k$ is one back of $v$ in the chain}\\
0,&\text{$k$ start of some chain without $v$}\\
0,&\text{$k$ end of some chain (not necessary that with $v$)}\\
\gamma(t),&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

If
v
is at the first vertex of the chain, we have that:

\displaystyle o(v,C)_{k}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\alpha(t),&\text{$k$ is one ahead of $v$ in the chain}\\
0,&\text{$k$ start of some chain without $v$}\\
0,&\text{$k$ is the end of some chain (not necessary that with $v$)}\\
\gamma(t),&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

The transition probabilities are then given by softmax:

\pi(v\to k)=\frac{\exp(o(v,C)_{k})}{\sum_{k^{\prime}}\exp(o(v,C)_{k^{\prime}})}.

E.2
Preliminaries

This subsection establishes the foundational definitions and tools for analyzing the canonical Markov chain. We begin by defining key quantities such as hitting probabilities, depth, and long jumps (
Definitions

14
and
15
). We then introduce two surrogate chains (
Definitions

16
and
17
) that decompose the canonical chain’s behavior, enabling separate analysis of long-jump and local-step dynamics. Finally, we present the source function representation (
Lemma

16
) that expresses parameter derivatives in terms of expected sources along trajectories, which forms the basis for our derivative analysis in subsequent sections.

E.2.1
Useful definitions

Definition 13
0Hitting probabilities and same–side value).

For any state
v
(including absorbing states), let

h_{A}(v):=\Pr_{v}[X_{\tau}=a_{n}],\qquad h_{B}(v):=\Pr_{v}[X_{\tau}=b_{n}],

where
\tau
is the (almost surely finite) hitting time of
\{a_{n},b_{n}\}
.

For
v\in\{a_{1},\dots,a_{n-1}\}
on chain
A
, the “same–side absorption probability”
is
h_{A}(v)
; for
v\in\{b_{1},\dots,b_{n-1}\}
on chain
B
, it is
h_{B}(v)
.
Denote these by

S(v):=\begin{cases}h_{A}(v),&v\in\{a_{1},\dots,a_{n-1}\},\\
h_{B}(v),&v\in\{b_{1},\dots,b_{n-1}\}.\end{cases}

Definition 14
0Depth / distance to terminal).

Fix a chain length
n\geq 2
and consider the state space

V:=\{a_{1},\dots,a_{n},b_{1},\dots,b_{n}\}.

Define the
depth
(distance to the terminal on the same chain) by the function

d:V\to\{0,1,\dots,n-1\},\qquad d(a_{i}):=n-i,\quad d(b_{i}):=n-i.

Definition 15
0Long jump).

A transition
x\to y
is a
long jump
if the depth changes by
2
or more, i.e.

|d(y)-d(x)|\geq 2.

E.2.2
Surrogate chains

To analyze the canonical chain, we introduce two auxiliary Markov chains: the long-jump-absorbing chain and the no-long-jump chain.
Lemma

15
below shows that the derivative of the same-side absorption probability can be decomposed into contributions from these two surrogate chains. This decomposition is fundamental to our analysis, as it separates the effect of long-range transitions from local transitions.

Definition 16
0Long–jump–absorbing chain).

The
long–jump–absorbing chain
is the Markov chain obtained from the two–chain model by

redirecting every long jump

x\to y
to a single new absorbing state
e
:
all non–long–jump transition probabilities are unchanged, and the total probability mass of long jumps
out of
x
is assigned to
e
. The absorbing set is
T:=\{a_{n},b_{n},e\}
.

Definition 17
0No–long–jump (NL) chain).

The
no–long–jump (NL) chain
is the Markov chain obtained from the two–chain model by

disallowing
all long jumps: from any nonterminal state
x
, the only allowed transitions are to
states
y\neq x
with
|d(y)-d(x)|\leq 1
, and the transition probabilities are obtained from the original chain by conditioning on this. (As in the original chain, the terminal states
a_{n},b_{n}
are absorbing).

Proposition 5
0Long-jump conditional absorption is
\tfrac{1}{2}
).

Fix parameters (hence a fixed transition kernel) and a start state
v
.
Let

\tau:=\inf\{t\geq 0:\ X_{t}\in\{a_{n},b_{n}\}\},\qquad J:=\inf\{t\geq 0:\ (X_{t}\to X_{t+1})\ \text{is a long jump}\},

and write
LJ:=\{J\leq\tau\}
for the event that a long jump occurs before absorption.
Then

\Pr_{v}[X_{\tau}=a_{n}\mid LJ]=\tfrac{1}{2}.

Proof.

Let
\varphi
be the chain-swap map on states:
\varphi(a_{i})=b_{i}
and
\varphi(b_{i})=a_{i}
.
For any state
y
, by chain-swap symmetry of the model we have

\Pr_{y}[X_{\tau}=a_{n}]+\Pr_{\varphi(y)}[X_{\tau}=a_{n}]=1,

since swapping chains interchanges the absorbing events
\{X_{\tau}=a_{n}\}
and
\{X_{\tau}=b_{n}\}
.

Now condition on the time and landing state of the first long jump.
On
LJ
, let
Y:=X_{J+1}
be the landing state of the first long jump.
By definition of a long jump and the logit structure, the distribution of
Y
is symmetric under

\varphi
(targets come in
\varphi
-paired states with equal one-step probability from any fixed source),
so
\Pr_{v}[Y=y\mid LJ]=\Pr_{v}[Y=\varphi(y)\mid LJ]
for every
y
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\Pr_{v}[X_{\tau}=a_{n}\mid LJ]

\displaystyle=\sum_{y}\Pr_{v}[Y=y\mid LJ]\;\Pr_{y}[X_{\tau}=a_{n}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{y}\Pr_{v}[Y=y\mid LJ]\Big(\Pr_{y}[X_{\tau}=a_{n}]+\Pr_{\varphi(y)}[X_{\tau}=a_{n}]\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{y}\Pr_{v}[Y=y\mid LJ]\cdot 1=\tfrac{1}{2},

as claimed.
∎

Lemma 15
0Derivative decomposition).

Fix
\theta\in\{\alpha,\beta,\gamma\}
and a start state
v
. Let

\tau:=\inf\{t\geq 0:X_{t}\in\{a_{n},b_{n}\}\},\qquad J:=\inf\{t\geq 0:\ (X_{t}\to X_{t+1})\ \text{is a long jump}\},

and define
NL:=\{J>\tau\}
and
LJ:=\{J\leq\tau\}
. Set

S_{\theta}(v):=\Pr^{\theta}_{v}[X_{\tau}=a_{n}],\quad h_{\theta}(v):=\Pr^{\theta}_{v}(LJ),\quad\bar{S}^{NL}_{\theta}(v):=\Pr^{\theta}_{v}(X_{\tau}=a_{n}\mid NL),\quad\bar{S}^{LJ}_{\theta}(v):=\Pr^{\theta}_{v}(X_{\tau}=a_{n}\mid LJ).

Then

S_{\theta}(v)=(1-h_{\theta}(v))\bar{S}^{NL}_{\theta}(v)+h_{\theta}(v)\bar{S}^{LJ}_{\theta}(v),

and

\partial_{\theta}S_{\theta}(v)=(\partial_{\theta}h_{\theta}(v))\big(\bar{S}^{LJ}_{\theta}(v)-\bar{S}^{NL}_{\theta}(v)\big)+(1-h_{\theta}(v))\,\partial_{\theta}\bar{S}^{NL}_{\theta}(v)+h_{\theta}(v)\,\partial_{\theta}\bar{S}^{LJ}_{\theta}(v).

By Proposition
5
,
\bar{S}^{LJ}_{\theta}(v)=\tfrac{1}{2}
, hence

S_{\theta}(v)=\tfrac{1}{2}+(1-h_{\theta}(v))\Big(\bar{S}^{NL}_{\theta}(v)-\tfrac{1}{2}\Big),\qquad\partial_{\theta}S_{\theta}(v)=-(\partial_{\theta}h_{\theta}(v))\Big(\bar{S}^{NL}_{\theta}(v)-\tfrac{1}{2}\Big)+(1-h_{\theta}(v))\,\partial_{\theta}\bar{S}^{NL}_{\theta}(v).

Proof.

The argument is identical for
\theta\in\{\alpha,\beta,\gamma\}
, so we fix an arbitrary

\theta
throughout.

Since
\{NL,LJ\}
is a partition of the sample space,

S_{\theta}(v)=\Pr^{\theta}_{v}(X_{\tau}=a_{n},NL)+\Pr^{\theta}_{v}(X_{\tau}=a_{n},LJ).

Writing
\Pr^{\theta}_{v}(X_{\tau}=a_{n},NL)=\Pr^{\theta}_{v}(NL)\Pr^{\theta}_{v}(X_{\tau}=a_{n}\mid NL)=(1-h_{\theta}(v))\bar{S}^{NL}_{\theta}(v)
and
\Pr^{\theta}_{v}(X_{\tau}=a_{n},LJ)=\Pr^{\theta}_{v}(LJ)\Pr^{\theta}_{v}(X_{\tau}=a_{n}\mid LJ)=h_{\theta}(v)\bar{S}^{LJ}_{\theta}(v)
yields

S_{\theta}(v)=(1-h_{\theta}(v))\bar{S}^{NL}_{\theta}(v)+h_{\theta}(v)\bar{S}^{LJ}_{\theta}(v).

Differentiate the preceding identity with respect to
\theta
and apply the product rule:

\displaystyle\partial_{\theta}S_{\theta}(v)

\displaystyle=-(\partial_{\theta}h_{\theta}(v))\bar{S}^{NL}_{\theta}(v)+(1-h_{\theta}(v))\partial_{\theta}\bar{S}^{NL}_{\theta}(v)+(\partial_{\theta}h_{\theta}(v))\bar{S}^{LJ}_{\theta}(v)+h_{\theta}(v)\partial_{\theta}\bar{S}^{LJ}_{\theta}(v)

\displaystyle=(\partial_{\theta}h_{\theta}(v))\big(\bar{S}^{LJ}_{\theta}(v)-\bar{S}^{NL}_{\theta}(v)\big)+(1-h_{\theta}(v))\partial_{\theta}\bar{S}^{NL}_{\theta}(v)+h_{\theta}(v)\,\partial_{\theta}\bar{S}^{LJ}_{\theta}(v).

By Proposition
5
,
\bar{S}^{LJ}_{\theta}(v)=\tfrac{1}{2}
; substituting this constant into the value identity and
its derivative identity yields the displayed simplifications.
∎

E.2.3
Source function of surrogate chains and derivative form

To analyze how the absorption probabilities change under gradient flow, we require a tractable representation of their parameter derivatives
\partial_{\theta}u(v)
where
\theta\in\{\alpha,\beta,\gamma\}
and
u(v)
is an absorption probability of interest. Direct computation of these derivatives is challenging due to the complex dependency of multi-step trajectories on the parameters. The source function presented bellow is advantageous because sources depend only on single-step transition sensitivities and can be computed explicitly from our logit structure, enabling the quantitative bounds developed in
Sections

E.3
and
E.4
.

Lemma 16
0Source representation).

Let
(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be a finite Markov chain with transition matrix
P_{\theta}

smoothly parameterized by
\theta\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Let
T
be a nonempty absorbing set and

\tau:=\inf\{t\geq 0:X_{t}\in T\}
.
Fix a target state
t^{\star}\in T
and define the absorption probability

u_{\theta}(x):=\Pr^{\theta}_{x}[X_{\tau}=t^{\star}],\qquad x\in\mathcal{S},

so
u_{\theta}(t^{\star})=1
and
u_{\theta}(t)=0
for
t\in T\setminus\{t^{\star}\}
.
Then for every nonterminal state
x\notin T
,
u_{\theta}
is
P_{\theta}
–harmonic:

u_{\theta}(x)=\sum_{y\in\mathcal{S}}P_{\theta}(x,y)\,u_{\theta}(y).

Define the
source
for
\partial_{\theta}u_{\theta}
by

s_{\theta}(x):=\sum_{y\in\mathcal{S}}\big(\partial_{\theta}P_{\theta}(x,y)\big)\,u_{\theta}(y),\qquad x\notin T.

Then
g_{\theta}:=\partial_{\theta}u_{\theta}
satisfies, for all
x\notin T
,

g_{\theta}(x)=\sum_{y}P_{\theta}(x,y)g_{\theta}(y)+s_{\theta}(x),\qquad g_{\theta}(t)=0,\ \ t\in T,

and admits the trajectory representation

\partial_{\theta}u_{\theta}(x)=\mathbb{E}^{\theta}_{x}\!\left[\sum_{t=0}^{\tau-1}s_{\theta}(X_{t})\right],\qquad x\in\mathcal{S}.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be the finite state space and let
T\subset\mathcal{S}
be the absorbing set.
Write
U:=\mathcal{S}\setminus T
for the transient states. Reorder states so that transient
states come first, and write the transition matrix in block form

P_{\theta}=\begin{pmatrix}Q_{\theta}&R_{\theta}\\
0&I\end{pmatrix},

where
Q_{\theta}
is the
|U|\times|U|
transient-to-transient submatrix. Fix a target

t^{\star}\in T
and define the absorption probability

u_{\theta}(x):=\Pr_{x}^{\theta}[X_{\tau}=t^{\star}],\qquad\tau:=\inf\{t\geq 0:X_{t}\in T\}.

Then
u_{\theta}(t^{\star})=1
and
u_{\theta}(t)=0
for
t\in T\setminus\{t^{\star}\}
, and for
x\in U
,
the harmonic equations read

u_{\theta}(x)=\sum_{y\in U}Q_{\theta}(x,y)u_{\theta}(y)+\sum_{t\in T}R_{\theta}(x,t)u_{\theta}(t).

Equivalently, in vector form on
U
,

u_{\theta}=Q_{\theta}u_{\theta}+r_{\theta},\qquad r_{\theta}(x):=\sum_{t\in T}R_{\theta}(x,t)u_{\theta}(t)=R_{\theta}(x,t^{\star}).

Hence

(I-Q_{\theta})u_{\theta}=r_{\theta}.

(22)

Differentiate (
22
) with respect to
\theta
:

-(\partial_{\theta}Q_{\theta})u_{\theta}+(I-Q_{\theta})\,\partial_{\theta}u_{\theta}=\partial_{\theta}r_{\theta}.

Let
g_{\theta}:=\partial_{\theta}u_{\theta}
. Rearranging gives the linear system on
U
,

(I-Q_{\theta})g_{\theta}=s_{\theta},\qquad s_{\theta}:=\partial_{\theta}r_{\theta}+(\partial_{\theta}Q_{\theta})u_{\theta}.

(23)

Noting that
u_{\theta}
is constant on
T
, we also have the boundary condition

g_{\theta}|_{T}\equiv 0
.

Because the chain is absorbing, the spectral radius satisfies
\rho(Q_{\theta})<1
, hence

(I-Q_{\theta})
is invertible and

(I-Q_{\theta})^{-1}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}Q_{\theta}^{k}\quad\text{(absolutely convergent).}

Therefore from (
23
),

g_{\theta}=(I-Q_{\theta})^{-1}s_{\theta}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}Q_{\theta}^{k}s_{\theta}.

(24)

For
x\in U
, the vector
(Q_{\theta}^{k}s_{\theta})(x)
equals the expected source at time
k

before absorption:

(Q_{\theta}^{k}s_{\theta})(x)=\sum_{y\in U}Q_{\theta}^{k}(x,y)\,s_{\theta}(y)={\mathcal{E}}_{x}^{\theta}\!\big[s_{\theta}(X_{k})\,\mathbf{1}\{k<\tau\}\big].

Summing over
k
and justifying the exchange of sum and expectation by dominated convergence
(since
|s_{\theta}|
is bounded on the finite state space and
{\mathcal{E}}_{x}^{\theta}[\tau]<\infty
for every
transient
x
in a finite absorbing chain) gives

g_{\theta}(x)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}{\mathcal{E}}_{x}^{\theta}\!\big[s_{\theta}(X_{k})\,\mathbf{1}\{k<\tau\}\big]={\mathcal{E}}_{x}^{\theta}\!\left[\sum_{k=0}^{\tau-1}s_{\theta}(X_{k})\right].

Since
g_{\theta}=\partial_{\theta}u_{\theta}
and
g_{\theta}(t)=0
for
t\in T
, this holds for all

x\in\mathcal{S}
(trivially on
T
). This is the desired representation.
∎

E.3
Analysis of long-jump-absorbing chain

In this subsection, we analyze the long-jump-absorbing Markov chain defined in
Definition

16
. Our goal is to compute the source functions at each transient state
x
, which by
Lemma

16
determine the derivatives of the long-jump absorption probability. We establish in
Lemma

17
that these sources exhibit symmetry between the two chains, and then derive explicit formulas and bounds for the
\alpha
,
\beta
, and
\gamma
sources in subsequent lemmas. These results feed into
Section

E.3.1
where we obtain quantitative derivative estimates, and ultimately into
Section

E.6
where we show monotonicity of the loss as a function of the parameters.

Lemma 17
0Source symmetry).

Work in the two–chain model and in the long–jump–absorbing chain of
Definition
16
.
Let

T:=\{a_{n},b_{n},e\},\qquad\tau:=\inf\{t\geq 0:X_{t}\in T\},\qquad u_{\theta}(x):=\Pr_{x}^{\theta}[X_{\tau}=e].

For
\theta\in\{\alpha,\beta,\gamma\}
define the local source on transient states

s_{\theta}(x):=\sum_{y}\big(\partial_{\theta}P_{\theta}(x,y)\big)\,u_{\theta}(y),\qquad x\notin T,

where
P_{\theta}
is the transition matrix of the long–jump–absorbing chain.

Then for every
i\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}
and every
\theta\in\{\alpha,\beta,\gamma\}
,

s_{\theta}(a_{i})=s_{\theta}(b_{i}).

Proof.

Fix
\theta
and let
\varphi
be the chain-swap involution:
\varphi(a_{i})=b_{i}
,
\varphi(b_{i})=a_{i}

for
i\leq n
, and
\varphi(e)=e
. By symmetry of the logits, the long–jump redirection, and the
softmax, the kernel
P_{\theta}
is
\varphi
-invariant:

P_{\theta}(\varphi(x),\varphi(y))=P_{\theta}(x,y)\qquad(\forall x,y).

Hence
\partial_{\theta}P_{\theta}(\varphi(x),\varphi(y))=\partial_{\theta}P_{\theta}(x,y)
, and since the definition of
e
is symmetric with respect to the two chains,
also
u_{\theta}(\varphi(x))=u_{\theta}(x)
. Therefore, for any transient
x\notin T
,

s_{\theta}(\varphi(x))=\sum_{y}\partial_{\theta}P_{\theta}(\varphi(x),y)\,u_{\theta}(y)=\sum_{y}\partial_{\theta}P_{\theta}(x,y)\,u_{\theta}(y)=s_{\theta}(x),

where we reindexed
y\mapsto\varphi(y)
and used
\varphi
-invariance of
\partial_{\theta}P_{\theta}
and
u_{\theta}
.
∎

Lemma 18
0Monotonicity of long-jump absorption).

Work in the long–jump–absorbing chain (Definition
16
) with terminal index
n

(so
T=\{a_{n},b_{n},e\}
is absorbing) and depth function
d(\cdot)
as in Definition
14
.
Let
\tau:=\inf\{t\geq 0:\ X_{t}\in T\}
and define
u(v):=\Pr_{v}[X_{\tau}=e]
.
By chain–swap symmetry
u(a_{i})=u(b_{i})
; write
u_{i}:=u(a_{i})=u(b_{i})
, so
u_{n}=0
.

For
i=1,\dots,n-1
define the depth-grouped probabilities (aggregating by the effect on depth):

q_{i}:=\Pr(X_{t+1}=e\mid X_{t}\in\{a_{i},b_{i}\}),\quad f_{i}:=\Pr(d(X_{t+1})=d(X_{t})-1\mid X_{t}\in\{a_{i},b_{i}\}),

b_{i}:=\Pr(d(X_{t+1})=d(X_{t})+1\mid X_{t}\in\{a_{i},b_{i}\}),\quad r_{i}:=\Pr(d(X_{t+1})=d(X_{t})\mid X_{t}\in\{a_{i},b_{i}\}),

where the probabilities aggregate all allowed transitions by their effect on depth (ignoring which
chain the next state lies in). These satisfy
q_{i}+f_{i}+b_{i}+r_{i}=1
, with the boundary convention

b_{1}:=0
, and
f_{i}>0
.

Define forward differences
d_{i}:=u_{i}-u_{i+1}
for
i=1,\dots,n-1
, and set
d_{0}:=0
.
Then
d_{i}\geq 0
for all
i
, hence

u_{1}\geq u_{2}\geq\cdots\geq u_{n}=0.

Proof.

Fix
i\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}
. Since
u
is harmonic on transient states and depends only on depth by
symmetry, the harmonic equation at depth
i
can be written using the depth–grouped probabilities:

u_{i}=q_{i}\cdot u(e)+f_{i}u_{i+1}+b_{i}u_{i-1}+r_{i}u_{i}=q_{i}+f_{i}u_{i+1}+b_{i}u_{i-1}+r_{i}u_{i},

(25)

with
u_{n}=0
and
b_{1}=0
.
Rearranging gives

(1-r_{i})u_{i}=q_{i}+f_{i}u_{i+1}+b_{i}u_{i-1}.

Substitute
u_{i+1}=u_{i}-d_{i}
and
u_{i-1}=u_{i}+d_{i-1}
to obtain

(1-r_{i})u_{i}=q_{i}+f_{i}(u_{i}-d_{i})+b_{i}(u_{i}+d_{i-1})=q_{i}+(f_{i}+b_{i})u_{i}-f_{i}d_{i}+b_{i}d_{i-1}.

Using
1-r_{i}=q_{i}+f_{i}+b_{i}
, cancel
(f_{i}+b_{i})u_{i}
from both sides, yielding

q_{i}u_{i}=q_{i}-f_{i}d_{i}+b_{i}d_{i-1},

and hence

f_{i}d_{i}=b_{i}d_{i-1}+q_{i}(1-u_{i}).

(26)

Since
f_{i}>0
,
b_{i}\geq 0
,
q_{i}\geq 0
, and
1-u_{i}\geq 0
, the identity (
26
)
implies
d_{i}\geq 0
whenever
d_{i-1}\geq 0
. For
i=1
, we have
b_{1}=0
and
d_{0}=0
, so

f_{1}d_{1}=q_{1}(1-u_{1})\geq 0\quad\Rightarrow\quad d_{1}\geq 0.

Inductively,
d_{i}\geq 0
for all
i
, hence
u_{i}\geq u_{i+1}
for all
i
, proving monotonicity.
∎

Corollary 2
0Lower bounds on
d_{i}
via forward–step events).

Under the assumptions of Lemma
18
, for every
i=1,\dots,n-1
,

d_{i}\;\geq\;\frac{q_{i}}{f_{i}}\,(1-u_{i}).

Moreover, since the depth at state
a_{i}
is
n-i
, reaching the terminal requires
n-i
consecutive forward steps (each decreasing depth by
1
), and

1-u_{i}\;\geq\;\Pr(\text{$(n-i)$ consecutive forward steps starting from state $a_{i}$}),

hence

d_{i}\;\geq\;\frac{q_{i}}{f_{i}}\,\Pr(\text{$(n-i)$ consecutive forward steps starting from state $a_{i}$}).

In particular, if
f_{k}\geq f_{\min}>0
for all
k\in\{i,i+1,\dots,n-1\}
, then

\Pr(\text{$(n-i)$ consecutive forward steps starting from state $a_{i}$})\;\geq\;\prod_{j=0}^{n-i-1}f_{i+j}\;\geq\;(f_{\min})^{n-i},

and therefore

d_{i}\;\geq\;\frac{q_{i}}{f_{i}}\prod_{j=0}^{n-i-1}f_{i+j}\;\geq\;\frac{q_{i}}{f_{i}}(f_{\min})^{n-i}.

Proof.

From (
26
) and
b_{i}d_{i-1}\geq 0
we get

f_{i}d_{i}\geq q_{i}(1-u_{i})
, proving
d_{i}\geq\frac{q_{i}}{f_{i}}(1-u_{i})
.

Next,
1-u_{i}=\Pr(\tau\neq\tau_{e})=\Pr(\tau_{e}>\tau)
is the probability of not hitting
e
before
absorption. Since the depth at state
a_{i}
is
n-i
, reaching the terminal
a_{n}
(depth
0
) requires exactly
n-i
consecutive forward steps. The event that the chain makes
n-i
consecutive forward steps starting from
a_{i}

implies that
e
is not hit during those steps, hence is contained in
\{\tau_{e}>\tau\}
, giving

1-u_{i}\geq\Pr(\text{$(n-i)$ consecutive forward steps from $a_{i}$})
.
The remaining inequalities follow by multiplying the corresponding one–step lower bounds and using

f_{k}\geq f_{\min}
on the indicated range
k\in\{i,\ldots,n-1\}
.
∎

Lemma 19
0Long-jump absorption bound).

For every start state
x\notin T
,

u(x)\ \leq\ p(e)_{\max}\,{\mathcal{E}}_{x}[\tau].

In particular, whenever
{\mathcal{E}}_{x}[\tau]\leq D(x)/\zeta
(e.g. by applying Lemma
64
to the depth process under a drift gap
\zeta>0
),

u(x)\ \leq\ \frac{D(x)}{\zeta}\,p(e)_{\max}.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{t}:=\sigma(X_{0},\dots,X_{t})
and write

N_{e}:=\sum_{t=0}^{\tau-1}\mathbf{1}\{X_{t+1}=e\}.

Since
e
is absorbing and
e\in T
, the event
\{X_{\tau}=e\}
occurs iff the chain makes a transition to
e
before absorption,
so
u(x)=\Pr_{x}[X_{\tau}=e]={\mathcal{E}}_{x}[N_{e}]
.
By the tower property and Markov property,

{\mathcal{E}}_{x}[N_{e}]={\mathcal{E}}_{x}\!\left[\sum_{t=0}^{\tau-1}{\mathcal{E}}_{x}\!\left[\mathbf{1}\{X_{t+1}=e\}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t}\right]\right]={\mathcal{E}}_{x}\!\left[\sum_{t=0}^{\tau-1}\widetilde{P}(X_{t},e)\right]={\mathcal{E}}_{x}\!\left[\sum_{t=0}^{\tau-1}p(e;X_{t})\right]\leq p(e)_{\max}\,{\mathcal{E}}_{x}[\tau],

which proves the first display. The final inequality follows by substituting any upper bound on
{\mathcal{E}}_{x}[\tau]
, such as
{\mathcal{E}}_{x}[\tau]\leq D(x)/\zeta
.
∎

Lemma 20
0
\alpha
-source in LJ chain).

Let
\widetilde{P}
be the transition kernel of the long–jump–absorbing chain and let

u(x):=\Pr_{x}[X_{\tau}=e]
denote the absorption probability at
e

(so
u(e)=1
and
u(a_{n})=u(b_{n})=0
).
Let
F(x)\in S(x)
denote the forward neighbor when it exists and define

p_{f}(x):=\widetilde{P}(x,F(x))\quad(\text{set }p_{f}(x):=0\text{ if }F(x)\text{ does not exist}).

The
\alpha
–source in the long–jump–absorbing chain
satisfies for every transient
x\notin T
,

\boxed{s_{\alpha}(x)=p_{f}(x)\big(u(F(x))-u(x)\big),}

with the convention that
s_{\alpha}(x)=0
if
F(x)
does not exist.

Proof.

Fix a transient state
x\notin T
. Let
L(x)
be the set of long–jump targets in the original chain. Since
\widetilde{P}(x,y)=P(x,y)
for
y\in S(x)
and

\widetilde{P}(x,e)=\sum_{z\in L(x)}P(x,z)
, we have

s_{\alpha}(x)=\sum_{y\in S(x)}(\partial_{\alpha}P(x,y))\,u(y)+\left(\sum_{z\in L(x)}\partial_{\alpha}P(x,z)\right)u(e),

and
u(e)=1
. Because
P(x,\cdot)
is a softmax over the logits
\{o(x)_{y}\}
in the original chain
and only the forward logit depends on
\alpha
with derivative
1
, Lemma
62
gives,
for each original target
y
,

\partial_{\alpha}P(x,y)=P(x,y)\big(\mathbf{1}\{y=F(x)\}-P(x,F(x))\big)=P(x,y)\big(\mathbf{1}\{y=F(x)\}-p_{f}(x)\big).

Introduce the auxiliary successor value

w_{x}(y):=\begin{cases}u(y),&y\in S(x),\\
1,&y\in L(x).\end{cases}

Then the definition of
s_{\alpha}(x)
can be rewritten as

s_{\alpha}(x)=\sum_{y}(\partial_{\alpha}P(x,y))\,w_{x}(y),

where the sum ranges over all original targets
y
from
x
.
Substituting the softmax derivative yields

s_{\alpha}(x)=\sum_{y}P(x,y)\big(\mathbf{1}\{y=F(x)\}-p_{f}(x)\big)w_{x}(y)=P(x,F(x))w_{x}(F(x))-p_{f}(x)\sum_{y}P(x,y)w_{x}(y).

Since
w_{x}(F(x))=u(F(x))
and
P(x,F(x))=p_{f}(x)
, this becomes

s_{\alpha}(x)=p_{f}(x)\Big(u(F(x))-\sum_{y}P(x,y)w_{x}(y)\Big).

Finally, harmonicity of
u
for the long–jump–absorbing chain gives the aggregated identity

u(x)=\sum_{y\in S(x)}\widetilde{P}(x,y)u(y)+\widetilde{P}(x,e)u(e)=\sum_{y\in S(x)}P(x,y)u(y)+\sum_{z\in L(x)}P(x,z)\cdot 1=\sum_{y}P(x,y)w_{x}(y),

so
\sum_{y}P(x,y)w_{x}(y)=u(x)
, and hence

s_{\alpha}(x)=p_{f}(x)\big(u(F(x))-u(x)\big).

If
F(x)
does not exist then
\partial_{\alpha}o(x)_{y}\equiv 0
, hence
\partial_{\alpha}P(x,y)=0

for all
y
, which implies
\partial_{\alpha}\widetilde{P}(x,y)=0
for all
y\in S(x)\cup\{e\}
and
thus
s_{\alpha}(x)=0
, consistent with the stated convention.
∎

Lemma 21
0
\beta
-source in LJ chain).

Let
\widetilde{P}
be the transition kernel of the long–jump–absorbing chain and let

u(x):=\Pr_{x}[X_{\tau}=e]
(so
u(e)=1
and
u(a_{n})=u(b_{n})=0
).
Let
B(x)\in S(x)
denote the backward neighbor when it exists and define

p_{b}(x):=\widetilde{P}(x,B(x))\quad(\text{set }p_{b}(x):=0\text{ if }B(x)\text{ does not exist}).

The
\beta
–source in the long–jump–absorbing chain
satisfies for every transient
x\notin T
,

\boxed{s_{\beta}(x)=p_{b}(x)\big(u(B(x))-u(x)\big),}

with the convention that
s_{\beta}(x)=0
if
B(x)
does not exist.

Proof.

Fix a transient state
x\notin T
. Let
L(x)
be the set of long–jump targets in the original chain. Since
\widetilde{P}(x,y)=P(x,y)
for
y\in S(x)
and

\widetilde{P}(x,e)=\sum_{z\in L(x)}P(x,z)
, we have

s_{\beta}(x)=\sum_{y\in S(x)}(\partial_{\beta}P(x,y))\,u(y)+\left(\sum_{z\in L(x)}\partial_{\beta}P(x,z)\right)u(e),

and
u(e)=1
. Because
P(x,\cdot)
is a softmax over the logits
\{o(x)_{y}\}
in the original chain
and only the backward logit depends on
\beta
with derivative
1
, Lemma
62
gives,
for each original target
y
,

\partial_{\beta}P(x,y)=P(x,y)\big(\mathbf{1}\{y=B(x)\}-P(x,B(x))\big)=P(x,y)\big(\mathbf{1}\{y=B(x)\}-p_{b}(x)\big).

Introduce the auxiliary successor value

w_{x}(y):=\begin{cases}u(y),&y\in S(x),\\
1,&y\in L(x).\end{cases}

Then the definition of
s_{\beta}(x)
can be rewritten as

s_{\beta}(x)=\sum_{y}(\partial_{\beta}P(x,y))\,w_{x}(y),

where the sum ranges over all original targets
y
from
x
.
Substituting the softmax derivative yields

s_{\beta}(x)=\sum_{y}P(x,y)\big(\mathbf{1}\{y=B(x)\}-p_{b}(x)\big)w_{x}(y)=P(x,B(x))w_{x}(B(x))-p_{b}(x)\sum_{y}P(x,y)w_{x}(y).

Since
w_{x}(B(x))=u(B(x))
and
P(x,B(x))=p_{b}(x)
, this becomes

s_{\beta}(x)=p_{b}(x)\Big(u(B(x))-\sum_{y}P(x,y)w_{x}(y)\Big).

Finally, harmonicity of
u
for the long–jump–absorbing chain gives the aggregated identity

u(x)=\sum_{y\in S(x)}\widetilde{P}(x,y)u(y)+\widetilde{P}(x,e)u(e)=\sum_{y\in S(x)}P(x,y)u(y)+\sum_{z\in L(x)}P(x,z)\cdot 1=\sum_{y}P(x,y)w_{x}(y),

so
\sum_{y}P(x,y)w_{x}(y)=u(x)
, and hence

s_{\beta}(x)=p_{b}(x)\big(u(B(x))-u(x)\big).

If
B(x)
does not exist then
\partial_{\beta}o(x)_{y}\equiv 0
, hence
\partial_{\beta}P(x,y)=0

for all
y
, which implies
\partial_{\beta}\widetilde{P}(x,y)=0
for all
y\in S(x)\cup\{e\}
and
thus
s_{\beta}(x)=0
, consistent with the stated convention.
∎

Lemma 22
0
\gamma
-source in LJ chain).

Work in the long–jump–absorbing chain (Definition
16
) with terminal index
n

(so
T:=\{a_{n},b_{n},e\}
is absorbing) and assume self–transitions are disallowed.
Fix parameters
\alpha,\beta,\gamma
. Let

u(v):=\Pr_{v}[X_{\tau}=e],\qquad\tau:=\inf\{t\geq 0:\ X_{t}\in T\},

so
u(e)=1
and
u(a_{n})=u(b_{n})=0
. By chain–swap symmetry,
u(a_{i})=u(b_{i})
;
write
u_{i}:=u(a_{i})=u(b_{i})
and
u_{n}=0
.

For a transient state
x\notin T
, define the local
\gamma
–source

s_{\gamma}(x):=\sum_{z}(\partial_{\gamma}\widetilde{P}(x,z))\,u(z),

where
\widetilde{P}
is the transition kernel of the long–jump–absorbing chain.
Then the following explicit formulas hold.

(A) Deep middle
x=a_{i}
,
2<i<n-1
.

Let

Z_{\mathrm{mid}}:=e^{\alpha}+e^{\beta}+4+(2n-7)e^{\gamma}.

Then

s_{\gamma}(a_{i})=\frac{e^{\gamma}}{Z_{\mathrm{mid}}}\Big((2n-10)(1-u_{i})\;+\;u_{i-1}+u_{i+1}-2u_{i}\Big).

(B) First vertex
x=a_{1}
.

Let

Z_{1}:=e^{\alpha}+3+(2n-5)e^{\gamma}.

Then

s_{\gamma}(a_{1})=\frac{e^{\gamma}}{Z_{1}}\Big((2n-6)(1-u_{1})\;+\;(u_{2}-u_{1})\Big).

(C) Second vertex
x=a_{2}
.

Let

Z_{2}:=e^{\alpha}+e^{(\beta-\gamma)}+3+(2n-6)e^{\gamma}.

Then

s_{\gamma}(a_{2})=\frac{(2n-8)e^{\gamma}}{Z_{2}}(1-u_{2})+\frac{e^{\gamma}}{Z_{2}}(u_{3}-u_{2})+\frac{e^{(\beta-\gamma)}}{Z_{2}}(u_{2}-u_{1}).

(D) Penultimate vertex
x=a_{n-1}
.

Let

Z_{n-1}:=e^{(\alpha-\gamma)}+e^{\beta}+3+(2n-6)e^{\gamma}.

Then

s_{\gamma}(a_{n-1})=\frac{(2n-8)e^{\gamma}}{Z_{n-1}}(1-u_{n-1})+\frac{e^{\gamma}}{Z_{n-1}}(u_{n-2}-u_{n-1})+\frac{e^{(\alpha-\gamma)}}{Z_{n-1}}\,u_{n-1}.

Proof.

We argue as in Lemmas
20
and
21
.

Fix a transient state
x\notin T
. Let
L(x)
be the set of long–jump targets in the original chain.
Since
\widetilde{P}(x,y)=P(x,y)
for
y\in S(x)
and
\widetilde{P}(x,e)=\sum_{z\in L(x)}P(x,z)
, we have

s_{\gamma}(x)=\sum_{y\in S(x)}(\partial_{\gamma}P(x,y))\,u(y)+\left(\sum_{z\in L(x)}\partial_{\gamma}P(x,z)\right)u(e),

and
u(e)=1
. Introduce the auxiliary successor value

w_{x}(y):=\begin{cases}u(y),&y\in S(x),\\
1,&y\in L(x).\end{cases}

Then the definition of
s_{\gamma}(x)
can be rewritten as

s_{\gamma}(x)=\sum_{y}(\partial_{\gamma}P(x,y))\,w_{x}(y),

(27)

where the sum ranges over all original targets
y
from
x
.
Harmonicity of
u
for the long–jump–absorbing chain gives
u(x)=\sum_{y}P(x,y)\,w_{x}(y)
.

Because
P(x,\cdot)
is a softmax over the logits
\{o(x)_{y}\}
in the original chain,
Lemma
62
gives, for each original target
y
,

\partial_{\gamma}P(x,y)=P(x,y)\big(\partial_{\gamma}o(x,y)-\sum_{z}P(x,z)\partial_{\gamma}o(x,z)\big).

In each of the four cases below,
\partial_{\gamma}o(x,y)
is constant on the

\gamma
–dependent classes of targets, so (
27
)
reduces to a finite sum over those classes. We now enumerate the
\gamma
–dependent
targets and whether they are short/long in each case; all remaining targets have

\partial_{\gamma}o(x,y)=0
and contribute nothing to
s_{\gamma}(x)
.

(A) Deep middle
x=a_{i}
,
2<i<n-1
.

The
\gamma
–dependent logits are exactly the “otherwise” targets, which have
logit
\gamma
, hence
\partial_{\gamma}o=1
. There are
2n-7
such targets:
the three short switches
b_{i-1},b_{i},b_{i+1}
and
2n-10
others. Among these,

b_{i-1}
and
b_{i+1}
are short with values
u_{i-1},u_{i+1}
, while
b_{i}
is short with value

u_{i}
(hence contributes zero after centering), and the remaining
2n-10
are long jumps (value
1
).
All these have probability
e^{\gamma}/Z_{\mathrm{mid}}
, where

Z_{\mathrm{mid}}=e^{\alpha}+e^{\beta}+4+(2n-7)e^{\gamma}
. Substituting into
(
27
) yields the stated formula for
s_{\gamma}(a_{i})
.

(B) First vertex
x=a_{1}
.

The unique
\alpha
–target is
a_{2}
(logit
\alpha
); the special zero–logit
targets are
b_{1},a_{n},b_{n}
; all remaining
2n-5
targets have logit
\gamma
and thus

\partial_{\gamma}o=1
. Among the
\gamma
–targets, the only short one is
b_{2}
,
with value
u_{2}
; the other
2n-6

\gamma
–targets are long jumps (value
1
).
All
\gamma
–targets have probability
e^{\gamma}/Z_{1}
with

Z_{1}=e^{\alpha}+3+(2n-5)e^{\gamma}
. Plugging into (
27
)
gives the stated expression for
s_{\gamma}(a_{1})
.

(C) Second vertex
x=a_{2}
.

Here
a_{3}
has logit
\alpha
,
a_{1}
has logit
(\beta-\gamma)
so

\partial_{\gamma}o(a_{2},a_{1})=-1
, the special zero–logit targets are
b_{1},a_{n},b_{n}
,
and all remaining
2n-6
targets have logit
\gamma
and
\partial_{\gamma}o=1
.
Among these
\gamma
–targets, the short ones are
b_{2}
(value
u_{2}
, hence cancels) and

b_{3}
(value
u_{3}
); the remaining
2n-8

\gamma
–targets are long jumps (value
1
).
The softmax denominator is

Z_{2}=e^{\alpha}+e^{(\beta-\gamma)}+3+(2n-6)e^{\gamma}
,
so
P(a_{2},a_{1})=e^{(\beta-\gamma)}/Z_{2}
and each
\gamma
–target has probability

e^{\gamma}/Z_{2}
. Substituting these contributions into (
27
)
yields the stated formula for
s_{\gamma}(a_{2})
.

(D) Penultimate vertex
x=a_{n-1}
.

Here
a_{n}
has logit
(\alpha-\gamma)
so
\partial_{\gamma}o(a_{n-1},a_{n})=-1
,

a_{n-2}
has logit
\beta
, the special zero–logit targets are
a_{1},b_{1},b_{n}
,
and all remaining
2n-6
targets have logit
\gamma
and
\partial_{\gamma}o=1
.
Among these
\gamma
–targets, the short ones are
b_{n-2}
(value
u_{n-2}
) and

b_{n-1}
(value
u_{n-1}
, hence cancels); the remaining
2n-8

\gamma
–targets are
long jumps (value
1
). The denominator is

Z_{n-1}=e^{(\alpha-\gamma)}+e^{\beta}+3+(2n-6)e^{\gamma}
,
so
P(a_{n-1},a_{n})=e^{(\alpha-\gamma)}/Z_{n-1}
and each
\gamma
–target has probability

e^{\gamma}/Z_{n-1}
. Plugging these into (
27
) gives the stated
formula for
s_{\gamma}(a_{n-1})
.
∎

Lemma 23
0
\gamma
-source sign bounds).

Assume the setting and notation of Lemma
22
and
Lemma
18
. Let
\tau
be the absorption time into

T=\{a_{n},b_{n},e\}
. For a deep-middle index
i
with
2<i<n-1
, define the
deep-middle one-step absorption probability

q_{\rm mid}:=\widetilde{P}(a_{i},e)=\frac{(2n-10)e^{\gamma}}{Z_{\rm mid}}.

Assume a forward-drift condition at the depths under consideration:

f_{j}\geq b_{j}\qquad\text{for all relevant depths }j.

(28)

Then there exist constants
n_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
C_{\star}\geq 1
, depending only on

(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
, such that for all
n\geq n_{0}
the following bounds hold.

(A) Deep middle (
x=a_{j}
,
2<j<n-1
).

s_{\gamma}(a_{j})\ \geq\ \frac{1}{2}\,q_{j}\,(1-u_{j})\ >\ 0,\qquad q_{j}:=\widetilde{P}(a_{j},e).

In particular, since
q_{j}\geq C_{\star}^{-1}q_{\rm mid}
for
n\geq n_{0}
,

s_{\gamma}(a_{j})\ \geq\ \frac{1}{2C_{\star}}\,q_{\rm mid}\,(1-u_{j})\ >\ 0.

(B) First vertex (
x=a_{1}
).

s_{\gamma}(a_{1})\ \geq\ \frac{1}{2}\,q_{1}\,(1-u_{1})\ >\ 0,\qquad q_{1}:=\widetilde{P}(a_{1},e).

(C) Penultimate vertex (
x=a_{n-1}
).

s_{\gamma}(a_{n-1})\ \geq\ q_{n-1}\,(1-u_{n-1})\ \geq\ 0,\qquad q_{n-1}:=\widetilde{P}(a_{n-1},e).

(D) Second vertex (
x=a_{2}
).

Let
q_{2}:=\widetilde{P}(a_{2},e)
. Then

s_{\gamma}(a_{2})\ \geq\ -(d_{1}+d_{2}),\qquad d_{1}:=u_{1}-u_{2},\ \ d_{2}:=u_{2}-u_{3},

and moreover, for all
n\geq n_{0}
,

d_{1}+d_{2}\ \leq\ 3C_{\star}\,q_{\rm mid}\,{\mathcal{E}}_{a_{2}}[\tau],\qquad\text{hence}\qquad s_{\gamma}(a_{2})\ \geq\ -3C_{\star}\,q_{\rm mid}\,{\mathcal{E}}_{a_{2}}[\tau].

Moreover, for all
n\geq n_{0}
,

|s_{\gamma}(a_{2})|\ \leq\ q_{2}+(d_{1}+d_{2})\ \leq\ 4C_{\star}\,q_{\rm mid}\,{\mathcal{E}}_{a_{2}}[\tau],

using
q_{2}\leq C_{\star}q_{\rm mid}
from (
29
) and
{\mathcal{E}}_{a_{2}}[\tau]\geq 1
.
By symmetry, the same bounds hold with
a_{2}
replaced by
b_{2}
.

Finally, under (
28
) one has the crude polynomial bound

{\mathcal{E}}_{a_{2}}[\tau]\ \leq\ \mathrm{poly}(n),

and under the stronger drift condition
\inf_{j}(f_{j}-b_{j})\geq\Delta>0
one has

{\mathcal{E}}_{a_{2}}[\tau]\ \leq\ \frac{C}{\Delta}\,n

for a constant
C
depending only on the (logit-determined) depth-grouped probabilities.

Proof.

We use the explicit formulas of Lemma
22
and the
difference recurrence of Lemma
18
.

Step 1: Comparability of one-step
e
-absorption probabilities.

For each transient depth
j
, the quantity
q_{j}=\widetilde{P}(a_{j},e)
equals the total probability
of the long-jump class, whose numerator is
(2n+O(1))e^{\gamma}
and whose denominator equals

(2n+O(1))e^{\gamma}+O(1)
, where the
O(1)
terms depend only on
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
and
come from the finitely many non-
\gamma
logits and the finitely many special zero-logit targets.
Consequently, there exist
n_{0}
and
C_{\star}\geq 1
depending only on
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
such that
for all
n\geq n_{0}
,

C_{\star}^{-1}q_{\rm mid}\ \leq\ q_{j}\ \leq\ C_{\star}q_{\rm mid}\qquad\text{for all transient }j.

(29)

Step 2: Positive lower bound in the deep middle.

Fix
j
with
2<j<n-1
. Lemma
22
gives

s_{\gamma}(a_{j})=\frac{e^{\gamma}}{Z_{\mathrm{mid}}}\Big((2n-10)(1-u_{j})+u_{j-1}+u_{j+1}-2u_{j}\Big)=\frac{e^{\gamma}}{Z_{\mathrm{mid}}}\Big((2n-10)(1-u_{j})+d_{j-1}-d_{j}\Big).

By the recurrence (
26
) at depth
j
,

d_{j}=\frac{b_{j}}{f_{j}}d_{j-1}+\frac{q_{j}}{f_{j}}(1-u_{j}),

hence

d_{j-1}-d_{j}=d_{j-1}\Big(1-\frac{b_{j}}{f_{j}}\Big)-\frac{q_{j}}{f_{j}}(1-u_{j})\ \geq\ -\frac{q_{j}}{f_{j}}(1-u_{j}),

where we used
f_{j}\geq b_{j}
so that
1-\frac{b_{j}}{f_{j}}\geq 0
. Therefore

(2n-10)(1-u_{j})+d_{j-1}-d_{j}\ \geq\ \Big((2n-10)-\frac{q_{j}}{f_{j}}\Big)(1-u_{j}).

Using
\frac{e^{\gamma}}{Z_{\mathrm{mid}}}=\frac{q_{j}}{2n-10}
yields

s_{\gamma}(a_{j})\ \geq\ q_{j}(1-u_{j})\Big(1-\frac{q_{j}}{f_{j}(2n-10)}\Big).

For all
n
large enough (depending only on fixed parameters through
f_{j}
and the bound
q_{j}\leq 1
),
the parenthesis is at least
1/2
, giving

s_{\gamma}(a_{j})\geq\frac{1}{2}q_{j}(1-u_{j})>0.

The refinement in terms of
q_{\rm mid}
follows from (
29
).

Step 3: Positive lower bound at the first vertex.

Lemma
22
gives

s_{\gamma}(a_{1})=\frac{e^{\gamma}}{Z_{1}}\Big((2n-6)(1-u_{1})+(u_{2}-u_{1})\Big)=\frac{e^{\gamma}}{Z_{1}}\Big((2n-6)(1-u_{1})-d_{1}\Big).

At
j=1
, the recurrence (
26
) with
b_{1}=0
gives

f_{1}d_{1}=q_{1}(1-u_{1})
, hence
d_{1}=\frac{q_{1}}{f_{1}}(1-u_{1})
. Thus

s_{\gamma}(a_{1})=\frac{e^{\gamma}}{Z_{1}}\Big((2n-6)-\frac{q_{1}}{f_{1}}\Big)(1-u_{1})=q_{1}(1-u_{1})\Big(1-\frac{q_{1}}{f_{1}(2n-6)}\Big),

where we used
\frac{e^{\gamma}}{Z_{1}}=\frac{q_{1}}{2n-6}
. For
n
large enough the parenthesis is at least
1/2
,
yielding
s_{\gamma}(a_{1})\geq\frac{1}{2}q_{1}(1-u_{1})>0
.

Step 4: Nonnegativity at the penultimate vertex.

Lemma
22
gives

s_{\gamma}(a_{n-1})=\frac{(2n-8)e^{\gamma}}{Z_{n-1}}(1-u_{n-1})+\frac{e^{\gamma}}{Z_{n-1}}(u_{n-2}-u_{n-1})+\frac{e^{(\alpha-\gamma)}}{Z_{n-1}}u_{n-1}.

Each term in brackets is nonnegative (
1-u_{n-1}\geq 0
,
u_{n-2}-u_{n-1}=d_{n-2}\geq 0
, and
u_{n-1}\geq 0
),
so dropping the last two terms yields
s_{\gamma}(a_{n-1})\geq q_{n-1}(1-u_{n-1})\geq 0
.

Step 5: Second-vertex negativity and control by
q_{\rm mid}\cdot{\mathcal{E}}[\tau]
.

From Lemma
22
,

s_{\gamma}(a_{2})=q_{2}(1-u_{2})-P(a_{2},b_{3})d_{2}-P(a_{2},a_{1})d_{1}.

Since
q_{2}(1-u_{2})\geq 0
and the probabilities are at most
1
, we obtain the crude lower bound

s_{\gamma}(a_{2})\ \geq\ -(d_{1}+d_{2}).

Also, using
|q_{2}(1-u_{2})-P(a_{2},b_{3})d_{2}-P(a_{2},a_{1})d_{1}|\leq q_{2}(1-u_{2})+d_{1}+d_{2}\leq q_{2}+d_{1}+d_{2}
, we have the crude absolute bound

|s_{\gamma}(a_{2})|\ \leq\ q_{2}+(d_{1}+d_{2}).

Next,
d_{2}=u_{2}-u_{3}\leq u_{2}
and
d_{1}=u_{1}-u_{2}\leq u_{1}
, hence
d_{1}+d_{2}\leq u_{1}+u_{2}
.

We now use the hazard identity for absorption at
e
in the long-jump-absorbing chain.
Let
q(X_{t}):=\widetilde{P}(X_{t},e)
. Since the chain can enter
e
at most once,

u(x)=\Pr_{x}(X_{\tau}=e)={\mathcal{E}}_{x}\Big[\sum_{t=0}^{\tau-1}\mathbf{1}\{X_{t+1}=e\}\Big]={\mathcal{E}}_{x}\Big[\sum_{t=0}^{\tau-1}q(X_{t})\Big].

In particular,

u(x)\ \leq\ q_{\max}\,{\mathcal{E}}_{x}[\tau],\qquad q_{\max}:=\sup_{y\notin T}\widetilde{P}(y,e).

By the comparability (
29
),
q_{\max}\leq C_{\star}q_{\rm mid}
for
n\geq n_{0}
, hence

u(x)\ \leq\ C_{\star}q_{\rm mid}\,{\mathcal{E}}_{x}[\tau]\qquad(n\geq n_{0}).

Applying this to
x=a_{2}
gives
u_{2}\leq C_{\star}q_{\rm mid}{\mathcal{E}}_{a_{2}}[\tau]
.
For
u_{1}
, observe that from
a_{1}
in one step one either absorbs or moves to depth
2
, so

{\mathcal{E}}_{a_{1}}[\tau]\leq 1+\sup_{y\in\{a_{2},b_{2}\}}{\mathcal{E}}_{y}[\tau]=1+{\mathcal{E}}_{a_{2}}[\tau]\leq 2{\mathcal{E}}_{a_{2}}[\tau],

using
{\mathcal{E}}_{a_{2}}[\tau]\geq 1
. Therefore

u_{1}\leq C_{\star}q_{\rm mid}{\mathcal{E}}_{a_{1}}[\tau]\leq 2C_{\star}q_{\rm mid}{\mathcal{E}}_{a_{2}}[\tau].

Combining yields

d_{1}+d_{2}\leq u_{1}+u_{2}\leq 3C_{\star}q_{\rm mid}{\mathcal{E}}_{a_{2}}[\tau],

and hence

s_{\gamma}(a_{2})\ \geq\ -3C_{\star}q_{\rm mid}\,{\mathcal{E}}_{a_{2}}[\tau].

Finally, the stated polynomial (or linear under strict drift) upper bounds on
{\mathcal{E}}_{a_{2}}[\tau]
follow
from standard biased (or nonnegatively drifted) one-dimensional random-walk estimates applied to the
depth process under (
28
) (and are used only as coarse growth controls).
∎

E.3.1
Derivative estimates

In this subsubsection we derive quantitative bounds on the derivatives of the absorption probability
at the long–jump state
e
in the long–jump–absorbing chain (Definition
16
).
We leverage the source computations from the preceding subsubsections with the source representation of the derivative (Lemma
16
).
Throughout,
h_{\theta}(v)
denotes this absorption probability, i.e.,
h_{\theta}(v):=\Pr_{v}[X_{\tau}=e]
.

Lemma 24
0Derivative estimate for
\alpha
).

In the
long–jump–absorbing chain
, let

\tau:=\inf\{t\geq 0:\ X_{t}\in\{a_{n},b_{n},e\}\},\qquad v_{\mathrm{pen}}:=\begin{cases}a_{n-1},&v\in\{a_{1},\dots,a_{n-1}\},\\
b_{n-1},&v\in\{b_{1},\dots,b_{n-1}\},\end{cases}\qquad\sigma:=\inf\{t\geq 0:\ X_{t}=v_{\mathrm{pen}}\}.

Then the derivative
\partial_{\alpha}h_{\alpha}(v)
satisfies

\partial_{\alpha}h_{\alpha}(v)={\mathcal{E}}_{v}\!\left[\sum_{t=0}^{\tau-1}s_{\alpha}(X_{t})\right]\ \leq\ s_{\alpha}(v)\;+\;s_{\alpha}(v_{\mathrm{pen}})\,\Pr_{v}(0<\sigma<\tau),

where

s_{\alpha}(x)=p_{f}(x)\big(h_{\alpha}(F(x))-h_{\alpha}(x)\big)\leq 0,

with the convention
s_{\alpha}(x)=0
if the (same–chain) forward neighbor
F(x)
does not exist.

In particular, if
v
is
k
steps from the terminal on its chain, i.e.
v\in\{a_{n-k},b_{n-k}\}
with

2\leq k\leq n-1
, then

\Pr_{v}(0<\sigma<\tau)\ \geq\ \Pr_{v}\Big(X_{i}=F^{i}(v)\ \ \forall i=1,\dots,k-1\Big),

and hence

\partial_{\alpha}h_{\alpha}(v)\ \leq\ s_{\alpha}(v)\;+\;s_{\alpha}(v_{\mathrm{pen}})\,\Pr_{v}\Big(X_{i}=F^{i}(v)\ \ \forall i=1,\dots,k-1\Big).

Proof.

Let
u(\cdot):=h_{\alpha}(\cdot)
be the absorption probability at
e
in the long–jump–absorbing chain,
so
u(x)=\Pr_{x}[X_{\tau}=e]
with
\tau:=\inf\{t\geq 0:X_{t}\in\{a_{n},b_{n},e\}\}
.
Apply the source/trajectory representation (Lemma
16
) with
\theta=\alpha
:

\partial_{\alpha}h_{\alpha}(v)=\partial_{\alpha}u(v)={\mathcal{E}}_{v}\!\left[\sum_{t=0}^{\tau-1}s_{\alpha}(X_{t})\right],

where
s_{\alpha}(x):=\sum_{y}(\partial_{\alpha}P(x,y))\,u(y)
is the local
\alpha
–source.

By the explicit
\alpha
–source identity in the long–jump–absorbing chain (Lemma
20
),

s_{\alpha}(x)=p_{f}(x)\big(u(F(x))-u(x)\big),

with the convention
s_{\alpha}(x)=0
if
F(x)
does not exist. By monotonicity of
u
along depth
(Lemma
18
), we have
u(F(x))\leq u(x)
whenever
F(x)
exists, hence

s_{\alpha}(x)\leq 0
for all transient
x
. Therefore

\partial_{\alpha}h_{\alpha}(v)={\mathcal{E}}_{v}\!\left[\sum_{t=0}^{\tau-1}s_{\alpha}(X_{t})\right]\leq{\mathcal{E}}_{v}\!\left[s_{\alpha}(X_{0})+s_{\alpha}(X_{\sigma})\,\mathbf{1}\{0<\sigma<\tau\}\right]=s_{\alpha}(v)+s_{\alpha}(v_{\mathrm{pen}})\,\Pr_{v}(0<\sigma<\tau),

where
\sigma:=\inf\{t\geq 0:\ X_{t}=v_{\mathrm{pen}}\}
and
v_{\mathrm{pen}}
is the penultimate state on the same chain as
v
.
Indeed, on
\{0<\sigma<\tau\}
we have
X_{\sigma}=v_{\mathrm{pen}}
, and for all other times
t\in\{0,\dots,\tau-1\}\setminus\{0,\sigma\}

we have
s_{\alpha}(X_{t})\leq 0
, so discarding those terms only increases the sum.
∎

Lemma 25
0Derivative estimate for
\beta
).

In the
long–jump–absorbing chain
, with
\tau,\sigma,v_{\mathrm{pen}}
as in
Lemma
24
, the derivative
\partial_{\beta}h_{\beta}(v)
satisfies

\partial_{\beta}h_{\beta}(v)={\mathcal{E}}_{v}\!\left[\sum_{t=0}^{\tau-1}s_{\beta}(X_{t})\right]\ \geq\ s_{\beta}(v)\;+\;s_{\beta}(v_{\mathrm{pen}})\,\Pr_{v}(0<\sigma<\tau),

where

s_{\beta}(x)=p_{b}(x)\big(h_{\beta}(B(x))-h_{\beta}(x)\big)\geq 0,

with the convention
s_{\beta}(x)=0
if the (same–chain) backward neighbor
B(x)
does not exist.

In particular, if
v
is
k
steps from the terminal on its chain, i.e.
v\in\{a_{n-k},b_{n-k}\}
with

2\leq k\leq n-1
, then

\Pr_{v}(0<\sigma<\tau)\ \geq\ \Pr_{v}\Big(X_{i}=F^{i}(v)\ \ \forall i=1,\dots,k-1\Big),

and hence

\partial_{\beta}h_{\beta}(v)\ \geq\ s_{\beta}(v)\;+\;s_{\beta}(v_{\mathrm{pen}})\,\Pr_{v}\Big(X_{i}=F^{i}(v)\ \ \forall i=1,\dots,k-1\Big).

Proof.

The argument is analogous to that of Lemma
24
; we include it here for completeness.

Let
u(\cdot):=h_{\beta}(\cdot)
be the absorption probability at
e
in the long–jump–absorbing chain,
so
u(x)=\Pr_{x}[X_{\tau}=e]
with
\tau:=\inf\{t\geq 0:X_{t}\in\{a_{n},b_{n},e\}\}
.
Apply the source/trajectory representation (Lemma
16
) with
\theta=\beta
:

\partial_{\beta}h_{\beta}(v)=\partial_{\beta}u(v)={\mathcal{E}}_{v}\!\left[\sum_{t=0}^{\tau-1}s_{\beta}(X_{t})\right],

where
s_{\beta}(x):=\sum_{y}(\partial_{\beta}P(x,y))\,u(y)
is the local
\beta
–source.

By the explicit
\beta
–source identity in the long–jump–absorbing chain (Lemma
21
),

s_{\beta}(x)=p_{b}(x)\big(u(B(x))-u(x)\big),

with the convention
s_{\beta}(x)=0
if
B(x)
does not exist. By monotonicity of
u
along depth
(Lemma
18
), we have
u(B(x))\geq u(x)
whenever
B(x)
exists, hence

s_{\beta}(x)\geq 0
for all transient
x
. Therefore the random sum in the trajectory representation is
almost surely nonnegative, and so for
\sigma:=\inf\{t\geq 0:\ X_{t}=v_{\mathrm{pen}}\}
,

\partial_{\beta}h_{\beta}(v)={\mathcal{E}}_{v}\!\left[\sum_{t=0}^{\tau-1}s_{\beta}(X_{t})\right]\geq{\mathcal{E}}_{v}\!\left[s_{\beta}(X_{0})+s_{\beta}(X_{\sigma})\,\mathbf{1}\{0<\sigma<\tau\}\right]=s_{\beta}(v)+s_{\beta}(v_{\mathrm{pen}})\,\Pr_{v}(0<\sigma<\tau),

since
X_{0}=v
and on
\{0<\sigma<\tau\}
we have
X_{\sigma}=v_{\mathrm{pen}}
.
∎

Lemma 26
0Derivative estimate for
\gamma
).

Assume the forward-drift condition (
28
) and the setting of
Lemma
23
. Fix a start state
v_{0}=a_{n-K}

at distance
K\geq 1
from the terminal, where
K
is a fixed constant independent of
n
.
Let

v_{\mathrm{pen}}:=a_{n-1},\qquad\tau:=\inf\{t\geq 0:\ X_{t}\in\{a_{n},b_{n},e\}\},\qquad\sigma:=\inf\{t\geq 0:\ X_{t}=v_{\mathrm{pen}}\}.

Then for all sufficiently large
n
(depending on
K,\alpha,\beta,\gamma
),

\partial_{\gamma}h_{\gamma}(v_{0})\ \geq\ \Big(s_{\gamma}(v_{\mathrm{pen}})\Big)\Pr_{v_{0}}(0<\sigma<\tau)\;+\;\frac{1}{2}\,s_{\gamma}(v_{0})\ >\ 0,

and

\Pr_{v_{0}}(0<\sigma<\tau)\ \geq\ \Pr_{v_{0}}\Big(X_{i}=F^{i}(v_{0})\ \ \forall i=1,\dots,K-1\Big).

where
s_{\gamma}(v_{0})
is the local
\gamma
–source at the initial state
(as given explicitly in Lemma
22
and lower-bounded
in Lemma
23
).

Proof.

Let
u(v):=\Pr_{v}[X_{\tau}=e]
denote the absorption probability at
e
in
the long–jump–absorbing chain, with
\tau:=\inf\{t\geq 0:X_{t}\in T\}
and

T:=\{a_{n},b_{n},e\}
. By the source/trajectory representation (Lemma
16
),

\partial_{\gamma}u(v_{0})={\mathcal{E}}_{v_{0}}\!\left[\sum_{t=0}^{\tau-1}s_{\gamma}(X_{t})\right].

(30)

Since
v_{0}=a_{n-K}
with
K\geq 1
fixed, Lemma
23

applies (using case (C) when
K=1
, and case (A) when
K\geq 2
) and gives

s_{\gamma}(v_{0})\ \geq\ \frac{1}{2}\,q_{n-K}\,(1-u(v_{0}))\ >\ 0,

where
q_{n-K}=\widetilde{P}(a_{n-K},e)
is the one-step absorption probability.
The factor
1-u(v_{0})\geq\eta>0
is bounded below by a positive constant depending
only on
K
and the fixed parameters (since the event of
K
consecutive forward
moves has probability at least
(f_{\min})^{K}>0
).

Let
S_{2}:=\{a_{2},b_{2}\}
. By Lemma
23
(A)–(C),
for all states
x\notin S_{2}
with
x\notin T
, we have
s_{\gamma}(x)\geq 0
.
Thus

{\mathcal{E}}_{v_{0}}\!\left[\sum_{t=0}^{\tau-1}s_{\gamma}(X_{t})\right]\ \geq\ s_{\gamma}(v_{0})+{\mathcal{E}}_{v_{0}}\!\left[s_{\gamma}(X_{\sigma})\,\mathbf{1}\{0<\sigma<\tau\}\right]\;+\;{\mathcal{E}}_{v_{0}}\!\left[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}t:\,X_{t}\in S_{2}\end{subarray}}s_{\gamma}(X_{t})\right].

On the event
\{0<\sigma<\tau\}
we have
X_{\sigma}=v_{\mathrm{pen}}=a_{n-1}
, hence

{\mathcal{E}}_{v_{0}}\!\left[s_{\gamma}(X_{\sigma})\,\mathbf{1}\{0<\sigma<\tau\}\right]=s_{\gamma}(v_{\mathrm{pen}})\,\Pr_{v_{0}}(0<\sigma<\tau).

Moreover, since
v_{0}=a_{n-K}
, the event of
K-1
consecutive forward steps implies
\{0<\sigma<\tau\}
, so

\Pr_{v_{0}}(0<\sigma<\tau)\ \geq\ \Pr_{v_{0}}\Big(X_{i}=F^{i}(v_{0})\ \ \forall i=1,\dots,K-1\Big)=\prod_{j=0}^{K-2}p_{f}\!\big(F^{j}(v_{0})\big).

Under the forward-drift condition
\alpha>\beta
, there exists
\rho\in(0,1)

(depending only on
\alpha,\beta,\gamma
) such that
p_{b}(x)/p_{f}(x)\leq\rho

for all deep–middle states. By the gambler’s–ruin estimate
(Lemma
63
), the probability of reaching the second vertex

S_{2}=\{a_{2},b_{2}\}
from a start state at depth
K
before absorption satisfies

\Pr_{v_{0}}(H_{2})\ \leq\ \rho^{\,n-K-2},

where
H_{2}:=\{\exists\,t<\tau:X_{t}\in S_{2}\}
.

To control the (potentially negative) contribution from visits to
S_{2}
, we use the absolute
bound from Lemma
23
(D): for each
x\in S_{2}
,

|s_{\gamma}(x)|\ \leq\ 4C_{\star}\,q_{\rm mid}\,{\mathcal{E}}_{x}[\tau].

Let
N_{2}:=\sum_{t<\tau}\mathbf{1}\{X_{t}\in S_{2}\}
.
Then

\left|{\mathcal{E}}_{v_{0}}\!\left[\sum_{t:\,X_{t}\in S_{2}}s_{\gamma}(X_{t})\right]\right|\ \leq\ \sup_{x\in S_{2}}|s_{\gamma}(x)|\cdot{\mathcal{E}}_{v_{0}}[N_{2}]\ \leq\ 4C_{\star}q_{\rm mid}\Big(\sup_{x\in S_{2}}{\mathcal{E}}_{x}[\tau]\Big){\mathcal{E}}_{v_{0}}[N_{2}],

where we used
{\mathcal{E}}_{v_{0}}[N_{2}]\leq\Pr_{v_{0}}(H_{2})\cdot\sup_{x\in S_{2}}{\mathcal{E}}_{x}[\tau]

and the polynomial bound on hitting times from Lemma
23
.
Combining with
\Pr_{v_{0}}(H_{2})\leq\rho^{\,n-K-2}
, we obtain

\left|{\mathcal{E}}_{v_{0}}\!\left[\sum_{t:\,X_{t}\in S_{2}}s_{\gamma}(X_{t})\right]\right|\ \leq\ C^{\prime}\,q_{\rm mid}\,\mathrm{poly}(n)\cdot\rho^{\,n-K-2},

for a constant
C^{\prime}>0
depending only on
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma,K)
.

Combining the above bounds, we have

\partial_{\gamma}u(v_{0})\ \geq\ s_{\gamma}(v_{0})+s_{\gamma}(v_{\mathrm{pen}})\,\Pr_{v_{0}}(0<\sigma<\tau)-C^{\prime}\,q_{\rm mid}\,\mathrm{poly}(n)\cdot\rho^{\,n-K-2}.

Finally, note that the main term is itself proportional to
q_{\rm mid}
: by the lower bound
above together with (
29
),

s_{\gamma}(v_{0})\ \geq\ \frac{1}{2}\,q_{n-K}\,\eta\ \geq\ \frac{\eta}{2C_{\star}}\,q_{\rm mid}.

Thus both the positive contribution
s_{\gamma}(v_{0})
and the worst-case negative correction from
S_{2}

carry a factor
q_{\rm mid}
, but the latter is suppressed by the exponentially small prefactor

\mathrm{poly}(n)\rho^{\,n-K-2}
. Since
\rho<1
, for all sufficiently large
n
we have

C^{\prime}\,q_{\rm mid}\,\mathrm{poly}(n)\cdot\rho^{\,n-K-2}\ \leq\ \frac{1}{2}\,s_{\gamma}(v_{0}),

and hence

\partial_{\gamma}h_{\gamma}(v_{0})\ \geq\ s_{\gamma}(v_{\mathrm{pen}})\,\Pr_{v_{0}}(0<\sigma<\tau)+\frac{1}{2}\,s_{\gamma}(v_{0})\ >\ 0.

∎

E.4
Analysis of no-long-jump chain

In this subsection, we analyze the no-long-jump (NL) chain defined in
Definition

17
, which captures the canonical chain’s behavior conditioned on never making a long jump before absorption. The analysis mirrors that of
Section

E.3
: we compute the source functions for the NL chain, establish symmetry properties, and derive derivative estimates in
Section

E.5.1
.

We recall the base model assumptions from
Section

6.1
:

Assumption 1
0Base model (from the main paper)).

For any input sequence
s_{1:L_{0}}
, let
p_{\text{fwd}}
,
p_{\text{bwd}}
, and
p_{\text{switch}}
denote the probabilities that the model performs a forward, backward, or switch step respectively at the first step (time
t=L_{0}+1
).
We assume that for any
s_{1:L_{0}}
,
p_{\text{fwd}}>p_{\text{bwd}}+\zeta
for some constant
\zeta>0
, and additionally
p_{\text{switch}}>\upsilon
for some
\upsilon>0
.

Setup (NL chain).

Throughout this section we work with the
no–long–jump (NL) chain
on the two–chain graph of length
n
,
with absorbing set
\{a_{n},b_{n}\}
