. We write
p_{\mathrm{NL}}(v\to k)
for its one–step transition probabilities.
Let
D(\cdot)
denote depth (distance from the absorbing endpoint on its chain) and let

\tau:=\inf\{t\geq 0:\ X_{t}\in\{a_{n},b_{n}\}\},\qquad h(v):=\Pr_{v}^{NL}[X_{\tau}=a_{n}].

For any parameter
\theta\in\{\alpha,\beta,\gamma\}
define the local NL source

s_{\theta,\mathrm{NL}}(v):=\sum_{k\in\mathcal{N}(v)}\big(\partial_{\theta}p_{\mathrm{NL}}(v\to k)\big)\,h(k),\qquad\mathcal{N}(v):=\{k\in V\setminus\{v\}:\ |D(k)-D(v)|\leq 1\}.

We also write
p_{\mathrm{sw}}(v):=\sum_{k\in\mathcal{S}(v)}p_{\mathrm{NL}}(v\to k)
for the single–step switch probability
(where
\mathcal{S}(v)\subseteq\mathcal{N}(v)
are the cross–chain neighbors) and

p_{\mathrm{sw}}^{\max}:=\sup_{v\notin\{a_{n},b_{n}\}}p_{\mathrm{sw}}(v)
.
Finally let

\displaystyle p_{f}(v)

\displaystyle:=\Pr\big(D(X_{1})=D(v)-1\mid X_{0}=v\big),

\displaystyle p_{b}(v)

\displaystyle:=\Pr\big(D(X_{1})=D(v)+1\mid X_{0}=v\big),

\displaystyle\delta

\displaystyle:=\min_{v\notin\{a_{n},b_{n}\}}\big(p_{f}(v)-p_{b}(v)\big)>0,

where probabilities are under the NL chain.

Lemma 27
0Switch probability is
O(1/n)
in the NL chain).

Consider the
no–long–jump (NL) chain
on the two–chain graph of length
n
(Definition
17
), and write
p_{\mathrm{sw}}(v)
for the one-step switch probability in the NL chain (as in paragraph
E.4
below).
Under Assumption
1
and the logit structure of
Section

E.1
, there exists a constant
C>0
(depending only on fixed parameters) such that for all nonterminal states
v
,

p_{\mathrm{sw}}(v)\ \leq\ \frac{C}{n}.

Proof.

Fix a nonterminal state
v
, and let f be the logit of the forward probability. Because the forward probability is bounded below by a constant
\zeta>0
, we have that
e^{f}\geq\Theta(n)\zeta\cdot e^{\gamma}
. Because the logits of the switch targets are are all at most
\gamma
(recall that
\gamma>0
), and in the No Long jump chain, there are at most 3 such targets, it follows that the switch probability is bounded above
3\cdot\frac{e^{\gamma}}{e^{f}}\leq 3\cdot\frac{e^{\gamma}}{\Theta(n)\zeta\cdot e^{\gamma}}\leq\frac{C}{n}
.∎

Lemma 28
0NL transition scaling).

Let
p(v\to k)
denote the one-step transition probability in the
original
(unconditioned) chain, and
let
p_{\mathrm{NL}}(v\to k)
denote the one-step transition probability in the
no–long–jump (NL) chain
.
Fix any nonterminal state
v
and any state
k\neq v
such that the transition
v\to k
is
not
a long jump
(equivalently,
|D(k)-D(v)|\leq 1
). Then

p_{\mathrm{NL}}(v\to k)\ \leq\ \frac{1}{\zeta}\,p(v\to k),

where
\zeta
is the constant from Assumption
1
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{N}(v):=\{y\in V\setminus\{v\}:\ |D(y)-D(v)|\leq 1\}
be the set of non–long–jump targets from
v
.
By Definition
17
, the NL kernel is obtained by conditioning on the event
\{X_{1}\in\mathcal{N}(v)\}
, hence

p_{\mathrm{NL}}(v\to k)=\Pr(X_{1}=k\mid X_{0}=v,\ X_{1}\in\mathcal{N}(v))=\frac{p(v\to k)}{\Pr(X_{1}\in\mathcal{N}(v)\mid X_{0}=v)}.

Moreover, the forward move
v\to F(v)
is never a long jump, so
\Pr(X_{1}\in\mathcal{N}(v)\mid X_{0}=v)\geq p(v\to F(v))
.
By Assumption
1
,
p(v\to F(v))\geq\zeta
, hence
\Pr(X_{1}\in\mathcal{N}(v)\mid X_{0}=v)\geq\zeta
,
which yields the claimed bound.
∎

Lemma 29
0Same-side absorption in NL chain).

Fix
k\geq 1
. In the notation of the NL setup above (paragraph
E.4
), for
v=a_{n-k}
we have

1-h(v)\ \leq\ p_{\mathrm{sw}}^{\max}\cdot\frac{k}{\zeta}.

Consequently, for fixed
k
, the same-side absorption probability tends to 1 as
n
tends to infinity in the NL chain.

Proof.

Let
D(\cdot)
be the depth–distance to the end and define, for each transient state
x\in V\setminus\{a_{n},b_{n}\}
,

\displaystyle p_{f}(x)

\displaystyle:=\Pr\big(D(X_{1})=D(x)-1\mid X_{0}=x\big),

\displaystyle p_{b}(x)

\displaystyle:=\Pr\big(D(X_{1})=D(x)+1\mid X_{0}=x\big),

\displaystyle\delta

\displaystyle:=\min_{x\in V\setminus\{a_{n},b_{n}\}}\big(p_{f}(x)-p_{b}(x)\big)>0.

If
X_{\tau}=b_{n}
starting from
a_{n-k}
, then the trajectory must switch from chain
A
to chain
B

at least once before absorption. Let
E
be the event that at least one switch occurs before
\tau
,
and let
N_{\mathrm{sw}}
be the number of switches before
\tau
. Then

1-S^{NL}_{n}(a_{n-k})=\Pr(X_{\tau}=b_{n})\leq\Pr(E)\leq\Pr(N_{\mathrm{sw}}\geq 1)\leq{\mathcal{E}}[N_{\mathrm{sw}}].

Moreover,

{\mathcal{E}}[N_{\mathrm{sw}}]={\mathcal{E}}\Big[\sum_{t=0}^{\tau-1}\mathbf{1}\{\text{switch at }t\}\Big]\leq{\mathcal{E}}\Big[\sum_{t=0}^{\tau-1}p_{\mathrm{sw}}(X_{t})\Big]\leq p_{\mathrm{sw}}^{\max}\,{\mathcal{E}}[\tau].

By Lemma
64
applied to the depth process (using that
p_{f}(x)-p_{b}(x)\geq\delta
for all transient
x
),
{\mathcal{E}}[\tau]\leq k/\delta
. Combining yields the stated bound. The consequence now follows from lemma
27
.
∎

Lemma 30
0Same-side value differences).

Fix
k\geq 2
. In the notation of the NL setup above (paragraph
E.4
), for every
j\in\{1,\dots,k-1\}
,

\displaystyle|h(a_{n-j})-h(a_{n-(j+1)})|

\displaystyle\leq\max\!\Big(1-S^{NL}_{n}(a_{n-j}),\,1-S^{NL}_{n}(a_{n-(j+1)})\Big)

\displaystyle\leq p_{\mathrm{sw}}^{\max}\,\max\!\Big({\mathcal{E}}_{a_{n-j}}^{NL}[\tau],\,{\mathcal{E}}_{a_{n-(j+1)}}^{NL}[\tau]\Big)

\displaystyle\leq p_{\mathrm{sw}}^{\max}\,\max\!\Big(\tfrac{D(a_{n-j})}{\delta},\,\tfrac{D(a_{n-(j+1)})}{\delta}\Big)\;=\;p_{\mathrm{sw}}^{\max}\,\frac{j+1}{\delta},

where
D(a_{n-j})=j
.

Proof.

Let
u_{j}:=S^{NL}_{n}(a_{n-j})\in[0,1]
. Then

|u_{j}-u_{j+1}|=|(1-u_{j})-(1-u_{j+1})|\leq\max(1-u_{j},\,1-u_{j+1}).

Moreover, for any start state
x\in\{a_{1},\dots,a_{n-1}\}
, the event
\{X_{\tau}=b_{n}\}
implies that the trajectory
switches chains at least once before absorption, hence

1-\Pr_{x}^{NL}(X_{\tau}=a_{n})=\Pr_{x}^{NL}(X_{\tau}=b_{n})\leq{\mathcal{E}}_{x}^{NL}[N_{\mathrm{sw}}]\leq p_{\mathrm{sw}}^{\max}\,{\mathcal{E}}_{x}^{NL}[\tau],

where the last inequality follows from

{\mathcal{E}}_{x}^{NL}[N_{\mathrm{sw}}]\leq{\mathcal{E}}_{x}^{NL}\big[\sum_{t=0}^{\tau-1}p_{\mathrm{sw}}(X_{t})\big]\leq p_{\mathrm{sw}}^{\max}{\mathcal{E}}_{x}^{NL}[\tau]
.
Applying this with
x=a_{n-j}
and
x=a_{n-(j+1)}
and combining with the first display gives the stated bound.
For the final inequality, apply Lemma
64
to the depth process (with drift gap
\delta
)
to obtain
{\mathcal{E}}_{a_{n-j}}^{NL}[\tau]\leq D(a_{n-j})/\delta=j/\delta
and

{\mathcal{E}}_{a_{n-(j+1)}}^{NL}[\tau]\leq D(a_{n-(j+1)})/\delta=(j+1)/\delta
.
∎

E.5
Source computations in the no–long–jump (NL) chain

Lemma 31
0
\gamma
-source in NL chain).

In the notation of the NL setup above (paragraph
E.4
), consider the local

\gamma
–source
s_{\gamma,\mathrm{NL}}(v)
and the switch probability
p_{\mathrm{sw}}(v)
.
Define
\mathrm{osc}(h):=\sup_{x,y\in V}|h(x)-h(y)|\leq 1
.
Under the logit structure of
Section

E.1
(after the global shift by
-\gamma
), the only
\gamma
–dependence among allowed NL moves is:

•

If
v\notin\{a_{2},b_{2},a_{n-1},b_{n-1}\}
, then
\partial_{\gamma}o(v,k)=+1
for
k\in\mathcal{S}(v)
and

\partial_{\gamma}o(v,k)=0
for
k\in\mathcal{N}(v)\setminus\mathcal{S}(v)
.

•

If
v\in\{a_{2},b_{2}\}
, then in addition
\partial_{\gamma}o(v,v^{-})=-1
for the unique same–chain backward neighbor
v^{-}\in\mathcal{N}(v)
.

•

If
v\in\{a_{n-1},b_{n-1}\}
, then in addition
\partial_{\gamma}o(v,v^{+})=-1
for the unique same–chain forward neighbor
v^{+}\in\mathcal{N}(v)
.

Then:

(A) Generic vertices
v\notin\{a_{2},b_{2},a_{n-1},b_{n-1}\}
.

s_{\gamma,\mathrm{NL}}(v)=\sum_{k\in\mathcal{S}(v)}p_{\mathrm{NL}}(v\to k)\big(h(k)-h(v)\big),\qquad\text{hence}\qquad|s_{\gamma,\mathrm{NL}}(v)|\leq p_{\mathrm{sw}}(v)\,\mathrm{osc}(h)\leq p_{\mathrm{sw}}(v).

(B) Second vertex
v\in\{a_{2},b_{2}\}
.

s_{\gamma,\mathrm{NL}}(v)=\sum_{k\in\mathcal{S}(v)}p_{\mathrm{NL}}(v\to k)\big(h(k)-h(v)\big)\;-\;p_{\mathrm{NL}}(v\to v^{-})\big(h(v^{-})-h(v)\big).

Consequently,

|s_{\gamma,\mathrm{NL}}(v)|\leq p_{\mathrm{sw}}(v)\,\mathrm{osc}(h)\;+\;p_{\mathrm{NL}}(v\to v^{-})\,|h(v^{-})-h(v)|\leq p_{\mathrm{sw}}(v)\;+\;p_{\mathrm{NL}}(v\to v^{-})\,|h(v^{-})-h(v)|.

(C) Penultimate vertex
v\in\{a_{n-1},b_{n-1}\}
.

s_{\gamma,\mathrm{NL}}(v)=\sum_{k\in\mathcal{S}(v)}p_{\mathrm{NL}}(v\to k)\big(h(k)-h(v)\big)\;-\;p_{\mathrm{NL}}(v\to v^{+})\big(h(v^{+})-h(v)\big).

Consequently,

|s_{\gamma,\mathrm{NL}}(v)|\leq p_{\mathrm{sw}}(v)\,\mathrm{osc}(h)\;+\;p_{\mathrm{NL}}(v\to v^{+})\,|h(v^{+})-h(v)|\leq p_{\mathrm{sw}}(v)\;+\;p_{\mathrm{NL}}(v\to v^{+})\,|h(v^{+})-h(v)|.

Proof.

Fix
v\in U
. Abbreviate
p_{k}:=p_{\mathrm{NL}}(v\to k)
and
o_{k}:=o(v,k)
for
k\in\mathcal{N}(v)
.
By the (restricted) softmax derivative identity (Lemma
62
),

\partial_{\gamma}p_{k}=p_{k}\Big(\partial_{\gamma}o_{k}-\sum_{k^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}(v)}p_{k^{\prime}}\,\partial_{\gamma}o_{k^{\prime}}\Big).

Therefore

\displaystyle s_{\gamma,\mathrm{NL}}(v)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in\mathcal{N}(v)}\big(\partial_{\gamma}p_{k}\big)\,h(k)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in\mathcal{N}(v)}p_{k}\,h(k)\Big(\partial_{\gamma}o_{k}-\sum_{k^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}(v)}p_{k^{\prime}}\,\partial_{\gamma}o_{k^{\prime}}\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in\mathcal{N}(v)}p_{k}\,h(k)\,\partial_{\gamma}o_{k}-\Big(\sum_{k\in\mathcal{N}(v)}p_{k}\,h(k)\Big)\Big(\sum_{k^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}(v)}p_{k^{\prime}}\,\partial_{\gamma}o_{k^{\prime}}\Big).

Since
h
is harmonic at transient states for the NL chain,
h(v)={\mathcal{E}}_{v}[h(X_{1})]
, we can rewrite this in centered form as

s_{\gamma,\mathrm{NL}}(v)=\sum_{k\in\mathcal{N}(v)}p_{k}\,\big(\partial_{\gamma}o_{k}\big)\,\big(h(k)-h(v)\big).

Now apply the case split on
\partial_{\gamma}o(v,\cdot)
listed in the statement:

•

If
v\notin\{a_{2},b_{2},a_{n-1},b_{n-1}\}
, then
\partial_{\gamma}o(v,k)=1
on
k\in\mathcal{S}(v)
and zero otherwise, giving (A).

•

If
v\in\{a_{2},b_{2}\}
, then in addition
\partial_{\gamma}o(v,v^{-})=-1
, giving (B).

•

If
v\in\{a_{n-1},b_{n-1}\}
, then in addition
\partial_{\gamma}o(v,v^{+})=-1
, giving (C).

The displayed upper bounds follow from the triangle inequality together with
|h(k)-h(v)|\leq\mathrm{osc}(h)\leq 1
.
∎

Lemma 32
0
\alpha
-source in NL chain).

In the notation of the NL setup above (paragraph
E.4
), let
v^{+}
be the same–chain forward neighbor of
v
when it exists.
Let
v^{+}
denote the unique same–chain forward neighbor of
v
(so
v^{+}=a_{i+1}
if

v=a_{i}
with
i\leq n-1
, and similarly on chain
B
; in particular
a_{n-1}^{+}=a_{n}
and

b_{n-1}^{+}=b_{n}
). Then

s_{\alpha,\mathrm{NL}}(v)=p_{\mathrm{NL}}(v\to v^{+})\Big(h(v^{+})-h(v)\Big).

(31)

Moreover,

|s_{\alpha,\mathrm{NL}}(v)|\leq p_{\mathrm{NL}}(v\to v^{+})\,\mathrm{osc}_{v}(h)\leq p_{\mathrm{NL}}(v\to v^{+})\leq 1,\qquad\mathrm{osc}_{v}(h):=\max_{k\in\mathcal{N}(v)}h(k)-\min_{k\in\mathcal{N}(v)}h(k)\leq 1.

(32)

Proof.

Fix
v\in U
. Abbreviate
p_{k}:=p_{\mathrm{NL}}(v\to k)
and
o_{k}:=o(v,k)
for
k\in\mathcal{N}(v)
.
For restricted softmax,

\partial_{\alpha}p_{k}=p_{k}\Big(\partial_{\alpha}o_{k}-\sum_{k^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}(v)}p_{k^{\prime}}\,\partial_{\alpha}o_{k^{\prime}}\Big).

By the specified logits,
\alpha
appears only on the forward edge
k=v^{+}
with coefficient
+1
,
and nowhere else among allowed neighbors. Hence

\partial_{\alpha}o_{k}=\begin{cases}1,&k=v^{+},\\
0,&k\in\mathcal{N}(v)\setminus\{v^{+}\}.\end{cases}

Therefore,

\sum_{k^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}(v)}p_{k^{\prime}}\,\partial_{\alpha}o_{k^{\prime}}=p_{\mathrm{NL}}(v\to v^{+}),\qquad\sum_{k\in\mathcal{N}(v)}p_{k}\,h(k)\,\partial_{\alpha}o_{k}=p_{\mathrm{NL}}(v\to v^{+})\,h(v^{+}).

Now expand

s_{\alpha,\mathrm{NL}}(v)=\sum_{k\in\mathcal{N}(v)}(\partial_{\alpha}p_{k})\,h(k)=\sum_{k\in\mathcal{N}(v)}p_{k}h(k)\partial_{\alpha}o_{k}-\Big(\sum_{k\in\mathcal{N}(v)}p_{k}h(k)\Big)\Big(\sum_{k^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}(v)}p_{k^{\prime}}\partial_{\alpha}o_{k^{\prime}}\Big),

and substitute the two identities above to obtain (
31
).

For (
32
), note that
h
is harmonic for the NL chain at transient
v
, so

h(v)={\mathcal{E}}_{v}[h(X_{1})]=\sum_{k\in\mathcal{N}(v)}p_{k}\,h(k)
is a convex combination of
\{h(k):k\in\mathcal{N}(v)\}
.
Hence
|h(v^{+})-h(v)|\leq\mathrm{osc}_{v}(h)\leq 1
. Multiply by
p_{\mathrm{NL}}(v\to v^{+})
.
∎

Lemma 33
0
\beta
-source in NL chain).

In the notation of the NL setup above (paragraph
E.4
), let
v^{-}
be the same–chain backward neighbor of
v
when it exists.
Let
v^{-}
denote the unique same–chain backward neighbor of
v
when it exists
(so
v^{-}=a_{i-1}
if
v=a_{i}
with
i\geq 2
, and similarly on chain
B
).
Then for all
v\in U
,

s_{\beta,\mathrm{NL}}(v)=\begin{cases}p_{\mathrm{NL}}(v\to v^{-})\Big(h(v^{-})-h(v)\Big),&\text{if }v^{-}\text{ exists},\\[2.58334pt]
0,&\text{if }v\in\{a_{1},b_{1}\}.\end{cases}

(33)

Moreover, whenever
v^{-}
exists,

|s_{\beta,\mathrm{NL}}(v)|\leq p_{\mathrm{NL}}(v\to v^{-})\,\mathrm{osc}_{v}(h)\leq p_{\mathrm{NL}}(v\to v^{-})\leq 1,\qquad\mathrm{osc}_{v}(h)\leq 1.

(34)

Proof.

Fix
v\in U
. If
v\in\{a_{1},b_{1}\}
, the logits do not contain
\beta
on any allowed edge,
so
\partial_{\beta}o(v,k)=0
for all
k\in\mathcal{N}(v)
and hence
s_{\beta,\mathrm{NL}}(v)=0
.

Otherwise, the specified logits place
\beta
only on the single backward edge
k=v^{-}
,
with coefficient
+1
(including the boundary case
v\in\{a_{2},b_{2}\}
where the back logit is

(\beta-\gamma)
). Thus

\partial_{\beta}o_{k}=\begin{cases}1,&k=v^{-},\\
0,&k\in\mathcal{N}(v)\setminus\{v^{-}\}.\end{cases}

The rest of the argument is identical to the proof of Lemma
32
:
use the restricted softmax derivative, expand
s_{\beta,\mathrm{NL}}(v)
, and substitute

\sum_{k^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}(v)}p_{\mathrm{NL}}(v\to k^{\prime})\,\partial_{\beta}o_{k^{\prime}}=p_{\mathrm{NL}}(v\to v^{-}),\qquad\sum_{k\in\mathcal{N}(v)}p_{\mathrm{NL}}(v\to k)\,h(k)\,\partial_{\beta}o_{k}=p_{\mathrm{NL}}(v\to v^{-})\,h(v^{-}),

to get (
33
). The bound (
34
) follows from

|h(v^{-})-h(v)|\leq\mathrm{osc}_{v}(h)\leq 1
(and
{\mathcal{E}}_{v}[h(X_{1})]=h(v)
by harmonicity).
∎

E.5.1
Derivative estimates

Having computed the source functions for the NL chain in the preceding lemmas, we now derive quantitative bounds on the derivatives of the same-side absorption probability.

Lemma 34
0Truncation bound for
\partial_{\beta}u
).

In the notation of the NL setup above (paragraph
E.4
), let

u(x):=\Pr^{NL}_{x}[X_{\tau}=a_{n}],\qquad u_{\beta}(x):=\partial_{\beta}u(x),

and fix
v=a_{n-k}
with
k
independent of
n
.
Assume the base model assumptions of Assumption
1
(in particular, a uniform drift gap
\zeta>0
).
Then there exist constants
C,c>0
(depending only on fixed parameters and
\zeta
) such that for all
n
large enough,

|u_{\beta}(v)|\ \leq\ C\,\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\zeta}\,p_{b}^{(2\lfloor n^{1/4}\rfloor)}\,p_{\mathrm{sw}}^{\max}\;+\;C\,n\,\exp\!\left(-\frac{c^{2}}{8}\,n^{1/4}\right)\cdot\sup_{x\notin\{a_{n},b_{n}\}}|s_{\beta,\mathrm{NL}}(x)|.

Proof.

Fix
K:=\lfloor n^{1/4}\rfloor
. By the trajectory/source representation (Lemma
16
) for the NL chain,

u_{\beta}(v)={\mathcal{E}}_{v}\!\left[\sum_{t=0}^{\tau-1}s_{\beta,\mathrm{NL}}(X_{t})\right].

Decompose on
\{\tau\leq K\}
and
\{\tau>K\}
. On
\{\tau\leq K\}
, the sum has at most
K
terms and the depth can increase by at most one per step, so every visited state
satisfies
D(X_{t})\leq D(v)+t\leq k+K\leq 2K
for all large
n
. Therefore,

|u_{\beta}(v)|\leq K\cdot\sup_{x:\,D(x)\leq 2K}\,|s_{\beta,\mathrm{NL}}(x)|.

By Lemma
33
,

|s_{\beta,\mathrm{NL}}(x)|\leq p_{b}(x)\,|u(B(x))-u(x)|.

By Lemma
30
,

|u(B(x))-u(x)|\leq p_{\mathrm{sw}}^{\max}{\mathcal{E}}_{x}[\tau].

Under the base model forward-bias gap
\zeta>0
, the depth-to-terminal process has
{\mathcal{E}}_{x}[\tau]\leq D(x)/\zeta\leq 2K/\zeta
on the band
D(x)\leq 2K
.
Combining yields

\sup_{x:\,D(x)\leq 2K}|s_{\beta,\mathrm{NL}}(x)|\ \leq\ C\,p_{b}^{(2K)}\,p_{\mathrm{sw}}^{\max}\cdot\frac{2K}{\zeta},

for a constant
C>0
, hence
|u_{\beta}(v)|\lesssim\frac{K^{2}}{\zeta}\,p_{b}^{(2K)}p_{\mathrm{sw}}^{\max}
.
For
\{\tau>K\}
, write
\sum_{t=0}^{\tau-1}=\sum_{t=0}^{K-1}+\sum_{t=K}^{\tau-1}
. The first part is bounded identically by the same
K
-step band argument above. For the tail, since
\sup_{x}{\mathcal{E}}_{x}[\tau]\leq n/\zeta
under the drift gap and
|s_{\beta,\mathrm{NL}}(x)|\leq\sup_{y}|s_{\beta,\mathrm{NL}}(y)|
,
the contribution after time
K
is at most on the order of
n\cdot\sup_{y}|s_{\beta,\mathrm{NL}}(y)|
.
Multiplying by
\Pr_{v}(\tau>K)
and using Lemma
65
(with
t=\lfloor n^{1/4}\rfloor
) gives

\Pr_{v}(\tau>K)\cdot n\cdot\sup_{y}|s_{\beta,\mathrm{NL}}(y)|\ \leq\ n\cdot\exp\!\left(-\frac{c^{2}}{8}\,n^{1/4}\right)\cdot\sup_{y}|s_{\beta,\mathrm{NL}}(y)|,

∎

The next lemma is completely analogous to Lemma
34
and is included for completeness.

Lemma 35
0Truncation bound for
\partial_{\alpha}u
).

In the notation of the NL setup above (paragraph
E.4
), let

u(x):=\Pr^{NL}_{x}[X_{\tau}=a_{n}],\qquad u_{\alpha}(x):=\partial_{\alpha}u(x),

and fix
v=a_{n-k}
with
k
independent of
n
.
Assume the base model assumptions of Assumption
1
(in particular, a uniform drift gap
\zeta>0
).
Then there exist constants
C,c>0
(depending only on fixed parameters and
\zeta
) such that for all
n
large enough,

|u_{\alpha}(v)|\ \leq\ C\,\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\zeta}\,p_{f}^{(2\lfloor n^{1/4}\rfloor)}\,p_{\mathrm{sw}}^{\max}\;+\;C\,n\,\exp\!\left(-\frac{c^{2}}{8}\,n^{1/4}\right)\cdot\sup_{x\notin\{a_{n},b_{n}\}}|s_{\alpha,\mathrm{NL}}(x)|,

where
p_{f}^{(m)}:=\sup\{p_{\mathrm{NL}}(x\to x^{+}):\ x\notin\{a_{n},b_{n}\},\ D(x)\leq m\}
.

Proof.

Fix
K:=\lfloor n^{1/4}\rfloor
. By the trajectory/source representation (Lemma
16
) for the NL chain,

u_{\alpha}(v)={\mathcal{E}}_{v}\!\left[\sum_{t=0}^{\tau-1}s_{\alpha,\mathrm{NL}}(X_{t})\right].

Decompose on
\{\tau\leq K\}
and
\{\tau>K\}
. On
\{\tau\leq K\}
, the sum has at most
K
terms and the depth can increase by at most one per step, so every visited state
satisfies
D(X_{t})\leq D(v)+t\leq k+K\leq 2K
for all large
n
. Therefore,

|u_{\alpha}(v)|\leq K\cdot\sup_{x:\,D(x)\leq 2K}\,|s_{\alpha,\mathrm{NL}}(x)|.

By Lemma
32
,

|s_{\alpha,\mathrm{NL}}(x)|\leq p_{\mathrm{NL}}(x\to x^{+})\,|u(x^{+})-u(x)|.

By Lemma
30
(applied to the appropriate chain),

|u(x^{+})-u(x)|\leq p_{\mathrm{sw}}^{\max}{\mathcal{E}}_{x}[\tau].

Under the base model forward-bias gap
\zeta>0
, the depth-to-terminal process has
{\mathcal{E}}_{x}[\tau]\leq D(x)/\zeta\leq 2K/\zeta
on the band
D(x)\leq 2K
.
Combining yields

\sup_{x:\,D(x)\leq 2K}|s_{\alpha,\mathrm{NL}}(x)|\ \leq\ C\,p_{f}^{(2K)}\,p_{\mathrm{sw}}^{\max}\cdot\frac{2K}{\zeta},

for a constant
C>0
, hence
|u_{\alpha}(v)|\lesssim\frac{K^{2}}{\zeta}\,p_{f}^{(2K)}p_{\mathrm{sw}}^{\max}
.
For
\{\tau>K\}
, write
\sum_{t=0}^{\tau-1}=\sum_{t=0}^{K-1}+\sum_{t=K}^{\tau-1}
. The first part is bounded identically by the same
K
-step band argument above. For the tail, since
\sup_{x}{\mathcal{E}}_{x}[\tau]\leq n/\zeta
under the drift gap and
|s_{\alpha,\mathrm{NL}}(x)|\leq\sup_{y}|s_{\alpha,\mathrm{NL}}(y)|
,
the contribution after time
K
is at most on the order of
n\cdot\sup_{y}|s_{\alpha,\mathrm{NL}}(y)|
.
Multiplying by
\Pr_{v}(\tau>K)
and using Lemma
65
(with
t=\lfloor n^{1/4}\rfloor
) gives

\Pr_{v}(\tau>K)\cdot n\cdot\sup_{y}|s_{\alpha,\mathrm{NL}}(y)|\ \leq\ n\cdot\exp\!\left(-\frac{c^{2}}{8}\,n^{1/4}\right)\cdot\sup_{y}|s_{\alpha,\mathrm{NL}}(y)|,

as needed.
∎

The next lemma is completely analogous to Lemmas
34
and
35
and is included for completeness.

Lemma 36
0Truncation bound for
\partial_{\gamma}u
).

In the notation of the NL setup above (paragraph
E.4
), let

u(x):=\Pr^{NL}_{x}[X_{\tau}=a_{n}],\qquad u_{\gamma}(x):=\partial_{\gamma}u(x),

and fix
v=a_{n-k}
with
k
independent of
n
.
Assume the base model assumptions of Assumption
1
(in particular, a uniform drift gap
\zeta>0
).
Then there exist constants
C,c>0
(depending only on fixed parameters and
\zeta
) such that for all
n
large enough,

|u_{\gamma}(v)|\ \leq\ C\,n^{1/4}\,p_{\mathrm{sw}}^{\max}\;+\;C\,\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\zeta}\,p_{f}^{(2\lfloor n^{1/4}\rfloor)}\,p_{\mathrm{sw}}^{\max}\;+\;C\,n\,\exp\!\left(-\frac{c^{2}}{8}\,n^{1/4}\right)\cdot\sup_{x\notin\{a_{n},b_{n}\}}|s_{\gamma,\mathrm{NL}}(x)|,

where
p_{f}^{(m)}:=\sup\{p_{\mathrm{NL}}(x\to x^{+}):\ x\notin\{a_{n},b_{n}\},\ D(x)\leq m\}
.

Proof.

Fix
K:=\lfloor n^{1/4}\rfloor
. By the trajectory/source representation (Lemma
16
) for the NL chain,

u_{\gamma}(v)={\mathcal{E}}_{v}\!\left[\sum_{t=0}^{\tau-1}s_{\gamma,\mathrm{NL}}(X_{t})\right].

Decompose on
\{\tau\leq K\}
and
\{\tau>K\}
. On
\{\tau\leq K\}
, the sum has at most
K
terms and the depth can increase by at most one per step, so every visited state
satisfies
D(X_{t})\leq D(v)+t\leq k+K\leq 2K
for all large
n
. Therefore,

|u_{\gamma}(v)|\leq K\cdot\sup_{x:\,D(x)\leq 2K}\,|s_{\gamma,\mathrm{NL}}(x)|.

For
x
with
D(x)\leq 2K
, Lemma
31
gives a uniform upper bound:
by part (A) we have
|s_{\gamma,\mathrm{NL}}(x)|\leq p_{\mathrm{sw}}(x)
, while at the boundary
x\in\{a_{n-1},b_{n-1}\}
part (C) yields

|s_{\gamma,\mathrm{NL}}(x)|\leq p_{\mathrm{sw}}(x)+p_{\mathrm{NL}}(x\to x^{+})\,|u(x^{+})-u(x)|.

By Lemma
30
(applied to the appropriate chain),

|u(x^{+})-u(x)|\leq p_{\mathrm{sw}}^{\max}{\mathcal{E}}_{x}[\tau].

Under the base model forward-bias gap
\zeta>0
, the depth-to-terminal process has
{\mathcal{E}}_{x}[\tau]\leq D(x)/\zeta\leq 2K/\zeta
on the band
D(x)\leq 2K
.
Combining yields

\sup_{x:\,D(x)\leq 2K}|s_{\gamma,\mathrm{NL}}(x)|\ \leq\ C\,p_{\mathrm{sw}}^{\max}\;+\;C\,p_{f}^{(2K)}\,p_{\mathrm{sw}}^{\max}\cdot\frac{2K}{\zeta},

and hence
|u_{\gamma}(v)|\lesssim K\,p_{\mathrm{sw}}^{\max}+\frac{K^{2}}{\zeta}\,p_{f}^{(2K)}p_{\mathrm{sw}}^{\max}
.
For
\{\tau>K\}
, write
\sum_{t=0}^{\tau-1}=\sum_{t=0}^{K-1}+\sum_{t=K}^{\tau-1}
. The first part is bounded identically by the same
K
-step band argument above. For the tail, since
\sup_{x}{\mathcal{E}}_{x}[\tau]\leq n/\zeta
under the drift gap and
|s_{\gamma,\mathrm{NL}}(x)|\leq\sup_{y}|s_{\gamma,\mathrm{NL}}(y)|
,
the contribution after time
K
is at most on the order of
n\cdot\sup_{y}|s_{\gamma,\mathrm{NL}}(y)|
.
Multiplying by
\Pr_{v}(\tau>K)
and using Lemma
65
(with
t=\lfloor n^{1/4}\rfloor
) gives

\Pr_{v}(\tau>K)\cdot n\cdot\sup_{y}|s_{\gamma,\mathrm{NL}}(y)|\ \leq\ n\cdot\exp\!\left(-\frac{c^{2}}{8}\,n^{1/4}\right)\cdot\sup_{y}|s_{\gamma,\mathrm{NL}}(y)|,

as needed.
∎

E.6
Monotonicity of
\alpha,\beta,\gamma
and derivative estimates

We establish monotonicity and estimates for the derivativesof
\alpha,\beta,\gamma
at initialization, but they in fact hold throughout the learning process as long as the forward transition probability remains non-decreasing and the long-jump probability stays bounded away from zero. Since our analysis shows that both conditions are maintained for all
t\leq t_{0}
, the estimates below in fact hold for all
t\leq t_{0}
.

Lemma 37
0Transition mass at penultimate vertex).

Assume
\gamma>0
and the logit structure of
Section

E.1
(after the global shift by
-\gamma
),
and consider the original chain (equivalently, the long–jump–absorbing chain before redirection of long jumps).
For a transient state
x\notin\{a_{n},b_{n}\}
, let

\displaystyle p_{b}(x)

\displaystyle:=\Pr(X_{1}=B(x)\mid X_{0}=x)\quad(\text{set }p_{b}(x):=0\text{ if }B(x)\text{ does not exist}),

\displaystyle p(e;x)

\displaystyle:=\Pr(\text{a long jump at time }1\mid X_{0}=x),

where
B(x)
is the same–chain backward neighbor when it exists. Then
there exists
n_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}}
(depending only on fixed parameters
\alpha,\beta,\gamma
) such that for all
n\geq n_{0}
,

\max_{x\notin\{a_{n},b_{n}\}}p_{b}(x)\ \leq\ 2\,p_{b}(a_{n-1}),\qquad\max_{x\notin\{a_{n},b_{n}\}}p(e;x)\ \leq\ 2\,p(e;a_{n-1}),

and by chain-swap symmetry the same bounds hold with
a_{n-1}
replaced by
b_{n-1}
.

Proof.

Fix a chain (say
A
); the statement for chain
B
follows by chain-swap symmetry.
Write the one-step kernel as a softmax over logits: for each transient
x
and each allowed target
y
,

P(x,y)=\frac{e^{o(x,y)}}{Z(x)},\qquad Z(x):=\sum_{z}e^{o(x,z)},

where the sum ranges over all original one-step targets
z
from
x
(including long-jump targets).
Therefore

p_{b}(x)=\frac{e^{o(x,B(x))}}{Z(x)},\qquad p(e;x)=\sum_{z\in L(x)}\frac{e^{o(x,z)}}{Z(x)}=\frac{\sum_{z\in L(x)}e^{o(x,z)}}{Z(x)}.

Under the logit specification of
Section

E.1
(after the harmless global shift by
-\gamma
), the logits take only finitely many values,
and both the numerator
\sum_{z\in L(x)}e^{o(x,z)}
and the normalizer
Z(x)
can be written explicitly in each of the four boundary regimes.
In particular (see the enumeration underlying Lemma
22
for the corresponding target counts and denominators):

•

Deep middle
x=a_{i}
,
2<i<n-1
:

Z_{\mathrm{mid}}=e^{\alpha}+e^{\beta}+4+(2n-7)e^{\gamma},\qquad p_{b}(a_{i})=\frac{e^{\beta}}{Z_{\mathrm{mid}}},\qquad p(e;a_{i})=\frac{(2n-10)e^{\gamma}}{Z_{\mathrm{mid}}}.

•

Second vertex
x=a_{2}
:

Z_{2}=e^{\alpha}+e^{(\beta-\gamma)}+3+(2n-6)e^{\gamma},\qquad p_{b}(a_{2})=\frac{e^{(\beta-\gamma)}}{Z_{2}},\qquad p(e;a_{2})=\frac{(2n-8)e^{\gamma}}{Z_{2}}.

•

First vertex
x=a_{1}
:

Z_{1}=e^{\alpha}+3+(2n-5)e^{\gamma},\qquad p_{b}(a_{1})=0,\qquad p(e;a_{1})=\frac{(2n-6)e^{\gamma}}{Z_{1}}.

•

Penultimate vertex
x=a_{n-1}
:

Z_{n-1}=e^{(\alpha-\gamma)}+e^{\beta}+3+(2n-6)e^{\gamma},\qquad p_{b}(a_{n-1})=\frac{e^{\beta}}{Z_{n-1}},\qquad p(e;a_{n-1})=\frac{(2n-8)e^{\gamma}}{Z_{n-1}}.

Set
C_{\max}:=e^{\alpha}+e^{\beta}+4
. For every transient
x
on chain
A
, the normalizer has the form

Z(x)=C_{x}+c_{x}e^{\gamma},\qquad 0\leq C_{x}\leq C_{\max},\qquad c_{x}\in\{2n-7,\,2n-6,\,2n-5\},

and at the penultimate vertex
Z_{n-1}=C_{n-1}+(2n-6)e^{\gamma}
with
0\leq C_{n-1}\leq C_{\max}
.
Choose
n_{0}
so that
(2n_{0}-6)e^{\gamma}\geq 2C_{\max}
. Then for all
n\geq n_{0}
,

Z_{n-1}\ \leq\ (2n-6)e^{\gamma}+C_{\max}\ \leq\ \tfrac{3}{2}(2n-6)e^{\gamma},\qquad Z(x)\ \geq\ (2n-7)e^{\gamma}.

We first bound
p_{b}
. The only vertices with a backward move are
a_{i}
for
i\geq 2
. Among these,
the backward weight is
e^{\beta}
except at
a_{2}
, where it is
e^{(\beta-\gamma)}\leq e^{\beta}
.
Hence for all
x
on chain
A
,

p_{b}(x)\ \leq\ \frac{e^{\beta}}{(2n-7)e^{\gamma}}.

On the other hand,

p_{b}(a_{n-1})=\frac{e^{\beta}}{Z_{n-1}}\ \geq\ \frac{e^{\beta}}{\tfrac{3}{2}(2n-6)e^{\gamma}}.

Therefore, for all
n\geq n_{0}
,

\frac{p_{b}(x)}{p_{b}(a_{n-1})}\ \leq\ \frac{\tfrac{3}{2}(2n-6)}{(2n-7)}\ \leq\ 2,

which proves
\max_{x}p_{b}(x)\leq 2p_{b}(a_{n-1})
on chain
A
.

Next we bound the long-jump mass. In each of the cases above we have
p(e;x)=N_{x}e^{\gamma}/Z(x)
with

N_{x}\in\{2n-6,2n-8,2n-10\}
. Thus for all
x
on chain
A
and all
n\geq n_{0}
,

p(e;x)\ \leq\ \frac{(2n-6)e^{\gamma}}{(2n-7)e^{\gamma}}=\frac{2n-6}{2n-7}.

Meanwhile,

p(e;a_{n-1})=\frac{(2n-8)e^{\gamma}}{Z_{n-1}}\ \geq\ \frac{(2n-8)e^{\gamma}}{\tfrac{3}{2}(2n-6)e^{\gamma}}=\frac{2}{3}\cdot\frac{2n-8}{2n-6}.

For
n
large enough (in particular, for
n\geq n_{0}
after possibly increasing
n_{0}
by a fixed constant),
the ratio
\frac{2n-6}{2n-7}\Big/\Big(\frac{2}{3}\cdot\frac{2n-8}{2n-6}\Big)
is at most
2
, and therefore

\max_{x}p(e;x)\leq 2p(e;a_{n-1})
on chain
A
. The same argument applies on chain
B
.
∎

Recall the derivative decomposition from Lemma
15
. For a parameter

\theta\in\{\alpha,\beta,\gamma\}
and a start state
v
, we have

\partial_{\theta}S_{\theta}(v)=-(\partial_{\theta}h_{\theta}(v))\Big(\bar{S}^{NL}_{\theta}(v)-\tfrac{1}{2}\Big)+(1-h_{\theta}(v))\,\partial_{\theta}\bar{S}^{NL}_{\theta}(v),

where:

•

S_{\theta}(v):=\Pr^{\theta}_{v}[X_{\tau}=a_{n}]
is the same–side absorption probability in the original chain.

•

h_{\theta}(v)
is the probability of a long jump before absorption, which by definition equals the absorption
probability at
S_{LJ}
in the long–jump–absorbing chain where long jumps are redirected to the absorbing state
S_{LJ}
.

•

\bar{S}^{NL}_{\theta}(v):=\Pr^{\theta}_{v}(X_{\tau}=a_{n}\mid NL)
is the same–side absorption probability conditional on
no long jump occurring (i.e. in the no–long–jump chain).

•

By symmetry,
\bar{S}^{LJ}_{\theta}(v)=\tfrac{1}{2}
, so the long–jump conditional absorption probability equals
1/2
.

We will prove monotonicity results for each of the three parameters
\theta\in\{\alpha,\beta,\gamma\}
by showing
that the term involving the long–jump–absorbing chain,
-(\partial_{\theta}h_{\theta}(v))\Big(\bar{S}^{NL}_{\theta}(v)-\tfrac{1}{2}\Big)
,
dominates the remaining term
(1-h_{\theta}(v))\,\partial_{\theta}\bar{S}^{NL}_{\theta}(v)
in the derivative decomposition.

Lemma 38
0Monotonicity of
\alpha
).

Fix a constant
K\geq 1
independent of
n
and initialize at
v_{0}:=a_{n-K}
.
For all
n
large enough, the long–jump term in the decomposition of
Lemma
15
dominates the NL term, in the sense that

\partial_{\alpha}S_{\alpha}(v_{0})\ \geq\ \frac{1}{8}(\partial_{\alpha}h_{\alpha}(v_{0}))\geq 0.

Moreover,
\partial_{\alpha}h_{\alpha}(v_{0})\leq 0
, so the right-hand side is nonnegative.
In particular,
\partial_{\alpha}S_{\alpha}(v_{0})\geq 0
, so the same–side absorption probability
is (near-terminal) monotonically increasing in
\alpha
.

Proof.

Recall the derivative decomposition (Lemma
15
):

\partial_{\alpha}S_{\alpha}(v_{0})=-(\partial_{\alpha}h_{\alpha}(v_{0}))\Big(\bar{S}^{NL}_{\alpha}(v_{0})-\tfrac{1}{2}\Big)(1-h_{\alpha}(v_{0}))\,\partial_{\alpha}\bar{S}^{NL}_{\alpha}(v_{0}).

We first lower bound the long–jump contribution. By Lemma
24
,
together with Corollary
2
and the base model assumption

p_{\mathrm{fwd}}\geq\zeta
(Assumption
1
), there exists a constant

c_{\mathrm{LJ}}>0
(depending only on fixed parameters and
K
) such that

|\partial_{\alpha}h_{\alpha}(v_{0})|\ \geq\ c_{\mathrm{LJ}}\cdot p(e),\qquad p(e):=P^{\mathrm{LJ\text{-}abs}}(v_{0},e).

Moreover, by Lemma
29
applied in the NL chain at depth
K
,
for
n
large enough we have
\bar{S}^{NL}_{\alpha}(v_{0})\geq\tfrac{3}{4}
, hence

\bar{S}^{NL}_{\alpha}(v_{0})-\tfrac{1}{2}\geq\tfrac{1}{4}
.

We next upper bound the NL contribution. By the truncation bound in Lemma
35

(completely analogous to Lemma
34
), we have

\big|\partial_{\alpha}\bar{S}^{NL}_{\alpha}(v_{0})\big|\ \leq\ C\,\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\zeta}\,p_{f}^{(2\lfloor n^{1/4}\rfloor)}\,p_{\mathrm{sw}}^{\max}\;+\;C\,n\,\exp\!\left(-\frac{c^{2}}{8}\,n^{1/4}\right)\cdot\sup_{x\notin\{a_{n},b_{n}\}}|s_{\alpha,\mathrm{NL}}(x)|.

Using Lemma
30
,
\sup_{x}|s_{\alpha,\mathrm{NL}}(x)|\lesssim p_{\mathrm{sw}}^{\max}\sup_{x}{\mathcal{E}}_{x}^{NL}[\tau]\lesssim(n/\zeta)\,p_{\mathrm{sw}}^{\max}

and the exponential tail
\exp(-\Omega(n^{1/4}))
, the last term is a negligible fraction of
p_{\mathrm{sw}}^{\max}
.
Since
p_{f}^{(2\lfloor n^{1/4}\rfloor)}\leq 1
, we obtain
\big|\partial_{\alpha}\bar{S}^{NL}_{\alpha}(v_{0})\big|\leq C\sqrt{n}\,p_{\mathrm{sw}}^{\max}
for all
n
large enough.
Finally, note that the relevant comparison is with the one-step long-jump probability at the initialization,

p(e):=P^{\mathrm{LJ\text{-}abs}}(v_{0},e)
, which depends on
v_{0}
through the local softmax denominator.
Nevertheless, the same
1/n
ratio holds uniformly. Indeed, from any state
x
, the NL switch set has only
O(1)

available targets (at most three), each with logit at most
\gamma
, while the long-jump class consists of
\Theta(n)

targets at the same
\gamma
-logit scale. One can check this separately in the deep middle (where there are
2n+O(1)

long-jump targets) and at the penultimate vertex (where there are again
2n+O(1)
long-jump targets); in both cases the
ratio of the switch numerator to the long-jump numerator is
O(1/n)
, and conditioning on
NL
only rescales by a factor
bounded away from
0
(since the forward move has probability at least
\zeta
by Assumption
1
).
Consequently, for all
n
large enough we have the domination
p_{\mathrm{sw}}^{\max}\leq C\,p(e)/n
. Therefore,

\big|(1-h_{\alpha}(v_{0}))\,\partial_{\alpha}\bar{S}^{NL}_{\alpha}(v_{0})\big|\ \leq\ \frac{1}{2}\Big(-(\partial_{\alpha}h_{\alpha}(v_{0}))\Big(\bar{S}^{NL}_{\alpha}(v_{0})-\tfrac{1}{2}\Big)\Big),

and substituting into the decomposition yields the claimed factor-
\tfrac{1}{2}
dominance and
\partial_{\alpha}S_{\alpha}(v_{0})\geq 0
.
∎

Lemma 39
0Monotonicity of
\beta
).

Fix a constant
K\geq 1
independent of
n
and initialize at
v_{0}:=a_{n-K}
.
For all
n
large enough, the long–jump term in the decomposition of
Lemma
15
dominates the NL term, in the sense that

\partial_{\beta}S_{\beta}(v_{0})\ \leq\ -\frac{1}{8}\Big((\partial_{\beta}h_{\beta}(v_{0}))\Big)\leq 0.

Moreover,
\partial_{\beta}h_{\beta}(v_{0})\geq 0
, so the right-hand side is nonpositive.
In particular,
\partial_{\beta}S_{\beta}(v_{0})\leq 0
, so the same–side absorption probability
is (near-terminal) monotonically decreasing in
\beta
.

Proof.

Recall the derivative decomposition (Lemma
15
):

\partial_{\beta}S_{\beta}(v_{0})=-(\partial_{\beta}h_{\beta}(v_{0}))\Big(\bar{S}^{NL}_{\beta}(v_{0})-\tfrac{1}{2}\Big)+(1-h_{\beta}(v_{0}))\,\partial_{\beta}\bar{S}^{NL}_{\beta}(v_{0}).

We first lower bound the long–jump contribution. By Lemma
25
,

\partial_{\beta}h_{\beta}(v_{0})\ \geq\ s_{\beta}(v_{0})\;+\;s_{\beta}(v_{\mathrm{pen}})\,\Pr_{v_{0}}(0<\sigma<\tau),

where
v_{\mathrm{pen}}:=a_{n-1}
and
\sigma:=\inf\{t\geq 0:\ X_{t}=v_{\mathrm{pen}}\}
. We may drop the one of the two terms and get that regardless of whther
n=1
or
n>1
,

\partial_{\beta}h_{\beta}(v_{0})\ \geq\ s_{\beta}(v_{\mathrm{pen}})\,\Pr_{v_{0}}(0<\sigma<\tau).

Define the one-step long-jump probability at a state
x
in the long–jump–absorbing chain by

p(e;x):=P^{\mathrm{LJ\text{-}abs}}(x,e).

By the
\beta
-source identity
s_{\beta}(x)=p_{b}(x)\big(u(B(x))-u(x)\big)
(Lemma
21
),
Corollary
2
(applied at
B(v_{\mathrm{pen}})=a_{n-1}
), and the base model lower bound

p_{\mathrm{fwd}}\geq\zeta
(Assumption
1
), we obtain that

s_{\beta}(v_{\mathrm{pen}})\ \geq\ \zeta^{K}\cdot p_{b}(v_{\mathrm{pen}})\,p(e;v_{\mathrm{pen}}).

Moreover, if
v_{0}=a_{n-K}
then the event of
K-1
consecutive forward steps implies
\{0<\sigma<\tau\}
, hence

\Pr_{v_{0}}(0<\sigma<\tau)\ \geq\ \zeta^{K-1}.

Combining,

\partial_{\beta}h_{\beta}(v_{0})\ \geq\ \zeta^{2K-1}\cdot p_{b}(v_{\mathrm{pen}})\,p(e;v_{\mathrm{pen}}).

Moreover, by
Lemma
29
applied in the NL chain at depth
K
, for
n
large enough we have

\bar{S}^{NL}_{\beta}(v_{0})\geq\tfrac{3}{4}
, hence
\bar{S}^{NL}_{\beta}(v_{0})-\tfrac{1}{2}\geq\tfrac{1}{4}
.

We next upper bound the NL contribution. By Lemma
34
,

\big|\partial_{\beta}\bar{S}^{NL}_{\beta}(v_{0})\big|\ \leq\ C\,\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\zeta}\,p_{b}^{(2\lfloor n^{1/4}\rfloor)}\,p_{\mathrm{sw}}^{\max}\;+\;C\,n\,\exp\!\left(-\frac{c^{2}}{8}\,n^{1/4}\right)\cdot\sup_{x\notin\{a_{n},b_{n}\}}|s_{\beta,\mathrm{NL}}(x)|.

We now control the NL source term.
By the NL
\beta
–source identity (Lemma
33
) and the consecutive–value bound
(Lemma
30
), for every nonterminal
x
with backward neighbor
B(x)
,

|s_{\beta,\mathrm{NL}}(x)|\leq p_{\mathrm{NL}}(x\to B(x))\,|h(B(x))-h(x)|\leq p_{\mathrm{NL}}(x\to B(x))\,p_{\mathrm{sw}}^{\max}\,{\mathcal{E}}_{x}^{NL}[\tau].

Therefore,

\sup_{x}|s_{\beta,\mathrm{NL}}(x)|\leq\Big(\sup_{x}p_{\mathrm{NL}}(x\to B(x))\Big)\,p_{\mathrm{sw}}^{\max}\,\Big(\sup_{x}{\mathcal{E}}_{x}^{NL}[\tau]\Big)\leq\frac{n}{\zeta}\Big(\sup_{x}p_{\mathrm{NL}}(x\to B(x))\Big)\,p_{\mathrm{sw}}^{\max},

using
\sup_{x}{\mathcal{E}}_{x}^{NL}[\tau]\leq n/\zeta
under the drift gap.

Next we bound both
\sup_{x}p_{\mathrm{NL}}(x\to B(x))
and
p_{\mathrm{sw}}^{\max}
in terms of penultimate-vertex
probabilities in the original / LJ-absorbing chain.
By Lemma
28
, NL transition probabilities are inflated by at most
1/\zeta
relative to
the original chain, so in particular

\sup_{x}p_{\mathrm{NL}}(x\to B(x))\leq\frac{1}{\zeta}\max_{x}p_{b}(x),\qquad p_{\mathrm{sw}}^{\max}\leq\frac{1}{\zeta}\max_{x}p_{\mathrm{sw}}^{\mathrm{orig}}(x).

Moreover, in the original chain the switch class consists of
O(1)

\gamma
-logit targets while the long-jump class
has
\Theta(n)

\gamma
-logit targets, hence
p_{\mathrm{sw}}^{\mathrm{orig}}(x)\leq C\,p(e;x)/n
for all
x
.
Combining with Lemma
37
gives, for all
n
large enough,

\sup_{x}p_{\mathrm{NL}}(x\to B(x))\ \leq\ C\,p_{b}(v_{\mathrm{pen}}),\qquad p_{\mathrm{sw}}^{\max}\ \leq\ C\,\frac{p(e;v_{\mathrm{pen}})}{n}.

Also,
p_{b}^{(2\lfloor n^{1/4}\rfloor)}\leq\sup_{x}p_{\mathrm{NL}}(x\to B(x))\leq C\,p_{b}(v_{\mathrm{pen}})
. Substituting these
bounds into the two-term upper bound from Lemma
34
yields

\big|\partial_{\beta}\bar{S}^{NL}_{\beta}(v_{0})\big|\leq C\,\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\zeta}\,p_{b}(v_{\mathrm{pen}})\cdot\frac{p(e;v_{\mathrm{pen}})}{n}\leq\frac{C^{\prime}}{\zeta\sqrt{n}}\,p_{b}(v_{\mathrm{pen}})\,p(e;v_{\mathrm{pen}})

for all
n
large enough.

Therefore, for
n
large enough we have

\big|(1-h_{\beta}(v_{0}))\,\partial_{\beta}\bar{S}^{NL}_{\beta}(v_{0})\big|\ \leq\ \frac{1}{2}\Big((\partial_{\beta}h_{\beta}(v_{0}))\Big(\bar{S}^{NL}_{\beta}(v_{0})-\tfrac{1}{2}\Big)\Big),

and substituting into the decomposition yields

\partial_{\beta}S_{\beta}(v_{0})\leq\frac{1}{2}\big(-(\partial_{\beta}h_{\beta}(v_{0}))(\bar{S}^{NL}_{\beta}(v_{0})-\tfrac{1}{2})\big)\leq 0
.
∎

Lemma 40
0Monotonicity of
\gamma
).

Fix a constant
K\geq 1
independent of
n
and initialize at
v_{0}:=a_{n-K}
.
For all
n
large enough, the long–jump term in the decomposition of
Lemma
15
dominates the NL term, in the sense that

\partial_{\gamma}S_{\gamma}(v_{0})\ \leq\ -\frac{1}{8}\Big((\partial_{\gamma}h_{\gamma}(v_{0}))\Big)\leq 0.

Moreover,
\partial_{\gamma}h_{\gamma}(v_{0})\geq 0
, so the right-hand side is nonpositive.
In particular,
\partial_{\gamma}S_{\gamma}(v_{0})\leq 0
, so the same–side absorption probability
is (near-terminal) monotonically decreasing in
\gamma
.

Proof.

We analyze the derivative decomposition (Lemma
15
):

\partial_{\gamma}S_{\gamma}(v_{0})=-(\partial_{\gamma}h_{\gamma}(v_{0}))\Big(\bar{S}^{NL}_{\gamma}(v_{0})-\tfrac{1}{2}\Big)+(1-h_{\gamma}(v_{0}))\,\partial_{\gamma}\bar{S}^{NL}_{\gamma}(v_{0}).

We first lower bound the long–jump contribution in the same way as in the
\beta
case. Let
v_{\mathrm{pen}}:=a_{n-1}
and

\sigma:=\inf\{t\geq 0:\ X_{t}=v_{\mathrm{pen}}\}
in the long–jump–absorbing chain. By Lemma
26
,

\partial_{\gamma}h_{\gamma}(v_{0})\ \geq\ s_{\gamma}(v_{\mathrm{pen}})\,\Pr_{v_{0}}(0<\sigma<\tau)\;+\;\frac{1}{2}\,s_{\gamma}(v_{0})\ \geq\ s_{\gamma}(v_{\mathrm{pen}})\,\Pr_{v_{0}}(0<\sigma<\tau),

since
s_{\gamma}(v_{0})\geq 0
. Moreover, if
v_{0}=a_{n-K}
then the event of
K-1
consecutive forward steps implies
\{0<\sigma<\tau\}
, hence

\Pr_{v_{0}}(0<\sigma<\tau)\ \geq\ \zeta^{K-1}

by the base model lower bound
p_{\mathrm{fwd}}\geq\zeta
(Assumption
1
). Define the one-step long-jump probability at a state
x
in the
long–jump–absorbing chain by
p(e;x):=P^{\mathrm{LJ\text{-}abs}}(x,e)
. At the penultimate vertex, Lemma
23
(C) gives

s_{\gamma}(v_{\mathrm{pen}})\ \geq\ p(e;v_{\mathrm{pen}})\,\bigl(1-u(v_{\mathrm{pen}})\bigr)\ \geq\ \zeta\cdot p(e;v_{\mathrm{pen}}),

where
u(x)=\Pr_{x}[X_{\tau}=e]
and the last inequality uses
2
.
Combining these and considering cases where
n=1
or
n>1
yields
\partial_{\gamma}h_{\gamma}(v_{0})\geq 0.5\cdot\cdot p(e;v_{\mathrm{pen}})\,\zeta^{K}
. Moreover, by Lemma
29

applied in the NL chain at depth
K
, for
n
large enough we have
\bar{S}^{NL}_{\gamma}(v_{0})\geq\tfrac{3}{4}
, hence
\bar{S}^{NL}_{\gamma}(v_{0})-\tfrac{1}{2}\geq\tfrac{1}{4}
.

We next upper bound the NL contribution. By Lemma
36
,

\big|\partial_{\gamma}\bar{S}^{NL}_{\gamma}(v_{0})\big|\ \leq\ C\,n^{1/4}\,p_{\mathrm{sw}}^{\max}\;+\;C\,\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\zeta}\,p_{f}^{(2\lfloor n^{1/4}\rfloor)}\,p_{\mathrm{sw}}^{\max}\;+\;C\,n\,\exp\!\left(-\frac{c^{2}}{8}\,n^{1/4}\right)\cdot\sup_{x\notin\{a_{n},b_{n}\}}|s_{\gamma,\mathrm{NL}}(x)|.

We now control the three terms on the right-hand side using the same comparison-to-penultimate-vertex strategy as in the
\beta
case.

First, by Lemma
28
, NL probabilities are inflated by at most
1/\zeta
relative to the original chain.
In the original chain, the switch class consists of
O(1)

\gamma
-logit targets while the long-jump class has
\Theta(n)

\gamma
-logit targets, so
p_{\mathrm{sw}}^{\mathrm{orig}}(x)\leq C\,p(e;x)/n
for all
x
. Combining with
Lemma
37
gives, for all
n
large enough,

p_{\mathrm{sw}}^{\max}\ \leq\ \frac{1}{\zeta}\max_{x}p_{\mathrm{sw}}^{\mathrm{orig}}(x)\ \leq\ C\,\frac{\max_{x}p(e;x)}{n}\ \leq\ C\,\frac{p(e;v_{\mathrm{pen}})}{n}.

Next, we bound the NL
\gamma
-source. By the NL
\gamma
–source bounds (Lemma
31
),
each
s_{\gamma,\mathrm{NL}}(x)
is a linear combination of
p_{\mathrm{sw}}(x)
and (at the boundary) one additional
term involving a same–chain neighbor difference
|h(x^{\pm})-h(x)|
. By Lemma
30

and
\sup_{x}{\mathcal{E}}_{x}^{NL}[\tau]\leq n/\zeta
under the drift gap, these neighbor differences are at most

p_{\mathrm{sw}}^{\max}\sup_{x}{\mathcal{E}}_{x}^{NL}[\tau]\leq(n/\zeta)\,p_{\mathrm{sw}}^{\max}
. Therefore,

\sup_{x}|s_{\gamma,\mathrm{NL}}(x)|\ \leq\ C\,\frac{n}{\zeta}\,p_{\mathrm{sw}}^{\max}.

Substituting the bound on
p_{\mathrm{sw}}^{\max}
into the first two terms (using
p_{f}^{(2\lfloor n^{1/4}\rfloor)}\leq 1
),
and the source bound into the exponentially-suppressed third term, yields

\big|\partial_{\gamma}\bar{S}^{NL}_{\gamma}(v_{0})\big|\ \leq\ C\,\frac{n^{1/4}}{n}\,p(e;v_{\mathrm{pen}})\;+\;C\,\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\zeta}\cdot\frac{1}{n}\,p(e;v_{\mathrm{pen}})\;+\;C\,n\,\exp\!\left(-\frac{c^{2}}{8}\,n^{1/4}\right)\cdot\frac{n}{\zeta}\cdot\frac{1}{n}\,p(e;v_{\mathrm{pen}}).

In particular, for all
n
large enough,

\big|\partial_{\gamma}\bar{S}^{NL}_{\gamma}(v_{0})\big|\ \leq\ \frac{C^{\prime}}{\zeta\sqrt{n}}\,p(e;v_{\mathrm{pen}}).

Therefore, for
n
large enough we have

\big|(1-h_{\gamma}(v_{0}))\,\partial_{\gamma}\bar{S}^{NL}_{\gamma}(v_{0})\big|\ \leq\ \frac{1}{2}\Big((\partial_{\gamma}h_{\gamma}(v_{0}))\Big(\bar{S}^{NL}_{\gamma}(v_{0})-\tfrac{1}{2}\Big)\Big),

and substituting into the decomposition yields

\partial_{\gamma}S_{\gamma}(v_{0})\leq\frac{1}{2}\big(-(\partial_{\gamma}h_{\gamma}(v_{0}))(\bar{S}^{NL}_{\gamma}(v_{0})-\tfrac{1}{2})\big)\leq 0
.
∎

Lemma 41
0Near–terminal same–side derivative for
\gamma
).

Fix a constant
K\geq 1
independent of
n
, and initialize at

v_{0}:=a_{n-K}.

Write
p(e)
for the one–step probability of a long jump out of
v_{0}
in the original chain
(equivalently,
p(e)=P^{\mathrm{LJ\text{-}abs}}(v_{0},e)
in the long–jump–absorbing chain).
Write
p(e;x):=P^{\mathrm{LJ\text{-}abs}}(x,e)
and
p(e)_{\max}:=\sup_{x\notin\{a_{n},b_{n}\}}p(e;x)
.
Then

\big|\partial_{\gamma}S_{\gamma}(v_{0})\big|\ \leq\ C_{1,\gamma}\,\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\zeta}\,p(e)_{\max}.

Proof (upper bound).

Write the derivative decomposition (Lemma
15
) at
v_{0}
:

\partial_{\gamma}S_{\gamma}(v_{0})=-(\partial_{\gamma}h_{\gamma}(v_{0}))\Big(\bar{S}^{NL}_{\gamma}(v_{0})-\tfrac{1}{2}\Big)+(1-h_{\gamma}(v_{0}))\,\partial_{\gamma}\bar{S}^{NL}_{\gamma}(v_{0}).

In the proof of Lemma
40
(the domination step), for all
n
large enough we showed that

\big|(1-h_{\gamma}(v_{0}))\,\partial_{\gamma}\bar{S}^{NL}_{\gamma}(v_{0})\big|\ \leq\ \tfrac{1}{2}\,\big|(\partial_{\gamma}h_{\gamma}(v_{0}))\big|\cdot\Big|\bar{S}^{NL}_{\gamma}(v_{0})-\tfrac{1}{2}\Big|\ \leq\ \tfrac{1}{2}\,\big|\partial_{\gamma}h_{\gamma}(v_{0})\big|.

Therefore,

\big|\partial_{\gamma}S_{\gamma}(v_{0})\big|\ \leq\ \tfrac{3}{2}\,\big|\partial_{\gamma}h_{\gamma}(v_{0})\big|.

(35)

It remains to bound
|\partial_{\gamma}h_{\gamma}(v_{0})|
in the long–jump–absorbing chain.
Let
u(x):=\Pr_{x}[X_{\tau}=e]
denote the absorption probability at
e
(so
h_{\gamma}(\cdot)=u(\cdot)
), with

\tau:=\inf\{t\geq 0:X_{t}\in\{a_{n},b_{n},e\}\}
. By the trajectory/source representation (Lemma
16
),

\partial_{\gamma}h_{\gamma}(v_{0})=\partial_{\gamma}u(v_{0})={\mathcal{E}}_{v_{0}}\!\left[\sum_{t=0}^{\tau-1}s_{\gamma}(X_{t})\right],

where
s_{\gamma}
is the local
\gamma
–source in the long–jump–absorbing chain.
We use the standard band decomposition with
M:=\lfloor n^{1/4}\rfloor
.

On
\{\tau\leq M\}
, all visited states satisfy
D(X_{t})\leq D(v_{0})+t\leq 2M
for
n
large enough (since
D(v_{0})=K
is fixed), hence

\left|\sum_{t=0}^{\tau-1}s_{\gamma}(X_{t})\right|\mathbf{1}\{\tau\leq M\}\ \leq\ M\cdot\sup_{x:\,D(x)\leq 2M}|s_{\gamma}(x)|.

To bound the band supremum, use the explicit
\gamma
–source formulas in the long–jump–absorbing chain (Lemma
22
).
In each of the finitely many cases (deep middle / first / second / penultimate),
s_{\gamma}(x)
is a finite linear combination of terms of the form

\widetilde{P}(x,y)\cdot w_{x}(y),

where either
y=e
(a long jump in the original chain, redirected to
e
) so
\widetilde{P}(x,y)=p(e;x)\leq p(e)_{\max}
, or else
y
is one of the
O(1)

non–long–jump
\gamma
–dependent neighbors and then
\widetilde{P}(x,y)\leq 1

and the coefficient multiplies a value term
w_{x}(y)
which is a difference of absorption probabilities (or a centered version thereof).
On the band
D(\cdot)\leq 2M
, Lemma
19
together with the drift-gap bound
{\mathcal{E}}[\tau]\lesssim D/\zeta
gives

u(y)=\Pr_{y}[X_{\tau}=e]\ \lesssim\ \frac{M}{\zeta}\,p(e)_{\max},

and hence every such value term
w_{x}(y)
appearing in the case formulas is
O\!\big((M/\zeta)\,p(e)_{\max}\big)
.
Since each case has only
O(1)
such terms, we conclude that

\sup_{D(x)\leq 2M}|s_{\gamma}(x)|\ \leq\ C\,\frac{M}{\zeta}\,p(e)_{\max}

for a constant
C
.
Therefore the contribution on
\{\tau\leq M\}
is at most
C\,\frac{M^{2}}{\zeta}\,p(e)_{\max}
.

On
\{\tau>M\}
, use the crude bound
\left|\sum_{t<\tau}s_{\gamma}(X_{t})\right|\leq\tau\sup_{x}|s_{\gamma}(x)|
together with the tail bound
from Lemma
65
(with
t=M
). The same reasoning as above, with
D(\cdot)\leq n
instead of
2M
,
gives the global bound

\sup_{x}|s_{\gamma}(x)|\ \leq\ C\,\frac{n}{\zeta}\,p(e)_{\max}.

Multiplying by
\Pr_{v_{0}}(\tau>M)=\exp(-\Omega(M))
(with
M=\lfloor n^{1/4}\rfloor
) shows this tail contribution is negligible.
Thus,

|\partial_{\gamma}h_{\gamma}(v_{0})|\ \leq\ C\,\frac{M^{2}}{\zeta}\,p(e)_{\max}\ =\ C\,\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\zeta}\,p(e)_{\max}.

Combining with (
35
) yields the claimed upper bound.
∎

E.7
Hard example contributions to derivative are negligible

Recall the derivative decomposition from Lemma
15
. For a parameter

\theta\in\{\alpha,\beta,\gamma\}
and a start state
v
, we have

\partial_{\theta}S_{\theta}(v)=-(\partial_{\theta}h_{\theta}(v))\Big(\bar{S}^{NL}_{\theta}(v)-\tfrac{1}{2}\Big)+(1-h_{\theta}(v))\,\partial_{\theta}\bar{S}^{NL}_{\theta}(v),

where:

•

S_{\theta}(v):=\Pr^{\theta}_{v}[X_{\tau}=a_{n}]
is the same–side absorption probability in the original chain.

•

h_{\theta}(v)
is the probability of a long jump before absorption, which by definition equals the absorption
probability at
S_{LJ}
in the long–jump–absorbing chain where long jumps are redirected to the absorbing state
S_{LJ}
.

•

\bar{S}^{NL}_{\theta}(v):=\Pr^{\theta}_{v}(X_{\tau}=a_{n}\mid NL)
is the same–side absorption probability conditional on
no long jump occurring (i.e. in the no–long–jump chain).

•

By symmetry,
\bar{S}^{LJ}_{\theta}(v)=\tfrac{1}{2}
, so the long–jump conditional absorption probability equals
1/2
.

We will now argue using this decomposition that the addition of hard examples preserves the properties of the derivatives argued above. Specifically, we will show that the monotonicity and sign properties established for the derivatives
\partial_{\theta}S_{\theta}(v)
continue to hold when hard examples are included in the training distribution.

E.7.1
Upper bound on derivative for hard examples

We will now bound the derivatives of the same-side absorption probability for hard examples.

Lemma 42
.

Let
S
be finite and let
T\subseteq S
be absorbing. Fix
A\subseteq T
and a starting state
v\in S
.
For
\theta\in{\mathbb{R}}
, let
P_{\theta}
be a time-homogeneous Markov kernel on
S
with absorbing set
T
.
Let

\tau:=\inf\{t\geq 0:\ X_{t}\in T\},\qquad R:=\mathbf{1}\{X_{\tau}\in A\},\qquad u_{\theta}(v):={\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[R].

For
x\notin T
and
P_{\theta}(x,y)>0
, define the one-step score

\phi_{\theta}(x,y):=\partial_{\theta}\log P_{\theta}(x,y),

and the stopped cumulative score

L:=\sum_{t=0}^{\tau-1}\phi_{\theta}(X_{t},X_{t+1}).

Assume that all differentiations and exchanges of derivative and expectation below are valid, and that

{\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[L^{2}]<\infty
. Then
u_{\theta}(v)
is differentiable and

\partial_{\theta}u_{\theta}(v)={\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[R\,L]={\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}\!\big[(R-u_{\theta}(v))\,L\big]=\Cov_{v}^{\theta}(R,L).

Moreover, by Cauchy–Schwarz,

\bigl|\partial_{\theta}u_{\theta}(v)\bigr|\leq\sqrt{u_{\theta}(v)\bigl(1-u_{\theta}(v)\bigr)}\;\sqrt{{\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[L^{2}]}.

Proof.

By definition
u_{\theta}(v)={\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[R]
where
R=\mathbf{1}\{X_{\tau}\in A\}
depends on the trajectory
only through the absorbed terminal state. Using the log-derivative trick (score-function identity),

\partial_{\theta}u_{\theta}(v)=\partial_{\theta}{\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[R]={\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}\!\big[R\,\partial_{\theta}\log\Pr_{v}^{\theta}(X_{0},\dots,X_{\tau})\big].

Since the chain is time-homogeneous and
T
is absorbing, the (stopped) path likelihood factors as

\Pr_{v}^{\theta}(X_{0},\dots,X_{\tau})=\prod_{t=0}^{\tau-1}P_{\theta}(X_{t},X_{t+1}).

Therefore

\partial_{\theta}\log\Pr_{v}^{\theta}(X_{0},\dots,X_{\tau})=\sum_{t=0}^{\tau-1}\partial_{\theta}\log P_{\theta}(X_{t},X_{t+1})=\sum_{t=0}^{\tau-1}\phi_{\theta}(X_{t},X_{t+1})=L.

Plugging this back gives the REINFORCE form

\partial_{\theta}u_{\theta}(v)={\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[R\,L].

Next we show that
L
has mean zero (baseline fact). Condition on
X_{t}=x
(with
x\notin T
). Then

{\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[\phi_{\theta}(X_{t},X_{t+1})\mid X_{t}=x]=\sum_{y\in S}P_{\theta}(x,y)\,\partial_{\theta}\log P_{\theta}(x,y)=\sum_{y\in S}\partial_{\theta}P_{\theta}(x,y)=\partial_{\theta}\Big(\sum_{y\in S}P_{\theta}(x,y)\Big)=0.

Summing over
t=0,\dots,\tau-1
and using linearity of expectation yields

{\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[L]={\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}\!\left[\sum_{t=0}^{\tau-1}\phi_{\theta}(X_{t},X_{t+1})\right]=0.

Hence, for the constant baseline
b:=u_{\theta}(v)={\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[R]
,

{\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[(R-b)L]={\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[R\,L]-b\,{\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[L]={\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[R\,L].

Therefore

\partial_{\theta}u_{\theta}(v)={\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[R\,L]={\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[(R-u_{\theta}(v))\,L].

Since
{\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[L]=0
, this is exactly the covariance:

{\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[(R-u_{\theta}(v))\,L]={\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[R\,L]-{\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[R]{\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[L]=\Cov_{v}^{\theta}(R,L).

Finally, apply Cauchy–Schwarz:

\bigl|\partial_{\theta}u_{\theta}(v)\bigr|=\bigl|{\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[(R-u_{\theta}(v))\,L]\bigr|\leq\sqrt{{\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[(R-u_{\theta}(v))^{2}]}\;\sqrt{{\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[L^{2}]}.

Because
R
is Bernoulli with mean
u_{\theta}(v)
,

{\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[(R-u_{\theta}(v))^{2}]=\Var_{v}^{\theta}(R)=u_{\theta}(v)\bigl(1-u_{\theta}(v)\bigr).

Substituting yields

\bigl|\partial_{\theta}u_{\theta}(v)\bigr|\leq\sqrt{u_{\theta}(v)\bigl(1-u_{\theta}(v)\bigr)}\;\sqrt{{\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[L^{2}]}.

∎

We have the following decomposition for
E_{\theta}[L^{2}]
:

Lemma 43
.

Let
S
be finite and let
T\subseteq S
be absorbing. Fix
\theta\in{\mathbb{R}}
and a starting state
v\in S
.
Let
(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be the time-homogeneous Markov chain with kernel
P_{\theta}
and absorption time

\tau:=\inf\{t\geq 0:\ X_{t}\in T\}.

For
x\notin T
and
P_{\theta}(x,y)>0
define the one-step score

\phi_{\theta}(x,y):=\partial_{\theta}\log P_{\theta}(x,y),

and define the stopped cumulative score

L:=\sum_{t=0}^{\tau-1}\phi_{\theta}(X_{t},X_{t+1}).

Define the one-step Fisher-type quantity

I_{\theta}(x):=\sum_{y\in S}P_{\theta}(x,y)\,\phi_{\theta}(x,y)^{2}\qquad(x\in S\setminus T),

and set
I_{\theta}(x):=0
for
x\in T
.
Assume
{\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[L^{2}]<\infty
and
{\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[L]=0
. Then

\Var_{v}^{\theta}(L)={\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[L^{2}]={\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}\!\left[\sum_{t=0}^{\tau-1}I_{\theta}(X_{t})\right].

In particular, if
I_{\theta}^{\star}:=\max_{x\in S\setminus T}I_{\theta}(x)
, then

\Var_{v}^{\theta}(L)\leq I_{\theta}^{\star}\,{\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[\tau].

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{t}:=\sigma(X_{0},\dots,X_{t})
and define the stopped increments

D_{t}:=\phi_{\theta}(X_{t},X_{t+1})\,\mathbf{1}\{t<\tau\},\qquad t\geq 0,

so that
L=\sum_{t\geq 0}D_{t}
.

For each
t\geq 0
we have
{\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[D_{t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t}]=0
.
Indeed, on the event
\{t\geq\tau\}
we have
D_{t}=0
. On the event
\{t<\tau\}
we have
X_{t}\notin T
, and
conditioning on
X_{t}=x
gives

{\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[D_{t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t}]={\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[\phi_{\theta}(X_{t},X_{t+1})\mid X_{t}=x]=\sum_{y\in S}P_{\theta}(x,y)\,\partial_{\theta}\log P_{\theta}(x,y)=\sum_{y\in S}\partial_{\theta}P_{\theta}(x,y)=0.

Hence
(D_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is a martingale-difference sequence.

Expanding
L^{2}
gives

L^{2}=\left(\sum_{t\geq 0}D_{t}\right)^{2}=\sum_{t\geq 0}D_{t}^{2}+2\sum_{0\leq s<t}D_{s}D_{t}.

We claim
{\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[D_{s}D_{t}]=0
for all
0\leq s<t
.
Since
D_{s}
is
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-measurable when
s<t
, and using the tower property,

{\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[D_{s}D_{t}]={\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}\!\left[{\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[D_{s}D_{t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t}]\right]={\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}\!\left[D_{s}\,{\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[D_{t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t}]\right]=0,

by the martingale-difference property.
Therefore

{\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[L^{2}]=\sum_{t\geq 0}{\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[D_{t}^{2}].

For each
t
,

{\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[D_{t}^{2}]={\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}\!\left[{\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[D_{t}^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t}]\right]={\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}\!\left[\mathbf{1}\{t<\tau\}\,{\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}\!\big[\phi_{\theta}(X_{t},X_{t+1})^{2}\mid X_{t}\big]\right].

On
\{t<\tau\}
,
X_{t}\notin T
, and conditioning on
X_{t}=x
yields

{\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}\!\big[\phi_{\theta}(X_{t},X_{t+1})^{2}\mid X_{t}=x\big]=\sum_{y\in S}P_{\theta}(x,y)\,\phi_{\theta}(x,y)^{2}=I_{\theta}(x).

Hence
{\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[D_{t}^{2}]={\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[\mathbf{1}\{t<\tau\}I_{\theta}(X_{t})]
, and summing over
t
gives

{\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[L^{2}]=\sum_{t\geq 0}{\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[\mathbf{1}\{t<\tau\}I_{\theta}(X_{t})]={\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}\!\left[\sum_{t=0}^{\tau-1}I_{\theta}(X_{t})\right].

Since
{\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[L]=0
, we have
\Var_{v}^{\theta}(L)={\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[L^{2}]
, proving the main identity.

Finally,
I_{\theta}(X_{t})\leq I_{\theta}^{\star}
for
t<\tau
, so

\sum_{t=0}^{\tau-1}I_{\theta}(X_{t})\leq I_{\theta}^{\star}\,\tau,

and taking expectations yields
\Var_{v}^{\theta}(L)\leq I_{\theta}^{\star}\,{\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[\tau]
.
∎

We now bound these single step terms for the LJ absorbing chain:

Lemma 44
.

Work in the long-jump absorbing (LJ) chain with the softmax logit structure.
For each
\theta\in\{\alpha,\beta,\gamma\}
and each nonterminal state
x\notin T
, let

I_{\theta}(x)\;:=\;\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}(x)}P_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}(x,a)\Big(\partial_{\theta}\log P_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}(x,a)\Big)^{2}

denote the one-step Fisher-type term. Then

I_{\theta}(x)\leq 1\qquad\text{for all }\theta\in\{\alpha,\beta,\gamma\}\text{ and all }x\notin T.

Consequently,
I_{\theta}^{\star}:=\max_{x\notin T}I_{\theta}(x)\leq 1
for each
\theta\in\{\alpha,\beta,\gamma\}
.

Proof.

Fix
\theta\in\{\alpha,\beta,\gamma\}
and a nonterminal state
x\notin T
. Write the allowed moves out of
x

as
\mathcal{A}(x)
and write the outgoing probabilities in softmax form

p_{a}(\theta):=P_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}(x,a)=\frac{e^{\ell_{a}(\theta)}}{Z(\theta)},\qquad Z(\theta):=\sum_{b\in\mathcal{A}(x)}e^{\ell_{b}(\theta)}.

In the LJ logit structure, after the usual normalization for
\gamma
(subtracting a common
\gamma
from all logits,
which does not change the softmax probabilities), the dependence on
\theta
is affine with coefficient bounded by
1
:

\ell_{a}(\theta)=\ell_{a}^{(0)}+k_{a}\,\theta\qquad\text{with}\qquad|k_{a}|\leq 1.

Define
\bar{k}(\theta):=\sum_{b\in\mathcal{A}(x)}p_{b}(\theta)\,k_{b}
. Differentiating

\log p_{a}(\theta)=\ell_{a}(\theta)-\log Z(\theta)
gives

\partial_{\theta}\log p_{a}(\theta)=\partial_{\theta}\ell_{a}(\theta)-\frac{Z^{\prime}(\theta)}{Z(\theta)}=k_{a}-\bar{k}(\theta),

where we used

\frac{Z^{\prime}(\theta)}{Z(\theta)}=\frac{\sum_{b}e^{\ell_{b}(\theta)}\partial_{\theta}\ell_{b}(\theta)}{\sum_{b}e^{\ell_{b}(\theta)}}=\sum_{b}\frac{e^{\ell_{b}(\theta)}}{Z(\theta)}\,k_{b}=\bar{k}(\theta).

Therefore

I_{\theta}(x)=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}(x)}p_{a}(\theta)\bigl(\partial_{\theta}\log p_{a}(\theta)\bigr)^{2}=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}(x)}p_{a}(\theta)\,(k_{a}-\bar{k}(\theta))^{2}=\Var_{a\sim p(\theta)}(k_{a}).

Finally,
\Var(k)\leq{\mathcal{E}}[k^{2}]
and
|k_{a}|\leq 1
imply

\Var_{a\sim p(\theta)}(k_{a})\leq{\mathcal{E}}_{a\sim p(\theta)}[k_{a}^{2}]\leq 1,

hence
I_{\theta}(x)\leq 1
, as claimed. Taking the maximum over
x\notin T
yields
I_{\theta}^{\star}\leq 1
.
∎

Lemma 45
0Fisher bound for LJ chain derivative).

Work in the long–jump–absorbing (LJ) chain (Definition
16
) with absorbing set
T=\{a_{n},b_{n},e\}
.
Fix
\theta\in\{\alpha,\beta,\gamma\}
and define the long-jump absorption probability

u_{\theta}(v):=\Pr_{v}^{\theta}(X_{\tau}=e),\qquad\tau:=\inf\{t\geq 0:\ X_{t}\in T\}.

Assume that for all starting states
v\notin T
,

{\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[\tau]\ \leq\ \frac{n}{\zeta},

for some constant
\zeta>0
independent of
n
.
Then, for every
v\notin T
,

\big|\partial_{\theta}u_{\theta}(v)\big|\ \leq\ \sqrt{u_{\theta}(v)\bigl(1-u_{\theta}(v)\bigr)}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{n}{\zeta}}

Proof.

Apply Lemma
42
with
A=\{e\}
. This gives

|\partial_{\theta}u_{\theta}(v)|\leq\sqrt{u_{\theta}(v)\bigl(1-u_{\theta}(v)\bigr)}\;\sqrt{{\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[L^{2}]}.

By Lemma
43
,

{\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[L^{2}]={\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}\!\left[\sum_{t=0}^{\tau-1}I_{\theta}(X_{t})\right]\leq I_{\theta}^{\star}\,{\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[\tau].

Finally, Lemma
44
gives
I_{\theta}^{\star}\leq 1
, and by assumption

{\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{\theta}[\tau]\leq n/\zeta
. Combining yields

|\partial_{\theta}u_{\theta}(v)|\leq\sqrt{u_{\theta}(v)\bigl(1-u_{\theta}(v)\bigr)}\;\sqrt{\frac{n}{\zeta}}.

∎

Lemma 46
0Exponential decay of derivatives for hard examples).

Fix a constant
a\in(0,1)
and consider the original two–chain model of length
n
, together with its
long–jump–absorbing chain (Definition
16
).
Assume there exists a constant
q_{\min}\in(0,1)
, independent of
n
, such that the one-step long-jump probability satisfies

p(e;x):=P^{\mathrm{LJ\text{-}abs}}(x,e)\ \geq\ q_{\min}\qquad\text{for all nonterminal }x\notin\{a_{n},b_{n}\}.

Let
v
be any start state with depth
D(v)\geq an
. Then for each parameter
\theta\in\{\alpha,\beta,\gamma\}
there exist constants

C,c>0
(depending only on fixed parameters,
a
, and
q_{\min}
, but not on
n
) such that

\big|\partial_{\alpha}S_{\alpha}(v)\big|\ \leq\ C\,\frac{n}{\zeta}\,p_{f}^{\max}\,e^{-cn},\qquad\big|\partial_{\beta}S_{\beta}(v)\big|\ \leq\ C\,\frac{n}{\zeta}\,p_{b}^{\max}\,e^{-cn},\qquad\big|\partial_{\gamma}S_{\gamma}(v)\big|\ \leq\ C\,\frac{n}{\zeta}\,p(e)_{\max}\,e^{-cn},

where
\zeta>0
is the drift-gap constant from Assumption
1
,

\displaystyle p_{f}^{\max}

\displaystyle:=\sup_{x\notin\{a_{n},b_{n}\}}\Pr(X_{1}=F(x)\mid X_{0}=x),

\displaystyle p_{b}^{\max}

\displaystyle:=\sup_{x\notin\{a_{n},b_{n}\}}\Pr(X_{1}=B(x)\mid X_{0}=x),

\displaystyle p(e)_{\max}

\displaystyle:=\sup_{x\notin\{a_{n},b_{n}\}}p(e;x),

and
F(x),B(x)
denote the same–chain forward/backward neighbors when they exist (with the convention that the corresponding probability is
0
if the neighbor does not exist).

Proof.

Let
\tau:=\inf\{t\geq 0:\ X_{t}\in\{a_{n},b_{n}\}\}
be the absorption time in the original chain and let
NL:=\{J>\tau\}
be the event
of no long jump before absorption (with
J
as in Lemma
15
).
In the long–jump–absorbing chain (Definition
16
) with absorption time

\tau_{LJ}:=\inf\{t\geq 0:\ X_{t}\in\{a_{n},b_{n},e\}\},

one has
NL=\{X_{\tau_{LJ}}\in\{a_{n},b_{n}\}\}
and
LJ=\{X_{\tau_{LJ}}=e\}
.

We use the long–jump / no–long–jump
derivative
decomposition (Lemma
15
): for each

\theta\in\{\alpha,\beta,\gamma\}
,

\partial_{\theta}S_{\theta}(v)=-(\partial_{\theta}h_{\theta}(v))\Big(\bar{S}^{NL}_{\theta}(v)-\tfrac{1}{2}\Big)+(1-h_{\theta}(v))\,\partial_{\theta}\bar{S}^{NL}_{\theta}(v),

where
h_{\theta}(v)=\Pr_{v}^{\theta}(LJ)
and
1-h_{\theta}(v)=\Pr_{v}^{\theta}(NL)
.

Since
D(v)\geq an
, on
NL
the depth can decrease by at most
1
per step, so
\tau\geq an
. Under the assumption

p(e;x)\geq q_{\min}
for every nonterminal
x
, this gives

\Pr_{v}^{\theta}(NL)\leq\Pr_{v}^{\theta}(\text{no hit of }e\text{ in the first }an\text{ steps})\leq(1-q_{\min})^{an}\leq e^{-cn}

for some
c>0
depending only on
a,q_{\min}
.

Using
|\bar{S}^{NL}_{\theta}(v)-\tfrac{1}{2}|\leq\tfrac{1}{2}
, we have

\big|\partial_{\theta}S_{\theta}(v)\big|\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\,\big|\partial_{\theta}h_{\theta}(v)\big|+\Pr_{v}^{\theta}(NL)\cdot\big|\partial_{\theta}\bar{S}^{NL}_{\theta}(v)\big|.

Now
\partial_{\theta}h_{\theta}(v)=\partial_{\theta}\Pr_{v}^{\theta}(LJ)
is the derivative of the long-jump absorption probability in the
long–jump–absorbing chain (with absorbing set
T=\{a_{n},b_{n},e\}
and target
A=\{e\}
).
Therefore, applying Lemma
45
yields

|\partial_{\theta}h_{\theta}(v)|\leq c\,\sqrt{h_{\theta}(v)\bigl(1-h_{\theta}(v)\bigr)}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{n}{\zeta}}=c\,\sqrt{\Pr_{v}^{\theta}(LJ)\Pr_{v}^{\theta}(NL)}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{n}{\zeta}}\leq C\,\sqrt{\frac{n}{\zeta}}\,e^{-c^{\prime}n},

where we used
\Pr_{v}^{\theta}(NL)\leq e^{-cn}
and absorbed constants by possibly decreasing
c
.

It remains to control
|\partial_{\theta}\bar{S}^{NL}_{\theta}(v)|
by the relevant one-step probability scale.
By the source identities in the NL chain (Lemmas
32
,
33
, and the corresponding

\gamma
-source bound Lemma
31
), the NL sources satisfy

|s_{\alpha,NL}(x)|\leq C\,p_{f}(x)
,
|s_{\beta,NL}(x)|\leq C\,p_{b}(x)
, and
|s_{\gamma,NL}(x)|\leq C\,p_{\mathrm{sw}}(x)\leq C\,p(e)_{\max}
.
Therefore, by the trajectory/source representation (Lemma
16
) and the drift-gap absorption time bound

{\mathcal{E}}_{v}^{NL}[\tau]\leq n/\zeta
, we have

|\partial_{\alpha}\bar{S}^{NL}_{\alpha}(v)|\leq C\,\frac{n}{\zeta}\,p_{f}^{\max},\qquad|\partial_{\beta}\bar{S}^{NL}_{\beta}(v)|\leq C\,\frac{n}{\zeta}\,p_{b}^{\max},\qquad|\partial_{\gamma}\bar{S}^{NL}_{\gamma}(v)|\leq C\,\frac{n}{\zeta}\,p(e)_{\max}.

Multiplying by
\Pr_{v}^{\theta}(NL)\leq e^{-cn}
and combining with the bound on
|\partial_{\theta}h_{\theta}(v)|
yields the stated result.
∎

E.8
Easy+Hard examples derivative estimates

We now apply the results of the previous subsections to derive our three main derivative bounds that will be used under the training-distribution assumptions of
Theorem
2
.

Lemma 47
0Easy+hard expected derivative bound for
\gamma
(Theorem
2
)).

Assume the training distribution
{\mathcal{Q}}
satisfies the assumptions of Theorem
2
,
and that there exists a constant
q_{\min}\in(0,1)
, independent of
n
, such that
P^{\mathrm{LJ\text{-}abs}}(x,e)\geq q_{\min}
for all nonterminal
x
.
Let
v_{i}:=a_{i}
(equivalently, by symmetry one may take
v_{i}:=b_{i}
). Define

p(e)_{\max}:=\max_{x\ \mathrm{nonterminal}}P^{\mathrm{LJ\text{-}abs}}(x,e).

Then for all
n
large enough,

{\mathcal{E}}_{i\sim{\mathcal{Q}}}\big[\partial_{\gamma}S_{\gamma}(v_{i})\big]\leq 0,

and there exist constants
C_{1,\gamma},C_{2,\gamma}>0
(independent of
n
and
c
) such that

c\cdot C_{2,\gamma}\,p(e)_{\max}\ \leq\ \Big|{\mathcal{E}}_{i\sim{\mathcal{Q}}}\big[\partial_{\gamma}S_{\gamma}(v_{i})\big]\Big|\ \leq\ c\cdot C_{1,\gamma}\,\sqrt{n}\,p(e)_{\max}.

Proof.

Let
v_{i}:=a_{i}
. Define the index sets

\mathcal{E}:=\{n-k,\ldots,n-1\}\quad\text{(easy)},\qquad\mathcal{H}:=\{1,\ldots,\lfloor(1-a)n\rfloor\}\quad\text{(hard)}.

Since
{\mathcal{Q}}
has s-simplicity mass
c
we have
{\mathcal{Q}}(\mathcal{E})=c
, and since
{\mathcal{Q}}
also has
\lceil an\rceil
-simplicity mass
c
, we can decompose the expected derivative as

{\mathcal{E}}_{i\sim{\mathcal{Q}}}[\partial_{\gamma}S_{\gamma}(v_{i})]={\mathcal{Q}}(\mathcal{E})\cdot{\mathcal{E}}_{i\sim{\mathcal{Q}}}[\partial_{\gamma}S_{\gamma}(v_{i})\mid i\in\mathcal{E}]+{\mathcal{Q}}(\mathcal{H})\cdot{\mathcal{E}}_{i\sim{\mathcal{Q}}}[\partial_{\gamma}S_{\gamma}(v_{i})\mid i\in\mathcal{H}].

(36)

By Lemma
40
,
\partial_{\gamma}S_{\gamma}(v_{i})\leq 0
for all
n
large enough, and moreover

C_{2,\gamma}\,p(e)_{\max}\ \leq\ |\partial_{\gamma}S_{\gamma}(v_{i})|\ \leq\ C_{1,\gamma}\,\sqrt{n}\,p(e)_{\max},

where the constants
C_{1,\gamma},C_{2,\gamma}>0
depend only on
k
(not on
n
or
c
).
Therefore, the easy-example conditional expectation satisfies

C_{2,\gamma}\,p(e)_{\max}\ \leq\ \Big|{\mathcal{E}}_{i\sim{\mathcal{Q}}}[\partial_{\gamma}S_{\gamma}(v_{i})\mid i\in\mathcal{E}]\Big|\ \leq\ C_{1,\gamma}\,\sqrt{n}\,p(e)_{\max}.

(37)

For each
i\in\mathcal{H}
, the depth satisfies
D(v_{i})=n-i\geq an
.
By Lemma
46
, for all
n
large enough,

\big|{\mathcal{Q}}(\mathcal{H})\cdot{\mathcal{E}}_{i\sim{\mathcal{Q}}}[\partial_{\gamma}S_{\gamma}(v_{i})\mid i\in\mathcal{H}]\big|\leq C\,\frac{n}{\zeta}\,p(e)_{\max}\,e^{-cn}.

(38)

Combining (
37
) and (
38
) via the triangle inequality, and redefining the constants
C_{1,\gamma},C_{2,\gamma}
, we obtain the stated bounds.
∎

Lemma 48
0Easy+hard expected derivative lower bound for
\beta
(Theorem
2
)).

Assume the training distribution
{\mathcal{Q}}
satisfies the assumptions of Theorem
2
,
and that there exists a constant
q_{\min}\in(0,1)
, independent of
n
, such that
P^{\mathrm{LJ\text{-}abs}}(x,e)\geq q_{\min}
for all nonterminal
x
.
Let
v_{i}:=a_{i}
(equivalently, by symmetry one may take
v_{i}:=b_{i}
). Define

p(e)_{\max}:=\max_{x\ \mathrm{nonterminal}}P^{\mathrm{LJ\text{-}abs}}(x,e),\qquad p(b)_{\max}:=\max_{x\ \mathrm{nonterminal}}p_{b}(x),

where
p_{b}(x)
denotes the one–step same–chain backward probability from
x
in the original chain.
Then for all
n
large enough,

{\mathcal{E}}_{i\sim{\mathcal{Q}}}\big[\partial_{\beta}S_{\beta}(v_{i})\big]\leq 0,

and there exists a constant
C_{2,\beta}>0
(independent of
n
) such that

\Big|{\mathcal{E}}_{i\sim{\mathcal{Q}}}\big[\partial_{\beta}S_{\beta}(v_{i})\big]\Big|\ \geq\ c\cdot C_{2,\beta}\,p(e)_{\max}\,p(b)_{\max}.

Proof.

As in Lemma
47
, we decompose

{\mathcal{E}}_{i\sim{\mathcal{Q}}}[\partial_{\beta}S_{\beta}(v_{i})]={\mathcal{Q}}(\mathcal{E})\cdot{\mathcal{E}}[\partial_{\beta}S_{\beta}(v_{i})\mid i\in\mathcal{E}]+{\mathcal{Q}}(\mathcal{H})\cdot{\mathcal{E}}[\partial_{\beta}S_{\beta}(v_{i})\mid i\in\mathcal{H}].

For easy examples, Lemmas
39
and
25
give
\partial_{\beta}S_{\beta}(v_{i})\leq 0
and
|\partial_{\beta}S_{\beta}(v_{i})|\geq C_{2,\beta}\,p(e)_{\max}\,p(b)_{\max}
.
For hard examples, Lemma
46
gives
|\partial_{\beta}S_{\beta}(v_{i})|\leq C\,\frac{n}{\zeta}\,p_{b}^{\max}\,e^{-cn}
.
Since this is exponentially small, combining via the triangle inequality and redefining
C_{2,\beta}
yields the stated bounds.
∎

Lemma 49
0Easy+hard expected derivative lower bound for
\alpha
(Theorem
2
)).

Assume the training distribution
{\mathcal{Q}}
satisfies the assumptions of Theorem
2
,
and that there exists a constant
q_{\min}\in(0,1)
, independent of
n
, such that
P^{\mathrm{LJ\text{-}abs}}(x,e)\geq q_{\min}
for all nonterminal
x
.
Let
v_{i}:=a_{i}
(equivalently, by symmetry one may take
v_{i}:=b_{i}
). Define

p(e)_{\max}:=\max_{x\ \mathrm{nonterminal}}P^{\mathrm{LJ\text{-}abs}}(x,e),\qquad p(f)_{\min}:=\min_{x\ \mathrm{nonterminal}}p_{f}(x),

where
p_{f}(x)
denotes the one–step same–chain forward probability from
x
in the original chain.
Then for all
n
large enough,

{\mathcal{E}}_{i\sim{\mathcal{Q}}}\big[\partial_{\alpha}S_{\alpha}(v_{i})\big]\geq 0,

and there exists a constant
C_{2,\alpha}>0
(independent of
n
) such that

\Big|{\mathcal{E}}_{i\sim{\mathcal{Q}}}\big[\partial_{\alpha}S_{\alpha}(v_{i})\big]\Big|\ \geq c\cdot\ C_{2,\alpha}\,p(e)_{\max}\,p(f)_{\min}.

Proof.

As in Lemma
47
, we decompose

{\mathcal{E}}_{i\sim{\mathcal{Q}}}[\partial_{\alpha}S_{\alpha}(v_{i})]={\mathcal{Q}}(\mathcal{E})\cdot{\mathcal{E}}[\partial_{\alpha}S_{\alpha}(v_{i})\mid i\in\mathcal{E}]+{\mathcal{Q}}(\mathcal{H})\cdot{\mathcal{E}}[\partial_{\alpha}S_{\alpha}(v_{i})\mid i\in\mathcal{H}].

For easy examples, Lemmas
38
and
24
give
\partial_{\alpha}S_{\alpha}(v_{i})\geq 0
and
|\partial_{\alpha}S_{\alpha}(v_{i})|\geq C_{2,\alpha}\,p(e)_{\max}\,p(f)_{\min}
.
For hard examples, Lemma
46
gives
|\partial_{\alpha}S_{\alpha}(v_{i})|\leq C\,\frac{n}{\zeta}\,p_{f}^{\max}\,e^{-cn}
.
Since this is exponentially small, combining via the triangle inequality and redefining
C_{2,\alpha}
yields the stated bounds.
∎

E.9
Convergence rate

Main proof
We now present the proof of
Theorem

2
, bringing together the components developed in the previous subsections.

Proof of
Theorem

2
.

By
Lemma

61
, the dynamics of the attention parameters are governed by the gradients of the reduced loss
S:=\tilde{L}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
.
Since the loss
S
is an expectation over the starting vertex location
v
(sampled from
Q
), we write
S={\mathcal{E}}_{v\sim Q}[S_{v}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)]
, where
S_{v}
is the loss term for a specific
v
.
Substituting this into the dynamics from
Lemma

61
and taking absolute values, we have with
|V|=2n
:

\displaystyle|\dot{\alpha}(t)|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2n(2n-1)}\,\left|{\mathcal{E}}_{v}[\partial_{\alpha}S_{v}]\right|,

\displaystyle|\dot{\beta}(t)|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2n(2n-1)}\,\left|{\mathcal{E}}_{v}[\partial_{\beta}S_{v}]\right|,

\displaystyle|\dot{\gamma}(t)|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2n-2)\cdot 2n(2n-1)}\,\left|{\mathcal{E}}_{v}[\partial_{\gamma}S_{v}]\right|.

We apply
Lemmas

49
,
48
, and
47
to bound the derivatives.
These lemmas require the assumption that
p(e)_{\max}(t)\geq q_{\min}
for some constant
q_{\min}>0
independent of
n
. Under this assumption, they imply
{\mathcal{E}}_{v\sim Q}[\partial_{\alpha}S_{v}]\geq 0
,
{\mathcal{E}}_{v\sim Q}[\partial_{\beta}S_{v}]\leq 0
, and
{\mathcal{E}}_{v\sim Q}[\partial_{\gamma}S_{v}]\leq 0
.
Since
Lemma

61
gives
\dot{\theta}=-c_{\theta}\partial_{\theta}S
for positive constants
c_{\theta}
, we have
\dot{\alpha}(t)\geq 0
,
\dot{\beta}(t)\leq 0
, and
\dot{\gamma}(t)\leq 0
.

We will from now assume that
p(e)_{\max}(t)\geq q_{\min}
while analyzing the dynamics of the parameters
\alpha,\beta,\gamma
until some time
t_{0}
(to be determined later). Later, we will show that for all
t\leq t_{0}
indeed
p(e)_{\max}(t)\geq q_{\min}
.

We define the extremal probabilities at time
t
:

p(e)_{\max}(t):=\max{x\ \mathrm{nonterminal}}P^{\mathrm{LJ\text{-}abs}}(x,e;t),\qquad p(f)_{\min}(t):=\min_{x\ \mathrm{nonterminal}}p_{f}(x;t),

where the dependence on
t
comes through the parameters
\alpha(t),\beta(t),\gamma(t)
.

From
Lemmas

49
and
47
, the expected derivatives satisfy:

•

\big|{\mathcal{E}}_{v\sim Q}[\partial_{\alpha}S_{v}]\big|\geq c\cdot C_{2,\alpha}\,p(e)_{\max}(t)\,p(f)_{\min}(t)
,

•

c\cdot C_{2,\gamma}\,p(e)_{\max}(t)\leq\big|{\mathcal{E}}_{v\sim Q}[\partial_{\gamma}S_{v}]\big|\leq c\cdot C_{1,\gamma}\,\sqrt{n}\,p(e)_{\max}(t)
.

Next, we derive a lower bound on
|\dot{\alpha}(t)|
. From the dynamics and the lower bound on the expected
\alpha
-derivative:

|\dot{\alpha}(t)|\geq\frac{c\cdot C_{2,\alpha}}{2n(2n-1)}\cdot p(e)_{\max}(t)\,p(f)_{\min}(t).

By the softmax structure of the attention logits (
Lemma

68
), there exist constants
D>0
and
C>0
(independent of
n
) such that

p(f)_{\min}(0)=\frac{e^{\alpha(0)-D}}{e^{\alpha(0)-D}+C}.

Moreover, since
\dot{\beta},\dot{\gamma}\leq 0
, we have that

p(f)_{\min}(t)\geq\frac{e^{\alpha(t)-D}}{e^{\alpha(t)-D}+C}=:g(t).

By assumption (
Section

6.1
), at initialization we have
p(f)_{\min}(0)\geq\zeta
.

Define

q_{\min}:=\frac{1}{8}\cdot\frac{\varepsilon\,\zeta}{(1-\varepsilon)(1-\zeta)}\cdot\upsilon,

where
\upsilon>0
is the lower bound on the initial switch probability from
Section

6.1
.

Under the assumption that
p(e)_{\max}(t)\geq q_{\min}
, we have

|\dot{\alpha}(t)|\geq\frac{c\cdot C_{2,\alpha}\,q_{\min}}{2n(2n-1)}\cdot p(f)_{\min}(t).

Since
p(f)_{\min}(t)\geq g(t)
, we may apply
Lemma

67
to conclude that there exists a time

\displaystyle t_{0}

\displaystyle<\frac{16\,n(2n-1)}{c\cdot C_{2,\alpha}\,\upsilon}\cdot\frac{(1-\varepsilon)(1-\zeta)}{\varepsilon\,\zeta}\cdot\log\left(\frac{1-\varepsilon}{\varepsilon}\cdot\frac{1-\zeta}{\zeta}\right)

(39)

\displaystyle<O(\frac{n^{2}}{c\cdot\varepsilon^{2}})

(40)

such that
p(f)_{\min}(t_{0})\geq 1-\varepsilon
and

\alpha(t_{0})-\alpha(0)<\log\left(\frac{1-\varepsilon}{\varepsilon}\cdot\frac{1-\zeta}{\zeta}\right).

(41)

All that’s left to show is that indeed for any
t\leq t_{0}
,
p(e)_{\max}(t)\geq q_{\min}
for some constant
q_{\min}>0
independent of
n
. Assume the contrary, then there exists a minimal time
0\leq t_{1}<t_{0}
such that
p(e)_{\max}(t_{1})<q_{\min}
. First, we now show that for any constant
1>a_{1}>0
independent of
n
, to be determined later, for sufficiently large
n
, for any
t\in[0,t_{1}]
,
|\dot{\gamma}(t)|<a_{1}|\dot{\alpha}(t)|
.

Using the bounds from the lemmas:

|\dot{\alpha}(t)|\geq\frac{c\cdot C_{2,\alpha}}{2n(2n-1)}\cdot p(e)_{\max}(t)\,p(f)_{\min}(t),

and

|\dot{\gamma}(t)|\leq\frac{c\cdot C_{1,\gamma}\,\sqrt{n}}{(2n-2)\cdot 2n(2n-1)}\cdot p(e)_{\max}(t).

Taking the ratio and using that the
p(e)_{\max}(t)
terms cancel:

\frac{|\dot{\gamma}(t)|}{|\dot{\alpha}(t)|}\leq\frac{C_{1,\gamma}}{C_{2,\alpha}(2n-2)}\cdot\frac{\sqrt{n}}{p(f)_{\min}(t)}.

By assumption (
Section

6.1
), the model is initialized such that
p(f)_{\min}(0)\geq\zeta
, together with the logit structure (
Lemma

68
) and the fact that
\dot{\alpha}\geq 0
and
\dot{\beta},\dot{\gamma}\leq 0
implies that
p(f)_{\min}(t)\geq\zeta
for all
t
if
n
is sufficiently large.

Consequently, for sufficiently large
n
, we have
|\dot{\gamma}(t)|<a_{1}|\dot{\alpha}(t)|
.

This inequality allows us to apply
Lemma

66
with
x(t)=\alpha(t),y(t)=\gamma(t)
and
c=1/a_{1}
. Note that
\dot{\alpha}\geq 0
,
\dot{\gamma}\leq 0
, and
\dot{\alpha}\geq-\dot{\gamma}/a_{1}
.
This yields

\alpha(t)-\alpha(0)\geq\frac{1}{a_{1}}(\gamma(0)-\gamma(t)).

Rearranging and exponentiating gives

e^{\gamma(t)}\geq e^{\gamma(0)+a_{1}(\alpha(0)-\alpha(t))}.

(42)

By
Equation

41
, for all
t\leq t_{1}
we have that

\alpha(t)-\alpha(0)<\log\left(\frac{1-\varepsilon}{\varepsilon}\cdot\frac{1-\zeta}{\zeta}\right),

which is a constant independent of
n
. Therefore, we can choose
a_{1}>0
sufficiently small (independent of
n
) such that for all
t\leq t_{0}
,

e^{\gamma(t)}\geq e^{\gamma(0)+a_{1}(\alpha(0)-\alpha(t))}\geq e^{\gamma(0)-a_{1}\log\left(\frac{1-\varepsilon}{\varepsilon}\cdot\frac{1-\zeta}{\zeta}\right)}\geq\frac{1}{2}e^{\gamma(0)}.

By the logit structure (
Lemma

68
), and the fact that
\alpha(t)>\beta(t),\gamma(t),0
at init and throughout the run (by signs of the derivatives), we have that for any
t\leq t_{1}
:

\displaystyle p(e)_{\max}(t)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{ne^{\gamma(t)}}{2ne^{\gamma(t)}+e^{\beta(t)}+e^{\alpha(t)}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{4}\cdot\frac{ne^{\gamma(0)}}{2ne^{\gamma(0)}+e^{\beta(0)}+e^{\alpha(0)+(\alpha(t)-\alpha(0))}}

By
Equation

41
, we have
\alpha(t)-\alpha(0)<\log\left(\frac{1-\varepsilon}{\varepsilon}\cdot\frac{1-\zeta}{\zeta}\right)
:

\displaystyle p(e)_{\max}(t)\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\cdot\frac{\varepsilon\,\zeta}{(1-\varepsilon)(1-\zeta)}\cdot\frac{ne^{\gamma(0)}}{2ne^{\gamma(0)}+e^{\beta(0)}+e^{\alpha(0)}}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{8}\cdot\frac{\varepsilon\,\zeta}{(1-\varepsilon)(1-\zeta)}\cdot p(e)_{\max}(0)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{8}\cdot\frac{\varepsilon\,\zeta}{(1-\varepsilon)(1-\zeta)}\cdot\upsilon\;=\;q_{\min}.

Thus
p(e)_{\max}(t)\geq q_{\min}
for all
t\leq t_{1}
, which contradicts the assumption that
p(e)_{\max}(t_{1})<q_{\min}
, as required.
∎

Appendix F
Proof of
Theorem

3

As in
Section

E.9
, we define the loss as
\mathcal{L}:=1-S
, where
S
is the same-side absorption probability (the reward). Gradient flow on
\mathcal{L}
is equivalent to gradient ascent on
S
.

The proof proceeds in four stages. First, we establish general total-variation bounds showing that small parameter changes induce small changes in trajectory distributions (
Lemma

50
–
Corollary

3
). Second, we bound the absorption time of the Markov chain (
Lemma

56
–
Lemma

59
). Third, we show that under the hard-example assumption, the loss gradient is exponentially small in the chain length
n
(
Lemma

60
) at initialization. Finally, we combine these ingredients, showing that for exponentially long times, the derivative bounds remain valid and hence the induced distribution over rollouts remains statistically indistinguishable from the rollout distribution at initialization.

F.1
Total-variation bounds for trajectory distributions

The first lemma records a data processing (pushforward contraction) property of total variation on finite spaces.

Lemma 50
0Data processing for total variation on finite spaces).

Let
\Omega,\mathcal{S}
be finite sets. Let
P,Q
be probability measures on
\Omega
, and let

f:\Omega\to\mathcal{S}
be any map. Denote the pushforward (image) measures by

(P\circ f^{-1})(B):=P\big(f^{-1}(B)\big),\qquad(Q\circ f^{-1})(B):=Q\big(f^{-1}(B)\big),\qquad B\subseteq\mathcal{S}.

Then

\|P\circ f^{-1}-Q\circ f^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\ \leq\ \|P-Q\|_{\mathrm{TV}}.

Proof.

By definition of total variation on a finite space,

\|P\circ f^{-1}-Q\circ f^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}=\sup_{B\subseteq\mathcal{S}}\big|(P\circ f^{-1})(B)-(Q\circ f^{-1})(B)\big|=\sup_{B\subseteq\mathcal{S}}\big|P(f^{-1}(B))-Q(f^{-1}(B))\big|.

Since
f^{-1}(B)\subseteq\Omega
for every
B\subseteq\mathcal{S}
, we have

\sup_{B\subseteq\mathcal{S}}\big|P(f^{-1}(B))-Q(f^{-1}(B))\big|\ \leq\ \sup_{A\subseteq\Omega}\big|P(A)-Q(A)\big|=\|P-Q\|_{\mathrm{TV}},

which proves the claim.
∎

The next lemma bounds the total variation between joint distributions sharing a common marginal.

Lemma 51
.

Let
{\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{Y}}
be finite sets. Let
\Pi,\Pi^{\prime}
be probability measures on
{\mathcal{X}}\times{\mathcal{Y}}
with the same first
marginal
\mu
on
{\mathcal{X}}
, i.e.

\Pi(x,{\mathcal{Y}})=\Pi^{\prime}(x,{\mathcal{Y}})=\mu(x)\qquad\forall\,x\in{\mathcal{X}}.

For each
x
with
\mu(x)>0
, let

K_{x}(\cdot):=\Pi(\cdot\mid X=x),\qquad L_{x}(\cdot):=\Pi^{\prime}(\cdot\mid X=x)

denote the conditional distributions on
{\mathcal{Y}}
. (For
x
with
\mu(x)=0
, define
K_{x}=L_{x}
arbitrarily.)
Then

\|\Pi-\Pi^{\prime}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\ \leq\ \sum_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}\mu(x)\,\|K_{x}-L_{x}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}.

Proof.

For any event
E\subseteq{\mathcal{X}}\times{\mathcal{Y}}
, write the
x
-section

E_{x}:=\{y\in{\mathcal{Y}}:\ (x,y)\in E\}\subseteq{\mathcal{Y}}.

We have that

\Pi(E)=\sum_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}\Pi(\{x\}\times E_{x})=\sum_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}\Pi(X=x)\,\Pi(E_{x}\mid X=x)=\sum_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}\mu(x)\,K_{x}(E_{x}),

and similarly

\Pi^{\prime}(E)=\sum_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}\mu(x)\,L_{x}(E_{x}),

since the first marginal is the same
\mu
for both measures. Therefore

\Pi(E)-\Pi^{\prime}(E)=\sum_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}\mu(x)\,\big(K_{x}(E_{x})-L_{x}(E_{x})\big).

Taking absolute values and using
|\sum a_{x}|\leq\sum|a_{x}|
gives

|\Pi(E)-\Pi^{\prime}(E)|\leq\sum_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}\mu(x)\,\big|K_{x}(E_{x})-L_{x}(E_{x})\big|.

For each fixed
x
, the set
E_{x}\subseteq{\mathcal{Y}}
is an admissible event in the definition of total
variation on
{\mathcal{Y}}
, hence

\big|K_{x}(E_{x})-L_{x}(E_{x})\big|\leq\sup_{B\subseteq{\mathcal{Y}}}\big|K_{x}(B)-L_{x}(B)\big|=\|K_{x}-L_{x}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}.

Substituting yields

|\Pi(E)-\Pi^{\prime}(E)|\leq\sum_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}\mu(x)\,\|K_{x}-L_{x}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}.

Finally, take a supremum over all events
E\subseteq{\mathcal{X}}\times{\mathcal{Y}}
to obtain
