\|\Pi-\Pi^{\prime}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}=\sup_{E\subseteq{\mathcal{X}}\times{\mathcal{Y}}}|\Pi(E)-\Pi^{\prime}(E)|\leq\sum_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}\mu(x)\,\|K_{x}-L_{x}\|_{\mathrm{TV}},

as claimed.
∎

We now extend the above lemma to the case of trajectories sampled from Markov chains.

Lemma 52
0TV bound for trajectories).

Let
{\mathcal{X}}
be a finite state space and let
P,Q
be Markov kernels on
{\mathcal{X}}
.
Fix an initial distribution
\mu
on
{\mathcal{X}}
. For
T\geq 1
, let

\mathbb{P}^{(T)}_{\mu,P}
and
\mathbb{P}^{(T)}_{\mu,Q}
denote the laws on
{\mathcal{X}}^{T+1}
of the
length-
(T+1)
trajectories
(X_{0},\dots,X_{T})
with
X_{0}\sim\mu
and one-step transitions
given by
P
and
Q
, respectively. Then

\big\|\mathbb{P}^{(T)}_{\mu,P}-\mathbb{P}^{(T)}_{\mu,Q}\big\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\ \leq\ \sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\sup_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}\big\|P(x,\cdot)-Q(x,\cdot)\big\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\ =\ T\cdot\sup_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}\big\|P(x,\cdot)-Q(x,\cdot)\big\|_{\mathrm{TV}}.

Proof.

For
t=0,1,\dots,T
, let
R^{(t)}
be the law on
{\mathcal{X}}^{T+1}
of the trajectory

(X_{0},\dots,X_{T})
obtained by starting from
X_{0}\sim\mu
, using
P
for the first
t

transitions, and using
Q
for the remaining transitions. Thus

R^{(0)}=\mathbb{P}^{(T)}_{\mu,Q}
and
R^{(T)}=\mathbb{P}^{(T)}_{\mu,P}
. By the triangle inequality,

\big\|\mathbb{P}^{(T)}_{\mu,P}-\mathbb{P}^{(T)}_{\mu,Q}\big\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\leq\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\|R^{(t+1)}-R^{(t)}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}.

Fix
t\in\{0,\dots,T-1\}
. Define
Y:=(X_{t+1},\dots,X_{T})\in{\mathcal{X}}^{T-t}
.
Under both
R^{(t)}
and
R^{(t+1)}
, the first
t
transitions are governed by
P
, hence the
marginal law of
X_{t}
is the same. Therefore conditioning on
X_{t}
and averaging yields

\|R^{(t+1)}-R^{(t)}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\leq\sum_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}\Pr_{R^{(t)}}(X_{t}=x)\,\Big\|R^{(t+1)}(\cdot\mid X_{t}=x)-R^{(t)}(\cdot\mid X_{t}=x)\Big\|_{\mathrm{TV}}.

Define a Markov kernel
M
from
{\mathcal{X}}
to
{\mathcal{X}}^{T-t}
as follows: for each
y\in{\mathcal{X}}
,

M(y,\cdot)
is the trajectory distribution on
{\mathcal{X}}^{T-t}
of length
(T-t)
for the
Q
-chain

(Z_{0},\dots,Z_{T-t-1})
started from
Z_{0}=y
.
We use the standard composition notation: if
\nu
is a distribution on
{\mathcal{X}}
, then
\nu M
denotes
the induced distribution on
{\mathcal{X}}^{T-t}
obtained by first sampling
Y_{0}\sim\nu
and then sampling a
trajectory
Y\sim M(Y_{0},\cdot)
; equivalently, for any
A\subseteq{\mathcal{X}}^{T-t}
,

(\nu M)(A)=\sum_{y\in{\mathcal{X}}}\nu(y)\,M(y,A).

Then, conditional on
X_{t}=x
,
under
R^{(t)}
we have
X_{t+1}\sim Q(x,\cdot)
and
Y
has distribution
Q(x,\cdot)M
, whereas
under
R^{(t+1)}
we have
X_{t+1}\sim P(x,\cdot)
and
Y
has distribution
P(x,\cdot)M
.
Hence

\Big\|R^{(t+1)}(\cdot\mid X_{t}=x)-R^{(t)}(\cdot\mid X_{t}=x)\Big\|_{\mathrm{TV}}=\|P(x,\cdot)M-Q(x,\cdot)M\|_{\mathrm{TV}}.

Finally, total variation is contractive under a common Markov kernel (by Lemma
50
): for any distributions
\nu,\eta

on
{\mathcal{X}}
,

\|\nu M-\eta M\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\leq\|\nu-\eta\|_{\mathrm{TV}}.

Applying this with
\nu=P(x,\cdot)
and
\eta=Q(x,\cdot)
yields

\|P(x,\cdot)M-Q(x,\cdot)M\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\leq\|P(x,\cdot)-Q(x,\cdot)\|_{\mathrm{TV}}.

Substituting into the bound on
\|R^{(t+1)}-R^{(t)}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}
gives

\|R^{(t+1)}-R^{(t)}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\leq\sum_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}\Pr_{R^{(t)}}(X_{t}=x)\,\|P(x,\cdot)-Q(x,\cdot)\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\leq\sup_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}\|P(x,\cdot)-Q(x,\cdot)\|_{\mathrm{TV}}.

Summing over
t=0,\dots,T-1
completes the proof:

\big\|\mathbb{P}^{(T)}_{\mu,P}-\mathbb{P}^{(T)}_{\mu,Q}\big\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\leq T\cdot\sup_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}\|P(x,\cdot)-Q(x,\cdot)\|_{\mathrm{TV}}.

∎

We now prove a slight extension of the above lemma to the case of trajectories sampled from Markov chains with absorbing states, and hence the trajectory length is not fixed.

Lemma 53
.

Let
{\mathcal{X}}
be finite. Let
P,Q
be Markov kernels on
{\mathcal{X}}
and let
T\subseteq{\mathcal{X}}
be absorbing for both
P
and
Q
.
Let
\tau:=\inf\{t\geq 0:\ X_{t}\in T\}
. For an integer
M\geq 0
, define the truncated stopping time

\tau\wedge M
and consider the corresponding stopped paths
X_{0:\tau}
and
X_{0:\tau\wedge M}
.
Let
\mathcal{L}_{P}(X_{0:\tau})
and
\mathcal{L}_{Q}(X_{0:\tau})
denote the laws of
X_{0:\tau}
under
P
and
Q

(respectively), for the same initial law
\mu
.
Then

\big\|\mathcal{L}_{P}(X_{0:\tau})-\mathcal{L}_{Q}(X_{0:\tau})\big\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\ \leq\ M\cdot\sup_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}\|P(x,\cdot)-Q(x,\cdot)\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\;+\;\Pr_{P}(\tau>M)\;+\;\Pr_{Q}(\tau>M).

Proof.

Let
\mathbb{P}^{(M)}_{\mu,P}
and
\mathbb{P}^{(M)}_{\mu,Q}
be the laws of
(X_{0},\dots,X_{M})
under kernels

P
and
Q
, respectively. The truncated stopped path
X_{0:\tau\wedge M}
is a deterministic function
of
(X_{0},\dots,X_{M})
,
hence by the data processing inequality for total variation (Lemma
50
),

\big\|\mathcal{L}_{P}(X_{0:\tau\wedge M})-\mathcal{L}_{Q}(X_{0:\tau\wedge M})\big\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\ \leq\ \big\|\mathbb{P}^{(M)}_{\mu,P}-\mathbb{P}^{(M)}_{\mu,Q}\big\|_{\mathrm{TV}}.

By Lemma
52
,

\big\|\mathbb{P}^{(M)}_{\mu,P}-\mathbb{P}^{(M)}_{\mu,Q}\big\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\ \leq\ M\cdot\sup_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}\|P(x,\cdot)-Q(x,\cdot)\|_{\mathrm{TV}},

and therefore

\big\|\mathcal{L}_{P}(X_{0:\tau\wedge M})-\mathcal{L}_{Q}(X_{0:\tau\wedge M})\big\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\ \leq\ M\cdot\sup_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}\|P(x,\cdot)-Q(x,\cdot)\|_{\mathrm{TV}}.

It remains to compare the true stopped path
X_{0:\tau}
with the truncated one
X_{0:\tau\wedge M}
.
On the event
\{\tau\leq M\}
we have the identity
X_{0:\tau}=X_{0:\tau\wedge M}
. Hence for any event
A

in the stopped-path sigma-field,

\Pr_{P}(X_{0:\tau}\in A)\leq\Pr_{P}(X_{0:\tau\wedge M}\in A)\;+\;\Pr_{P}(\tau>M),

(43)

and similarly

\Pr_{Q}(X_{0:\tau}\in A)\leq\Pr_{Q}(X_{0:\tau\wedge M}\in A)\;+\;\Pr_{Q}(\tau>M).

(44)

Applying (
43
) to the complement
A^{c}
and using
\Pr(\cdot)=1-\Pr(\cdot^{c})
yields the
corresponding reverse inequality:

\Pr_{P}(X_{0:\tau}\in A)\geq\Pr_{P}(X_{0:\tau\wedge M}\in A)\;-\;\Pr_{P}(\tau>M),

and likewise

\Pr_{Q}(X_{0:\tau}\in A)\geq\Pr_{Q}(X_{0:\tau\wedge M}\in A)\;-\;\Pr_{Q}(\tau>M).

Combining upper and lower bounds, we obtain for every
A
,

\big|\Pr_{P}(X_{0:\tau}\in A)-\Pr_{Q}(X_{0:\tau}\in A)\big|\leq\big|\Pr_{P}(X_{0:\tau\wedge M}\in A)-\Pr_{Q}(X_{0:\tau\wedge M}\in A)\big|+\Pr_{P}(\tau>M)+\Pr_{Q}(\tau>M).

Taking a supremum over
A
gives

\big\|\mathcal{L}_{P}(X_{0:\tau})-\mathcal{L}_{Q}(X_{0:\tau})\big\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\ \leq\ \big\|\mathcal{L}_{P}(X_{0:\tau\wedge M})-\mathcal{L}_{Q}(X_{0:\tau\wedge M})\big\|_{\mathrm{TV}}+\Pr_{P}(\tau>M)+\Pr_{Q}(\tau>M).

Substituting the earlier bound on the truncated stopped-path TV completes the proof.
∎

The following lemma is a standard result about the total variation distance between distributions induced by softmax over logits.

Lemma 54
0Softmax Lipschitz bound).

Let
\mathcal{Y}
be finite. For logits
o,o^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{Y}}
, define

\sigma(o)(y):=\frac{e^{o(y)}}{\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Y}}e^{o(z)}}.

Then

\|\sigma(o)-\sigma(o^{\prime})\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\ \leq\ \|o-o^{\prime}\|_{\infty}.

Proof.

Let
o_{t}:=(1-t)o+to^{\prime}
and
p_{t}:=\sigma(o_{t})
. Then

\sigma(o^{\prime})-\sigma(o)=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{d}{dt}p_{t}\,dt.

By the softmax derivative identity (Lemma
62
) with parameter
t
, each coordinate satisfies

\Big|\frac{d}{dt}p_{t}(y)\Big|=p_{t}(y)\Big|(o^{\prime}(y)-o(y))-\sum_{z}p_{t}(z)(o^{\prime}(z)-o(z))\Big|\leq 2\,p_{t}(y)\,\|o^{\prime}-o\|_{\infty}.

Summing over
y
gives

\Big\|\frac{d}{dt}p_{t}\Big\|_{1}\leq 2\|o^{\prime}-o\|_{\infty},

hence

\|\sigma(o^{\prime})-\sigma(o)\|_{\mathrm{TV}}=\frac{1}{2}\|\sigma(o^{\prime})-\sigma(o)\|_{1}\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}\Big\|\frac{d}{dt}p_{t}\Big\|_{1}\,dt\leq\|o^{\prime}-o\|_{\infty}.

∎

We now apply the above lemmas to the case of Markov kernels induced by the logit structure (
Section

E.1
).

Lemma 55
0Kernel TV bound).

Let
P_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}
and
P_{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}}
be Markov kernels induced
by the logit structure with parameters
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
and
(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})

(see
Section

E.1
).
Then

\sup_{x}\big\|P_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}(x,\cdot)-P_{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}}(x,\cdot)\big\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\ \leq\ |\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}|+|\beta-\beta^{\prime}|+|\gamma-\gamma^{\prime}|.

Proof.

Fix
x
. By the logit structure,
P_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}(x,\cdot)
is a softmax over logits

o_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}(x,\cdot)
. By inspection of the logit structure (including boundary cases),
each coordinate of
o(x,\cdot)
changes by at most

|\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}|+|\beta-\beta^{\prime}|+|\gamma-\gamma^{\prime}|
when the parameters are changed, hence

\|o_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}(x,\cdot)-o_{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}}(x,\cdot)\|_{\infty}\leq|\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}|+|\beta-\beta^{\prime}|+|\gamma-\gamma^{\prime}|.

Lemma
54
implies the stated bound after taking
a supremum over
x
.

∎

Combining the kernel TV bound with the trajectory TV lemmas yields the following corollary.

Corollary 3
0Trajectory TV bound from parameter changes).

Let
P_{\theta}
and
P_{\theta^{\prime}}
be Markov kernels (induced by the logit structure) at

\theta=(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
and
\theta^{\prime}=(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})
, with the same initial
distribution
\mu
. Then for every
T\geq 1
,

\big\|\mathbb{P}^{(T)}_{\mu,P_{\theta}}-\mathbb{P}^{(T)}_{\mu,P_{\theta^{\prime}}}\big\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\ \leq\ T\,\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|_{1}.

If
T\subseteq{\mathcal{X}}
is absorbing and
\tau
is the corresponding hitting time, then for every
integer
M\geq 0
,

\big\|p_{\text{roll}}(\theta)-p_{\text{roll}}(\theta^{\prime})\big\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\ \leq\ M\,\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|_{1}+\Pr_{\theta}(\tau>M)+\Pr_{\theta^{\prime}}(\tau>M).

Proof.

Combine Lemma
52
with Lemma
55
for the
fixed-horizon bound, and combine Lemma
53
with
Lemma
55
for the stopped-path bound.
∎

F.2
Absorption time bounds

The stopped-path TV bound in Corollary
3
involves tail probabilities
\Pr(\tau>M)
, which we control via a geometric bound on the absorption time.

Lemma 56
0Geometric absorption tail).

Let
P
be a Markov kernel on
{\mathcal{X}}
with terminal states
\{a_{n},b_{n}\}
,
and let
\tau:=\inf\{t\geq 0:\ X_{t}\in\{a_{n},b_{n}\}\}
.
If for every non-terminal state
x
, the two-step absorption probability satisfies
P^{2}(x,\{a_{n},b_{n}\})\geq p
for some
p\in(0,1]
, then for every integer
m\geq 0
,

\Pr(\tau>2m)\leq(1-p)^{m},\qquad\text{and hence}\qquad\mathbb{E}[\tau]\leq\frac{2}{p}.

Proof.

For each
t\geq 0
, on the event
\{\tau>2t\}
we have
X_{2t}\notin\{a_{n},b_{n}\}
and therefore

\Pr(\tau>2(t+1)\mid\tau>2t)\leq\Pr(X_{2(t+1)}\notin\{a_{n},b_{n}\}\mid X_{2t}\notin\{a_{n},b_{n}\})\leq 1-p.

Thus
\Pr(\tau>2(t+1))\leq(1-p)\Pr(\tau>2t)
, and iterating gives
\Pr(\tau>2m)\leq(1-p)^{m}
.
The expectation bound follows from
\mathbb{E}[\tau]\leq 2\sum_{m\geq 0}\Pr(\tau>2m)
.
∎

To apply Lemma
56
, we must verify that the two-step absorption probability is lower bounded at initialization.
The next lemma establishes this via a two-step argument: from any non-terminal state, one can first perform a long jump to a penultimate state, then move forward to the terminal.

Lemma 57
0Initial lower bound on two-step absorption probability).

Under the base model assumptions of
Section

6.1
—specifically that
p_{\mathrm{fwd}}>p_{\mathrm{bwd}}+\zeta
and
p_{\mathrm{switch}}>\upsilon
—the minimum two-step absorption probability satisfies

p_{\mathrm{abs}}^{(2)}(\theta_{0})\ :=\ \min_{x\notin\{a_{n},b_{n}\}}P_{\theta_{0}}^{2}(x,\{a_{n},b_{n}\})\ \geq\ \frac{c}{n}

for some constant
c=c(\upsilon,\zeta)>0
and all
n
sufficiently large.

Proof.

Fix a non-terminal vertex
x
. We show that with probability
\Omega(1/n)
, the chain reaches a terminal state in exactly two steps via the following path: first, a long jump to a penultimate state
a_{n-1}
or
b_{n-1}
; then, a forward step to the corresponding terminal
a_{n}
or
b_{n}
.

Step 1: Long jump to penultimate state.

By the logit structure (
Section

E.1
), all distant targets share the same logit
\gamma
.
The penultimate states
\{a_{n-1},b_{n-1}\}
are among the
\Theta(n)
distant targets from any non-terminal
x
.
Since the switch probability
p_{\mathrm{switch}}(x)>\upsilon
and all distant targets have equal logit, the probability of jumping to
\{a_{n-1},b_{n-1}\}
is at least
\Omega(\upsilon/n)
.

Step 2: Forward step to terminal.

From a penultimate state
a_{n-1}
(or
b_{n-1}
), the forward step leads to the terminal
a_{n}
(or
b_{n}
).
By the base model assumption, the forward probability satisfies
p_{\mathrm{fwd}}>p_{\mathrm{bwd}}+\zeta
, which implies
p_{\mathrm{fwd}}\geq\zeta/2
(since probabilities are non-negative).

Combining both steps, the two-step absorption probability is at least

P_{\theta_{0}}^{2}(x,\{a_{n},b_{n}\})\ \geq\ \Omega\Big(\frac{\upsilon}{n}\Big)\cdot\Omega(\zeta)\ =\ \Omega\Big(\frac{1}{n}\Big).

Taking the minimum over all non-terminal
x
yields the result.
∎

Having established that
p_{\mathrm{abs}}^{(2)}(\theta_{0})>0
at initialization, we need to ensure it remains positive throughout gradient flow.
The next lemma shows that
p_{\mathrm{abs}}^{(2)}
is Lipschitz in the parameters, so it cannot drop too quickly as
\theta
evolves.

Lemma 58
0Lipschitz stability of
p_{\mathrm{abs}}^{(2)}
in parameters).

Let
P_{\theta}
be the Markov kernel (induced by the logit structure) at parameters

\theta=(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
, and define

p_{\mathrm{abs}}^{(2)}(\theta)\ :=\ \min_{x\notin\{a_{n},b_{n}\}}P_{\theta}^{2}(x,\{a_{n},b_{n}\}).

Then for all
\theta,\theta^{\prime}
,

p_{\mathrm{abs}}^{(2)}(\theta)\ \geq\ p_{\mathrm{abs}}^{(2)}(\theta^{\prime})\ -\ C\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|_{1}

for some constant
C>0
.

Proof.

Fix
x\notin\{a_{n},b_{n}\}
. The two-step transition probability can be written as

P_{\theta}^{2}(x,\{a_{n},b_{n}\})=\sum_{y}P_{\theta}(x,y)P_{\theta}(y,\{a_{n},b_{n}\}).

By the triangle inequality and Lemma
55
, changing
\theta
to
\theta^{\prime}
affects each one-step kernel by at most
\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|_{1}
in total variation.
Since the two-step probability is a composition of two kernels, the change in
P^{2}(x,\{a_{n},b_{n}\})
is bounded by
C\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|_{1}
for some constant
C
.
Taking the minimum over all non-terminal
x
yields the result.
∎

For the gradient bounds below, we also need stability of the
long-jump probability
—the probability of jumping to a distant state in one step.

Lemma 59
0Lipschitz stability of long-jump probability).

Let
P_{\theta}
be the Markov kernel (induced by the logit structure) at parameters
\theta=(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
.
For each non-terminal state
x
, let
\mathrm{LJ}(x):=\{y:|D(y)-D(x)|\geq 2\}
denote the set of long-jump targets, and define

p_{\mathrm{lj}}(\theta)\ :=\ \min_{x\notin\{a_{n},b_{n}\}}P_{\theta}(x,\mathrm{LJ}(x)).

Then for all
\theta,\theta^{\prime}
,

p_{\mathrm{lj}}(\theta)\ \geq\ p_{\mathrm{lj}}(\theta^{\prime})\ -\ \|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|_{1}.

Proof.

Fix
x\notin\{a_{n},b_{n}\}
. By definition of total variation,

\big|P_{\theta}(x,\mathrm{LJ}(x))-P_{\theta^{\prime}}(x,\mathrm{LJ}(x))\big|\leq\big\|P_{\theta}(x,\cdot)-P_{\theta^{\prime}}(x,\cdot)\big\|_{\mathrm{TV}}.

By Lemma
55
, the right-hand side is at most
\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|_{1}
.
Taking the minimum over all non-terminal
x
yields the result.
∎

F.3
Exponentially small gradients for hard examples

The final ingredient shows that when all starting states are far from the terminal, the loss gradient is exponentially small.

Lemma 60
0Exponentially small gradient for hard starts).

Fix constants
a\in(0,1)
and
q_{0}\in(0,1)
. Let
\mathcal{H}\subseteq{\mathcal{X}}
be a set of start states with
D(v)\geq an
for all
v\in\mathcal{H}
, let
(w_{v})_{v\in\mathcal{H}}
be nonnegative weights summing to
1
, and define

\mathcal{L}(\theta)\ :=\ \sum_{v\in\mathcal{H}}w_{v}\,S(v;\theta),\qquad\mathcal{U}_{q_{0}}\ :=\ \Big\{\theta:\ p_{\mathrm{lj}}(\theta)\geq q_{0}\Big\}.

Then there exist constants
C,c>0
(independent of
n
) such that for every
\theta\in\mathcal{U}_{q_{0}}
,

\|\nabla\mathcal{L}(\theta)\|_{1}\ \leq\ C\,\frac{n}{\zeta}\,e^{-cn}.

Proof.

Fix
\theta\in\mathcal{U}_{q_{0}}
, so
p_{\mathrm{lj}}(\theta)\geq q_{0}
. By Lemma
46
(with the long-jump probability lower bounded by
q_{0}
) and the trivial bounds
p_{f}^{\max},p_{b}^{\max},p_{\mathrm{lj}}^{\max}\leq 1
, for each
\vartheta\in\{\alpha,\beta,\gamma\}
,

\sup_{v\in\mathcal{H}}\big|\partial_{\vartheta}S(v;\theta)\big|\ \leq\ C\,\frac{n}{\zeta}\,e^{-cn}.

Since
\mathcal{L}
is a convex combination, the triangle inequality gives
|\partial_{\vartheta}\mathcal{L}|\leq\sup_{v}|\partial_{\vartheta}S(v;\theta)|
. Summing over
\vartheta
yields the stated bound.
∎

F.4
Proof of
Theorem

3

We now assemble the above lemmas to prove the main result.

Proof of
Theorem

3
.

By Lemma
57
, the base model assumptions ensure that

q_{0}:=p_{\mathrm{abs}}^{(2)}(\theta_{0})\geq c/n>0
for some constant
c=c(\upsilon,\zeta)>0
and
n
sufficiently large.
By an analogous argument (with the same
\upsilon
bound on switch probability), the long-jump probability satisfies
p_{\mathrm{lj}}(\theta_{0})\geq c^{\prime}/n
for some constant
c^{\prime}>0
; without loss of generality, assume
c^{\prime}\geq c
so that
p_{\mathrm{lj}}(\theta_{0})\geq q_{0}
.

Define the safe region

\mathcal{U}\ :=\ \{\theta:\ p_{\mathrm{abs}}^{(2)}(\theta)\geq q_{0}/2\text{ and }p_{\mathrm{lj}}(\theta)\geq q_{0}/2\},

and the exit time

t^{\star}\ :=\ \inf\{t\geq 0:\ \theta(t)\notin\mathcal{U}\},

with the convention
t^{\star}=\infty
if the set is empty. By definition, for every
s\in[0,t^{\star})

we have
\theta(s)\in\mathcal{U}
, and hence Lemma
60
(applied with
q_{0}/2

in place of
q_{0}
) yields

\|\dot{\theta}(s)\|_{1}=\|\nabla\mathcal{L}(\theta(s))\|_{1}\ \leq\ C\,\frac{n}{\zeta}\,e^{-cn}\qquad(0\leq s<t^{\star}).

Consequently, for every
s\geq 0
,

\|\theta(s\wedge t^{\star})-\theta(0)\|_{1}\leq\int_{0}^{s\wedge t^{\star}}\|\dot{\theta}(u)\|_{1}\,du\leq(s\wedge t^{\star})\cdot\Big(C\,\frac{n}{\zeta}\,e^{-cn}\Big).

Applying Lemmas
58
and
59
with
\theta=\theta(s\wedge t^{\star})
and
\theta^{\prime}=\theta(0)
gives

\displaystyle p_{\mathrm{abs}}^{(2)}(\theta(s\wedge t^{\star}))

\displaystyle\geq q_{0}-(s\wedge t^{\star})\cdot\Big(C^{\prime}\cdot C\,\frac{n}{\zeta}\,e^{-cn}\Big),

\displaystyle p_{\mathrm{lj}}(\theta(s\wedge t^{\star}))

\displaystyle\geq q_{0}-(s\wedge t^{\star})\cdot\Big(C\,\frac{n}{\zeta}\,e^{-cn}\Big).

In particular, if
s\leq\frac{q_{0}}{2}\cdot\frac{\zeta}{C^{\prime\prime}n}\,e^{cn}
for
C^{\prime\prime}:=\max(C^{\prime}C,C)
, then both bounds remain at least
q_{0}/2
,
so
\theta(s\wedge t^{\star})\in\mathcal{U}
. If
t^{\star}\leq s
, then by definition of
t^{\star}
we would have

\theta(t^{\star})\notin\mathcal{U}
, a contradiction. Therefore
t^{\star}>s
.

Setting

t_{\mathrm{boot}}(n)\ :=\ \frac{q_{0}}{4}\cdot\frac{\zeta}{C^{\prime\prime}n}\,e^{cn}\ =\ \frac{c\,\zeta}{4C^{\prime\prime}n^{2}}\,e^{cn},

we conclude that for every
t\in[0,t_{\mathrm{boot}}(n)]
one has
t<t^{\star}
, and hence

p_{\mathrm{abs}}^{(2)}(\theta(u))\ \geq\ \frac{q_{0}}{2},\quad p_{\mathrm{lj}}(\theta(u))\ \geq\ \frac{q_{0}}{2}\qquad\forall\,u\in[0,t].

Now fix
t\in[0,t_{\mathrm{boot}}(n)]
and let
\tau:=\inf\{t\geq 0:\ X_{t}\in\{a_{n},b_{n}\}\}
.
Because
p_{\mathrm{abs}}^{(2)}(\theta(0))=q_{0}
and
p_{\mathrm{abs}}^{(2)}(\theta(t))\geq q_{0}/2
, Lemma
56
yields

\Pr_{\theta(0)}(\tau>2M)\leq(1-q_{0}/2)^{M},\qquad\Pr_{\theta(t)}(\tau>2M)\leq(1-q_{0}/2)^{M}\qquad\forall\,M\geq 0.

Finally, apply the stopped-path bound in Corollary
3
with

\theta=\theta(0)
and
\theta^{\prime}=\theta(t)
:

\big\|p_{\text{roll}}(\theta(t))-p_{\text{roll}}(\theta(0))\big\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\ \leq\ M\,\|\theta(t)-\theta(0)\|_{1}\;+\;\Pr_{\theta(t)}(\tau>M)+\Pr_{\theta(0)}(\tau>M).

Using the tail estimates (noting that
\Pr(\tau>M)\leq\Pr(\tau>2\lfloor M/2\rfloor)\leq(1-q_{0}/2)^{\lfloor M/2\rfloor}
) and the drift bound

\|\theta(t)-\theta(0)\|_{1}\leq t\cdot\big(C\frac{n}{\zeta}e^{-cn}\big)
gives, for all
M\geq 0
,

\big\|p_{\text{roll}}(\theta(t))-p_{\text{roll}}(\theta(0))\big\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\ \leq\ M\,t\cdot\Big(C\,\frac{n}{\zeta}\,e^{-cn}\Big)\;+\;2(1-q_{0}/2)^{M/2}.

We choose
M
to (approximately) minimize the right-hand side. Treat
M\geq 0
as a real variable and write

f(M)\ :=\ A\,M+2e^{-\lambda M},

where
A:=t\big(C\frac{n}{\zeta}e^{-cn}\big)
and
\lambda:=-\frac{1}{2}\log(1-q_{0}/2)>0
.
Differentiating gives

f^{\prime}(M)\ =\ A-2\lambda e^{-\lambda M},\qquad f^{\prime\prime}(M)\ =\ 2\lambda^{2}e^{-\lambda M}\ >\ 0,

so
f
is convex and has a unique minimizer whenever it exists in
[0,\infty)
.
If
A<2\lambda
, the minimizer satisfies
f^{\prime}(M^{\star})=0
, i.e.

e^{-\lambda M^{\star}}\ =\ \frac{A}{2\lambda}\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad M^{\star}\ =\ \frac{1}{\lambda}\log\Big(\frac{2\lambda}{A}\Big).

Substituting
e^{-\lambda M^{\star}}=A/(2\lambda)
yields the optimized value

f(M^{\star})\ =\ A\cdot\frac{1}{\lambda}\log\Big(\frac{2\lambda}{A}\Big)\ +\ 2\cdot\frac{A}{2\lambda}\ =\ \frac{A}{\lambda}\Big(1+\log\Big(\frac{2\lambda}{A}\Big)\Big).

Returning to integer
M
, set
M_{\mathrm{opt}}:=\lceil M^{\star}\rceil
. Since
f
is convex and

f^{\prime}
is
A
-Lipschitz, one has
f(M_{\mathrm{opt}})\leq f(M^{\star})+A
. In particular, for all

A<2\lambda
,

\big\|p_{\text{roll}}(\theta(t))-p_{\text{roll}}(\theta(0))\big\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\ \leq\ f(M_{\mathrm{opt}})\ \leq\ \frac{A}{\lambda}\Big(1+\log\Big(\frac{2\lambda}{A}\Big)\Big)\ +\ A.

Equivalently, writing
x:=A/(2\lambda)\in(0,1)
, this becomes

f(M_{\mathrm{opt}})\ \leq\ 2x\big(1+\log(1/x)\big)\ +\ 2\lambda x.

Consequently, to guarantee the total-variation bound is at most
\varepsilon
, it suffices to enforce

\frac{A}{\lambda}\Big(1+\log\Big(\frac{2\lambda}{A}\Big)\Big)\ +\ A\ \leq\ \varepsilon,

where
A=t\big(C\frac{n}{\zeta}e^{-cn}\big)
and
\lambda=-\frac{1}{2}\log(1-q_{0}/2)
.

Since
q_{0}=c/n
, we have
\lambda=-\frac{1}{2}\log(1-c/(2n))\approx c/(4n)
for large
n
, i.e.,
\lambda=\Theta(1/n)
.

To match the bound in
Theorem

3
, we verify that
t<\varepsilon^{2}\cdot e^{\kappa n}
implies the above bound.
For
t=\varepsilon^{2}\cdot e^{\kappa n}
, we have

A\ =\ \varepsilon^{2}\cdot e^{\kappa n}\cdot\frac{Cn}{\zeta}\,e^{-cn}\ =\ \varepsilon^{2}\cdot\frac{Cn}{\zeta}\,e^{-(c-\kappa)n}.

The logarithmic factor satisfies

\log\Big(\frac{2\lambda}{A}\Big)\ =\ \log(2\lambda)-\log(\varepsilon^{2})-\log\Big(\frac{Cn}{\zeta}\Big)+(c-\kappa)n\ =\ (c-\kappa)n+O\big(\log n+\log(1/\varepsilon)\big).

Substituting into the bound gives

\frac{A}{\lambda}\Big(1+\log\Big(\frac{2\lambda}{A}\Big)\Big)+A\ \leq\ \frac{A}{\lambda}\cdot(c-\kappa)n\;+\;O\Big(\frac{A}{\lambda}\big(\log n+\log(1/\varepsilon)\big)\Big)\;+\;A.

Since
\lambda=\Theta(1/n)
, we have
A/\lambda=\Theta(n)\cdot A
. The dominant term is thus

\frac{A}{\lambda}(c-\kappa)n=O(1)\cdot\varepsilon^{2}\cdot\frac{C(c-\kappa)n^{3}}{\zeta}\,e^{-(c-\kappa)n}.

Since
n^{3}e^{-(c-\kappa)n}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
for any
\kappa<c
, all terms vanish exponentially.
Specifically, for any
\kappa\in(0,c)
, there exists
n_{0}=n_{0}(a,\upsilon,\varepsilon)
such that for all
n\geq n_{0}
, the left-hand side is at most
\varepsilon
.
Setting
\kappa:=c/2
yields the claimed result.

Finally, we verify that the time scale
t<\varepsilon^{2}e^{\kappa n}
lies within the bootstrap regime
t\leq t_{\mathrm{boot}}(n)
. Since
t_{\mathrm{boot}}(n)=\frac{q_{0}\zeta}{4C^{\prime}Cn}\,e^{cn}
and
q_{0}=c/n
, we have
t_{\mathrm{boot}}(n)=\frac{c\,\zeta}{4C^{\prime}Cn^{2}}\,e^{cn}
. With
\kappa=c/2
,

\frac{\varepsilon^{2}e^{\kappa n}}{t_{\mathrm{boot}}(n)}\ =\ \frac{4C^{\prime}C\varepsilon^{2}n^{2}}{c\,\zeta}\,e^{-(c-\kappa)n}\ =\ \frac{4C^{\prime}C\varepsilon^{2}n^{2}}{c\,\zeta}\,e^{-(c/2)n}\ \to\ 0

as
n\to\infty
. Thus, for
n\geq n_{0}
(possibly enlarging
n_{0}
), we have
\varepsilon^{2}e^{\kappa n}<t_{\mathrm{boot}}(n)
, so the analysis above is valid for all
t<\varepsilon^{2}e^{\kappa n}
.
∎

Appendix G
Supporting Lemmas

Lemma 61
.

Under the assumptions and notation of
Sections

6
and
4
. In particular, the distribution
D^{Q}_{m}
is invariant under vertex permutations, and the value matrix
A(t)
evolves by gradient flow

\frac{d}{dt}A(t)=-\nabla_{A}L(D^{Q}_{m},A(t)),

For
\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
, define
A(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
on the vertex–edge block by

A(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)_{v,(u,w)}=\begin{cases}\alpha&\text{if }v=u,\\
\beta&\text{if }v=w,\\
\gamma&\text{if }v\notin\{u,w\},\end{cases}\quad v,u,w\in V,\ u\neq w,

with all other entries defined as in
Section

6
.

Let
A(t)
solve gradient flow with
A(0)=A(\alpha_{0},\beta_{0},\gamma_{0})
for some
\alpha_{0},\beta_{0},\gamma_{0}
. Then for all
t\geq 0
there exist scalars
\alpha(t),\beta(t),\gamma(t)
such that

A_{v,(u,w)}(t)=\begin{cases}\alpha(t)&\text{if }v=u,\\
\beta(t)&\text{if }v=w,\\
\gamma(t)&\text{if }v\notin\{u,w\},\end{cases}\quad\forall\,v\in V,\ (u,w)\in E,\ u\neq w.

Define the reduced loss

\tilde{L}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma):=L\big(D^{Q}_{m},A(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\big).

The scalars
\alpha(t),\beta(t),\gamma(t)
satisfy

\displaystyle\dot{\alpha}(t)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2n(2n-1)}\,\partial_{\alpha}\tilde{L}\big(\alpha(t),\beta(t),\gamma(t)\big),

\displaystyle\dot{\beta}(t)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2n(2n-1)}\,\partial_{\beta}\tilde{L}\big(\alpha(t),\beta(t),\gamma(t)\big),

\displaystyle\dot{\gamma}(t)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{(2n-2)\cdot 2n(2n-1)}\,\partial_{\gamma}\tilde{L}\big(\alpha(t),\beta(t),\gamma(t)\big).

Proof.

For a permutation
\pi
of the vertex set
V
, define an operator
T_{\pi}
on matrices
A
by

\big(T_{\pi}A\big)_{v,(u,w)}:=A_{\pi^{-1}(v),\,(\pi^{-1}(u),\pi^{-1}(w))},\quad v,u,w\in V.

Equivalently,
(T_{\pi}A)_{\pi(v),(\pi(u),\pi(w))}=A_{v,(u,w)}
. This corresponds to consistently relabelling all vertices by
\pi
.

By construction of
D^{Q}_{m}
and the RL loss, relabelling vertices does not change the distribution over inputs or the loss, hence

L(D^{Q}_{m},T_{\pi}A)=L(D^{Q}_{m},A)\quad\text{for all }A,\pi.

Differentiating gives gradient equivariance

\nabla_{A}L(D^{Q}_{m},T_{\pi}A)=T_{\pi}\,\nabla_{A}L(D^{Q}_{m},A).

Now let
A(t)
solve gradient flow. Then

\frac{d}{dt}\big(T_{\pi}A(t)\big)=T_{\pi}\frac{d}{dt}A(t)=-T_{\pi}\nabla_{A}L(D^{Q}_{m},A(t))=-\nabla_{A}L(D^{Q}_{m},T_{\pi}A(t)).

So
T_{\pi}A(t)
is also a solution of the same ODE.

At
t=0
, the symmetric initialization from
Section

6.1
gives

A_{v,(u,w)}(0)=\begin{cases}\alpha_{0}&v=u,\\
\beta_{0}&v=w,\\
\gamma_{0}&v\notin\{u,w\},\end{cases}

which is clearly invariant under any permutation of vertex labels, hence
T_{\pi}A(0)=A(0)
for all
\pi
. By uniqueness of solutions to the ODE, we must have

T_{\pi}A(t)=A(t)\quad\text{for all }t\geq 0,\ \pi.

Thus
A(t)
always lies in the fixed-point subspace

\mathcal{S}:=\{A:T_{\pi}A=A\ \text{for all }\pi\}.

To describe
\mathcal{S}
, consider triples
(v,u,w)\in V^{3}
with
u\neq w
. Two such triples lie in the same orbit under the action of permutations if and only if they have the same equality pattern among
\{v,u,w\}
. There are exactly three possibilities:

1.

v=u
,
v\neq w
;

2.

v=w
,
v\neq u
;

3.

v,u,w
are all distinct.

Given any two triples with the same pattern, we can construct a permutation
\pi
mapping the first triple to the second (e.g., map
u\mapsto u^{\prime}
,
w\mapsto w^{\prime}
, and extend arbitrarily), so these are indeed the three orbits. If
A\in\mathcal{S}
, then for any two entries
A_{v,(u,w)}
and
A_{v^{\prime},(u^{\prime},w^{\prime})}
from the same orbit we must have

A_{v,(u,w)}=A_{v^{\prime},(u^{\prime},w^{\prime})},

because some
\pi
sends one triple to the other and
A=T_{\pi}A
. Therefore there exist scalars
\alpha,\beta,\gamma
such that

A_{v,(u,w)}=\begin{cases}\alpha&v=u,\\
\beta&v=w,\\
\gamma&v\notin\{u,w\},\end{cases}

which is exactly the form
A(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
. This gives the representation of
A(t)
in terms of
\alpha(t),\beta(t),\gamma(t)
.

For the dynamics, write
A(t)=A(\alpha(t),\beta(t),\gamma(t))
and define
\tilde{L}
as above. By the chain rule,

\partial_{\alpha}\tilde{L}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=\sum_{v=u,\,u\neq w}\frac{\partial}{\partial A_{v,(u,w)}}L(D^{Q}_{m},A(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)),

and similarly for
\beta,\gamma
. Gradient flow gives

\frac{d}{dt}A_{v,(u,w)}(t)=-\,\frac{\partial}{\partial A_{v,(u,w)}}L(D^{Q}_{m},A(t)).

By symmetry, all entries with
v=u
share the same value
\alpha(t)
and the same time derivative
\dot{\alpha}(t)
. Summing the previous identity over all such entries and comparing with the expression for
\partial_{\alpha}\tilde{L}
yields

N_{\alpha}\,\dot{\alpha}(t)=-\partial_{\alpha}\tilde{L}\big(\alpha(t),\beta(t),\gamma(t)\big),

where
N_{\alpha}
is the number of pairs
(v,(u,w))
with
v=u
and
u\neq w
. For each ordered pair
(u,w)
with
u\neq w
, there is exactly one
v
with
v=u
, so

N_{\alpha}=|E_{\text{dir}}|=|V|(|V|-1),

where
E_{\text{dir}}=\{(u,w)\in V^{2}:u\neq w\}
. The same reasoning gives
N_{\beta}=|V|(|V|-1)
, and for
\gamma
, for each
(u,w)
there are
|V|-2
vertices
v\notin\{u,w\}
, so

N_{\gamma}=(|V|-2)|V|(|V|-1).

Dividing by these constants gives the claimed ODEs for
\alpha,\beta,\gamma
.
∎

Lemma 62
0Softmax derivative).

Let
\mathcal{Y}
be a finite set. For each
\theta\in{\mathbb{R}}
, let
o_{\theta}:\mathcal{Y}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be
differentiable in
\theta
, and define the softmax distribution
P_{\theta}
on
\mathcal{Y}
by

P_{\theta}(y):=\frac{\exp(o_{\theta}(y))}{\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Y}}\exp(o_{\theta}(z))}.

Write
d_{\theta}(y):=\partial_{\theta}o_{\theta}(y)
. Then for every
y\in\mathcal{Y}
,

\partial_{\theta}P_{\theta}(y):=P_{\theta}(y)\Big(d_{\theta}(y)-\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Y}}P_{\theta}(z)\,d_{\theta}(z)\Big).

Equivalently, for any function
w:\mathcal{Y}\to{\mathbb{R}}
,

\sum_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}\big(\partial_{\theta}P_{\theta}(y)\big)\,w(y):=\sum_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}P_{\theta}(y)\,d_{\theta}(y)\,w(y)-\Big(\sum_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}P_{\theta}(y)\,d_{\theta}(y)\Big)\Big(\sum_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}P_{\theta}(y)\,w(y)\Big),

i.e.

\sum_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}\big(\partial_{\theta}P_{\theta}(y)\big)\,w(y):=\mathrm{Cov}_{Y\sim P_{\theta}}\!\big(d_{\theta}(Y),w(Y)\big).

Proof.

Fix
\theta
and abbreviate
o(y):=o_{\theta}(y)
,
P(y):=P_{\theta}(y)
, and

d(y):=\partial_{\theta}o_{\theta}(y)
. Let

Z:=\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Y}}e^{o(z)},\qquad\text{so}\qquad P(y)=\frac{e^{o(y)}}{Z}.

Differentiate using the quotient rule:

\partial_{\theta}P(y):=\frac{\partial_{\theta}e^{o(y)}}{Z}-\frac{e^{o(y)}}{Z^{2}}\,\partial_{\theta}Z.

Since
\partial_{\theta}e^{o(y)}=e^{o(y)}d(y)
and

\partial_{\theta}Z=\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Y}}\partial_{\theta}e^{o(z)}:=\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Y}}e^{o(z)}d(z),

we obtain

\partial_{\theta}P(y):=\frac{e^{o(y)}}{Z}d(y)-\frac{e^{o(y)}}{Z}\cdot\frac{1}{Z}\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Y}}e^{o(z)}d(z).

Recognizing
e^{o(y)}/Z=P(y)
and
e^{o(z)}/Z=P(z)
gives

\partial_{\theta}P(y):=P(y)\Big(d(y)-\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Y}}P(z)d(z)\Big),

which proves the first identity.

Multiplying this by
w(y)
and summing over
y\in\mathcal{Y}
yields

\sum_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}(\partial_{\theta}P(y))w(y):=\sum_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}P(y)d(y)w(y)-\Big(\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Y}}P(z)d(z)\Big)\Big(\sum_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}P(y)w(y)\Big),

which is the stated centered form. The covariance reformulation follows from

\mathrm{Cov}(A,B)={\mathcal{E}}[AB]-{\mathcal{E}}[A]{\mathcal{E}}[B]
with
Y\sim P_{\theta}
.
∎

Lemma 63
0Elementary gambler’s–ruin bound (no martingales)).

Let
(D_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be a Markov chain on
\{0,1,\dots,K\}
with absorbing boundaries
\{0,K\}
.
For each
i\in\{1,\dots,K-1\}
assume

\Pr(D_{t+1}=i-1\mid D_{t}=i)=p_{f}(i),\qquad\Pr(D_{t+1}=i+1\mid D_{t}=i)=p_{b}(i),

and
\Pr(D_{t+1}=i\mid D_{t}=i)=1-p_{f}(i)-p_{b}(i)
(holds allowed).
Assume
p_{f}(i),p_{b}(i)>0
and that there exists
\rho\in(0,1)
such that for all
i\in\{1,\dots,K-1\}
,

\frac{p_{b}(i)}{p_{f}(i)}\ \leq\ \rho.

Let
\tau_{0}:=\inf\{t\geq 0:D_{t}=0\}
and
\tau_{K}:=\inf\{t\geq 0:D_{t}=K\}
.
Then for every
k\in\{0,1,\dots,K\}
,

\Pr_{k}(\tau_{K}<\tau_{0})\ \leq\ \rho^{\,K-k}.

Proof.

Let
h(i):=\Pr_{i}(\tau_{K}<\tau_{0})
. Then
h(0)=0
,
h(K)=1
, and for
1\leq i\leq K-1
,
conditioning on one step gives

h(i)=p_{f}(i)h(i-1)+p_{b}(i)h(i+1)+\bigl(1-p_{f}(i)-p_{b}(i)\bigr)h(i),

hence

p_{f}(i)\bigl(h(i)-h(i-1)\bigr)=p_{b}(i)\bigl(h(i+1)-h(i)\bigr).

(45)

With
\Delta_{i}:=h(i)-h(i-1)
, (
45
) implies

\Delta_{i+1}=\frac{p_{f}(i)}{p_{b}(i)}\Delta_{i}
, hence

\Delta_{i}=\Delta_{1}\prod_{m=1}^{i-1}\frac{p_{f}(m)}{p_{b}(m)}.

Since
h(k)=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\Delta_{i}
and
h(K)=\sum_{i=1}^{K}\Delta_{i}=1
, we get

h(k)=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{k}\prod_{m=1}^{i-1}\frac{p_{f}(m)}{p_{b}(m)}}{\sum_{i=1}^{K}\prod_{m=1}^{i-1}\frac{p_{f}(m)}{p_{b}(m)}}.

Using
p_{f}(m)/p_{b}(m)\geq\rho^{-1}
in both numerator and denominator yields

h(k)\leq\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{k}\rho^{-(i-1)}}{\sum_{i=1}^{K}\rho^{-(i-1)}}:=\rho^{K-k}\cdot\frac{\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\rho^{j}}{\sum_{j=0}^{K-1}\rho^{j}}\leq\rho^{K-k}.

∎

Lemma 64
0Expected absorption time).

Let
(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be a Markov chain on
\{0,1,\dots,N\}
such that
N
is
absorbing, from each
i\in\{1,\dots,N-1\}
the chain moves to
i+1
with
probability
p
and to
i-1
with probability
q
, where
p,q\geq 0
,
p+q=1
and

p>q
, and from
0
the chain moves to
1
with probability
1
.
Let
T_{N}:=\inf\{t\geq 0:X_{t}=N\}
. Then for every
k\in\{0,1,\dots,N\}
,

\mathbb{E}_{k}[T_{N}]:=\frac{N-k}{p-q}+\frac{2pq}{(p-q)^{2}}\left[\left(\frac{q}{p}\right)^{N}-\left(\frac{q}{p}\right)^{k}\right],

and in particular,

\mathbb{E}_{k}[T_{N}]\leq\frac{N-k}{p-q}.

Proof.

For
i\in\{0,1,\dots,N\}
define
m_{i}:=\mathbb{E}_{i}[T_{N}]
.
If
1\leq i\leq N-1
, conditioning on the first step and using the Markov property
gives

m_{i}=1+p\,m_{i+1}+q\,m_{i-1}.

(46)

At
N
we have
m_{N}=0
since
N
is absorbing, and at
0
we have

m_{0}=1+m_{1}

(47)

since the chain moves deterministically from
0
to
1
in one step.

Set
\delta:=p-q>0
and rewrite (
46
) as

p\,m_{i+1}-m_{i}+q\,m_{i-1}=-1,\qquad 1\leq i\leq N-1.

(48)

A particular solution is given by
m_{i}^{\mathrm{part}}=-i/\delta
, because

p\left(-\frac{i+1}{\delta}\right)-\left(-\frac{i}{\delta}\right)+q\left(-\frac{i-1}{\delta}\right):=\frac{-p+q}{\delta}=-1

using
p+q=1
.
For the associated homogeneous equation

p\,u_{i+1}-u_{i}+q\,u_{i-1}=0,

a trial solution
u_{i}=r^{i}
leads to the characteristic equation

pr^{2}-r+q=0
, whose two roots are
r=1
and
r=q/p
.
Writing
\rho:=q/p\in(0,1)
, the general homogeneous solution is

u_{i}=A+B\rho^{i}
, hence every solution to (
48
) has the form

m_{i}=A+B\rho^{i}-\frac{i}{\delta}.

(49)

Imposing
m_{N}=0
in (
49
) yields

0=A+B\rho^{N}-\frac{N}{\delta}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad A=\frac{N}{\delta}-B\rho^{N}.

Next, (
47
) gives
m_{0}=1+m_{1}
. From (
49
),

m_{0}=A+B,\qquad m_{1}=A+B\rho-\frac{1}{\delta},

so

A+B=1+\left(A+B\rho-\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\quad\Longrightarrow\quad B(1-\rho)=1-\frac{1}{\delta}.

Now
1-\rho=1-q/p=(p-q)/p=\delta/p
and, since
p+q=1
, we have

1-\frac{1}{\delta}=\frac{\delta-1}{\delta}=\frac{(p-q)-(p+q)}{\delta}=-\frac{2q}{\delta}
. Therefore

B\cdot\frac{\delta}{p}=-\frac{2q}{\delta}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad B=-\frac{2pq}{\delta^{2}}.

Substituting back gives

A=\frac{N}{\delta}+\frac{2pq}{\delta^{2}}\rho^{N},

and hence for
k\in\{0,1,\dots,N\}
,

m_{k}:=\frac{N-k}{\delta}+\frac{2pq}{\delta^{2}}\bigl(\rho^{N}-\rho^{k}\bigr).

This is the stated closed form after recalling
\delta=p-q
and
\rho=q/p
.

To see that this solution is the unique solution to
(
46
)–(
47
), suppose
\tilde{m}
is another
solution and let
d_{i}:=m_{i}-\tilde{m}_{i}
. Then
d_{N}=0
,
d_{0}=d_{1}
, and
d
solves
the homogeneous recurrence
pd_{i+1}-d_{i}+qd_{i-1}=0
, so
d_{i}=A^{\prime}+B^{\prime}\rho^{i}
.
The condition
d_{0}=d_{1}
implies
A^{\prime}+B^{\prime}=A^{\prime}+B^{\prime}\rho
, hence
B^{\prime}(1-\rho)=0
and
therefore
B^{\prime}=0
, and then
d_{N}=0
forces
A^{\prime}=0
, so
d\equiv 0
.

Finally, since
\rho\in(0,1)
and
k\leq N
, we have
\rho^{N}\leq\rho^{k}
and thus

\rho^{N}-\rho^{k}\leq 0
. Because
2pq/\delta^{2}\geq 0
, the correction term

\frac{2pq}{\delta^{2}}(\rho^{N}-\rho^{k})
is nonpositive, and therefore

\mathbb{E}_{k}[T_{N}]=m_{k}\leq\frac{N-k}{\delta}=\frac{N-k}{p-q}.

∎

Lemma 65
0Absorption time tail bound).

Let
(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be a nearest–neighbor random walk with

\Pr(X_{t+1}=X_{t}+1)=p,\qquad\Pr(X_{t+1}=X_{t}-1)=q=1-p,\qquad p-q\geq c>0,

run on a chain of length
L
with the
right endpoint absorbing
. Assume the walk starts at distance

k
from the absorbing endpoint (i.e.
X_{0}=L-k
), where
k
is a fixed constant independent of
L
.
Let

\tau:=\inf\{t\geq 0:\ X_{t}=L\}

be the absorption time at the right edge. Then for any
t\geq 1
,

\Pr(\tau>t)\;\leq\;\Pr\!\left(\sum_{i=1}^{t}\xi_{i}\leq k-1\right),\qquad\xi_{i}\in\{+1,-1\},\ \ {\mathcal{E}}[\xi_{i}]=p-q=:\mu\ (\geq c),

and by Hoeffding’s inequality,

\Pr(\tau>t)\;\leq\;\exp\!\left(-\frac{(\mu t-(k-1))^{2}}{2t}\right)\;\leq\;\exp\!\left(-\frac{(ct-(k-1))^{2}}{2t}\right).

In particular, taking
t=\lfloor L^{1/4}\rfloor
and using that
k
is constant, for all
L
large enough
(e.g.
L^{1/4}\geq 2(k-1)/c
),

\Pr\!\big(\tau>\lfloor L^{1/4}\rfloor\big)\;\leq\;\exp\!\left(-\frac{c^{2}}{8}\,L^{1/4}\right).

Proof.

Write
X_{t}=X_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{t}\xi_{i}
with i.i.d. increments
\xi_{i}\in\{+1,-1\}
of mean
\mu=p-q\geq c
.
If
\tau>t
then
X_{t}\leq L-1
, hence
\sum_{i=1}^{t}\xi_{i}=X_{t}-X_{0}\leq(L-1)-(L-k)=k-1
, proving the first inequality.
Hoeffding’s inequality for bounded i.i.d. variables yields

\Pr\!\left(\sum_{i=1}^{t}\xi_{i}-\mu t\leq-(\mu t-(k-1))\right)\leq\exp\!\left(-\frac{(\mu t-(k-1))^{2}}{2t}\right),

and
\mu\geq c
gives the stated simplification. For
t=\lfloor L^{1/4}\rfloor
and
L^{1/4}\geq 2(k-1)/c
,
we have
\mu t-(k-1)\geq ct/2
, giving the final display.
∎

Lemma 66
0Dominated change under one–sided derivative domination).

Let
x,y:[0,T]\to{\mathbb{R}}
be differentiable functions with initial values

x(0)=a
and
y(0)=b
. Assume that for every
t\in[0,T]
,

x^{\prime}(t)\geq 0,\qquad y^{\prime}(t)\leq 0,

and there exists a constant
c>0
such that

x^{\prime}(t)\ \geq\ c\bigl(-y^{\prime}(t)\bigr).

Then for every
t\in[0,T]
,

x(t)-a\ \geq\ c\bigl(b-y(t)\bigr),

equivalently,

x(t)+c\,y(t)\ \geq\ a+c\,b.

Proof.

Define
F(t):=x(t)+c\,y(t)
. Then
F
is differentiable and

F^{\prime}(t)=x^{\prime}(t)+c\,y^{\prime}(t)\ \geq\ 0\qquad\text{for all }t\in[0,T],

by the assumed inequality
x^{\prime}(t)\geq c(-y^{\prime}(t))
. Hence
F
is nondecreasing on
[0,T]
,
so

x(t)+c\,y(t)=F(t)\ \geq\ F(0)=x(0)+c\,y(0)=a+c\,b.

Rearranging gives
x(t)-a\geq c(b-y(t))
, as claimed.
∎

Lemma 67
.

Fix
C>0
,
K>0
,
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,
d>0
, and
D\in\mathbb{R}
.
Let
\alpha\in C^{1}([0,\infty))
satisfy

\dot{\alpha}(t)>K\qquad\text{for all }t\geq 0,

and define

g(t):=\frac{e^{\alpha(t)-D}}{e^{\alpha(t)-D}+C}\in(0,1).

Let
f:[0,\infty)\to(0,1)
be continuous and assume

f(0)=g(0)\qquad\text{and}\qquad f(t)>g(t)\ \ \text{for all }t>0.

If
f(0)<1-\varepsilon
, then there exists
T_{\varepsilon}\in(0,\bar{T}_{\varepsilon})
such that

f(T_{\varepsilon})=1-\varepsilon,

where

\bar{T}_{\varepsilon}:=\frac{1}{K}\log\!\left(\frac{1-\varepsilon}{\varepsilon}\cdot\frac{1-f(0)}{f(0)}\right).

Moreover,

\alpha(T_{\varepsilon})-\alpha(0)\;<\;\log\!\left(\frac{1-\varepsilon}{\varepsilon}\cdot\frac{1-f(0)}{f(0)}\right).

Proof.

Set

\Delta_{\varepsilon}:=\log\!\left(\frac{1-\varepsilon}{\varepsilon}\cdot\frac{1-f(0)}{f(0)}\right),\qquad\bar{T}_{\varepsilon}:=\frac{\Delta_{\varepsilon}}{dK}.

From
f(0)=g(0)=\frac{e^{\alpha(0)-D}}{e^{\alpha(0)-D}+C}
we obtain

e^{\alpha(0)-D}=C\frac{f(0)}{1-f(0)},\qquad\text{hence}\qquad\alpha(0)-D=\log\!\left(C\frac{f(0)}{1-f(0)}\right).

Consequently,

(\alpha(0)-D)+\Delta_{\varepsilon}=\log\!\left(C\frac{f(0)}{1-f(0)}\right)+\log\!\left(\frac{1-\varepsilon}{\varepsilon}\cdot\frac{1-f(0)}{f(0)}\right)=\log\!\left(C\frac{1-\varepsilon}{\varepsilon}\right).

Since
\dot{\alpha}(t)>K
for all
t\geq 0
, integration yields

\alpha(t)-\alpha(0)=\int_{0}^{t}\dot{\alpha}(s)\,ds>Kt\qquad(t>0).

Subtracting
D
gives

\alpha(t)-D>(\alpha(0)-D)+Kt\qquad(t>0).

In particular,

\alpha(\bar{T}_{\varepsilon})-D>(\alpha(0)-D)+K\bar{T}_{\varepsilon}=(\alpha(0)-D)+\Delta_{\varepsilon}=\log\!\left(C\frac{1-\varepsilon}{\varepsilon}\right).

The map
x\mapsto\frac{e^{x}}{e^{x}+C}
is strictly increasing, hence

g(\bar{T}_{\varepsilon})>\frac{e^{\log(C(1-\varepsilon)/\varepsilon)}}{e^{\log(C(1-\varepsilon)/\varepsilon)}+C}=1-\varepsilon.

By assumption
f(t)>g(t)
for
t>0
, thus
f(\bar{T}_{\varepsilon})>g(\bar{T}_{\varepsilon})>1-\varepsilon
, while
f(0)<1-\varepsilon
.
By continuity of
f
, the intermediate value theorem implies the existence of

T_{\varepsilon}\in(0,\bar{T}_{\varepsilon})
such that
f(T_{\varepsilon})=1-\varepsilon
.

Finally, since
T_{\varepsilon}>0
and
f(T_{\varepsilon})>g(T_{\varepsilon})
, we have
g(T_{\varepsilon})<1-\varepsilon
.
By monotonicity of
x\mapsto\frac{e^{x}}{e^{x}+C}
it follows that

\alpha(T_{\varepsilon})-D<\log\!\left(C\frac{1-\varepsilon}{\varepsilon}\right).

Subtracting
\alpha(0)-D=\log\!\left(C\frac{f(0)}{1-f(0)}\right)
yields

\alpha(T_{\varepsilon})-\alpha(0)<\log\!\left(C\frac{1-\varepsilon}{\varepsilon}\right)-\log\!\left(C\frac{f(0)}{1-f(0)}\right)=\log\!\left(\frac{1-\varepsilon}{\varepsilon}\cdot\frac{1-f(0)}{f(0)}\right).

∎

Lemma 68
0Logit computation).

Fix
n\geq 3
. Consider an input sequence
s_{1:L}
sampled autoregressively as described in
Section

4
, such that the prefix
s_{1:2n-2}
contains the multiset of edges
G
of two disjoint directed chains
\mathcal{C}_{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
and
\mathcal{C}_{b}=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{n})
, and all subsequent tokens
s_{2n-1},\ldots,s_{L}
are vertices in
\mathcal{C}_{a}\cup\mathcal{C}_{b}
(in particular, the last token
v=s_{L}
is a vertex).

Assume the transformer’s parameters satisfy:

1.

The attention matrix has the symmetric form
A=A(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
on the vertex–edge block as in
Lemma

61
and
\gamma
on all vertex–vertex blocks.

2.

The value matrix has the following form: there exist
\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that for every edge token
(u,w)\in{\mathcal{E}}
and any output token
k\in\Sigma
,

V_{(u,w),k}=\begin{cases}\alpha^{\prime}&\text{if }k=u,\\
\beta^{\prime}&\text{if }k=w,\\
\gamma^{\prime}&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}\qquad\text{and}\qquad V_{v,:}=0\ \text{for all vertex tokens }v\in{\mathcal{V}}.

For Softmax Attention, let
Z_{v}=\sum_{j=1}^{L}\exp(A_{s_{L},s_{j}})
be the partition function.
We define the weight function
w(\mu)
as:

w(\mu)=\begin{cases}\mu&\text{for Linear Attention},\\
e^{\mu}/Z_{v}&\text{for Softmax Attention}.\end{cases}

Note that for any token
x
, the attention weight from
v
to
x
is given by
w(A_{v,x})
.

Then the logits
H^{(1)}_{L,:}
depend on
v
,
G
, and in the softmax case, the partition function
Z_{v}
(we denote them by
o(v,G,Z_{v})
). In the case of Linear Attention, the dependence on
Z_{v}
is absent, and we simply write
o(v,G)
. Furthermore,
o(v,G,Z_{v})_{k}=\Psi_{k}+C
, where
C
is a constant independent of
k
, we define
\Delta_{\alpha}\coloneqq\alpha^{\prime}-\gamma^{\prime}
,
\Delta_{\beta}\coloneqq\beta^{\prime}-\gamma^{\prime}
, and
\Psi_{k}
is given by the following cases (and symmetrically when swapping
\mathcal{C}_{a}
and
\mathcal{C}_{b}
).

Case 1:
v
is a middle vertex.

If
v=a_{i}
for some
i\in\{3,\ldots,n-2\}
(and symmetrically if
v=b_{i}
), then:

\Psi_{k}=\begin{cases}\Delta_{\alpha}w(\gamma)+\Delta_{\beta}w(\alpha),&k=a_{i+1},\\
\Delta_{\alpha}w(\beta)+\Delta_{\beta}w(\gamma),&k=a_{i-1},\\
\Delta_{\alpha}w(\alpha)+\Delta_{\beta}w(\beta),&k=a_{i},\\
\Delta_{\alpha}w(\gamma),&k\in\{a_{1},b_{1}\},\\
\Delta_{\beta}w(\gamma),&k\in\{a_{n},b_{n}\},\\
(\Delta_{\alpha}+\Delta_{\beta})w(\gamma),&k\in(\mathcal{C}_{a}\cup\mathcal{C}_{b})\setminus\{a_{i-1},a_{i},a_{i+1},a_{1},a_{n},b_{1},b_{n}\},\\
0,&k\in{\mathcal{E}}.\end{cases}

Case 2:
v
is the penultimate vertex.

If
v=a_{n-1}
(and symmetrically if
v=b_{n-1}
), then:

\Psi_{k}=\begin{cases}\Delta_{\beta}w(\alpha),&k=a_{n},\\
\Delta_{\alpha}w(\beta)+\Delta_{\beta}w(\gamma),&k=a_{n-2},\\
\Delta_{\alpha}w(\alpha)+\Delta_{\beta}w(\beta),&k=a_{n-1},\\
\Delta_{\alpha}w(\gamma),&k\in\{a_{1},b_{1}\},\\
\Delta_{\beta}w(\gamma),&k\in\{b_{n}\},\\
(\Delta_{\alpha}+\Delta_{\beta})w(\gamma),&k\in(\mathcal{C}_{a}\cup\mathcal{C}_{b})\setminus\{a_{n-2},a_{n-1},a_{n},a_{1},b_{1},b_{n}\},\\
0,&k\in{\mathcal{E}}.\end{cases}

Case 3:
v
is the second vertex (and not penultimate).

If
m\geq 4
and
v=a_{2}
(and symmetrically if
v=b_{2}
), then:

\Psi_{k}=\begin{cases}\Delta_{\alpha}w(\gamma)+\Delta_{\beta}w(\alpha),&k=a_{3},\\
\Delta_{\alpha}w(\beta),&k=a_{1},\\
\Delta_{\alpha}w(\alpha)+\Delta_{\beta}w(\beta),&k=a_{2},\\
\Delta_{\beta}w(\gamma),&k\in\{a_{n},b_{n}\},\\
\Delta_{\alpha}w(\gamma),&k\in\{b_{1}\},\\
(\Delta_{\alpha}+\Delta_{\beta})w(\gamma),&k\in(\mathcal{C}_{a}\cup\mathcal{C}_{b})\setminus\{a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{n},b_{1},b_{n}\},\\
0,&k\in{\mathcal{E}}.\end{cases}

Case 4:
v
is the first vertex (and not penultimate).

If
m\geq 3
and
v=a_{1}
(and symmetrically if
v=b_{1}
), then:

\Psi_{k}=\begin{cases}\Delta_{\alpha}w(\gamma)+\Delta_{\beta}w(\alpha),&k=a_{2},\\
\Delta_{\alpha}w(\alpha),&k=a_{1},\\
\Delta_{\beta}w(\gamma),&k\in\{a_{n},b_{n}\},\\
\Delta_{\alpha}w(\gamma),&k\in\{b_{1}\},\\
(\Delta_{\alpha}+\Delta_{\beta})w(\gamma),&k\in(\mathcal{C}_{a}\cup\mathcal{C}_{b})\setminus\{a_{1},a_{2},a_{n},b_{1},b_{n}\},\\
0,&k\in{\mathcal{E}}.\end{cases}

Case 5:
v
is a terminal vertex.

If
v=a_{n}
(and symmetrically if
v=b_{n}
), then:

\Psi_{k}=\begin{cases}\Delta_{\beta}w(\beta),&k=a_{n},\\
\Delta_{\alpha}w(\beta)+\Delta_{\beta}w(\gamma),&k=a_{n-1},\\
\Delta_{\alpha}w(\gamma),&k\in\{a_{1},b_{1}\},\\
\Delta_{\beta}w(\gamma),&k\in\{b_{n}\},\\
(\Delta_{\alpha}+\Delta_{\beta})w(\gamma),&k\in(\mathcal{C}_{a}\cup\mathcal{C}_{b})\setminus\{a_{n-1},a_{n},a_{1},b_{1},b_{n}\},\\
0,&k\in{\mathcal{E}}.\end{cases}

Proof.

Let
s_{1:L}
denote the input sequence with
s_{L}=v
.
From the transformer architecture (
Section

3.2
), the output logits at position
L
are given by the attention-weighted sum of value vectors. Specifically:

H^{(1)}_{L,:}=\sum_{j=1}^{L}\text{weight}(s_{L},s_{j})V_{s_{j},:},

where
\text{weight}(s_{L},s_{j})
is the attention weight of token
s_{j}
when attending from
s_{L}
.
Using our definition of
w(\mu)
and the attention matrix entries
A_{s_{L},s_{j}}
, this weight is exactly
w(A_{s_{L},s_{j}})
.

By our assumption on the value matrix,
V_{s_{j},:}=0
whenever
s_{j}
is a vertex token.
Since the sequence
s_{1:L}
consists of edge tokens (the set
G
) and vertex tokens, the sum over
j=1,\ldots,L
restricts to the indices corresponding to edges in
G
:

H^{(1)}_{L,:}=\sum_{e\in G}w(A_{v,e})V_{e,:}.

This expression confirms that the logits depend on
v
,
G
, and implicitly on the context via
Z_{v}
(in the Softmax case), justifying the notation
o(v,G,Z_{v})
.

For a specific output token
k\in\Sigma
, we have:

o(v,G,Z_{v})_{k}=\sum_{e\in G}w(A_{v,e})V_{e,k}.

Using the symmetric structure of
V
where
V_{e,k}=\alpha^{\prime}
if
k
is the source of
e
,
\beta^{\prime}
if
k
is the target of
e
, and
\gamma^{\prime}
otherwise (including when
k
is an edge token), we substitute:

\displaystyle o(v,G,Z_{v})_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{e=(u,w)\in G}w(A_{v,e})\left(\alpha^{\prime}\mathbbm{1}\{k=u\}+\beta^{\prime}\mathbbm{1}\{k=w\}+\gamma^{\prime}\mathbbm{1}\{k\notin\{u,w\}\}\right)

\displaystyle=\alpha^{\prime}\sum_{e\in G,\text{src}(e)=k}w(A_{v,e})+\beta^{\prime}\sum_{e\in G,\text{tgt}(e)=k}w(A_{v,e})+\gamma^{\prime}\sum_{e\in G,k\notin e}w(A_{v,e}).

Let
W_{out}(v,k)=\sum_{e\in G,\text{src}(e)=k}w(A_{v,e})
be the total attention weight on edges outgoing from
k
, and
W_{in}(v,k)=\sum_{e\in G,\text{tgt}(e)=k}w(A_{v,e})
be the total weight on edges incoming to
k
.
The total weight is
W_{total}(v)=\sum_{e\in G}w(A_{v,e})
.
We can rewrite the third term using the identity
\mathbbm{1}\{k\notin e\}=1-\mathbbm{1}\{k=\text{src}(e)\}-\mathbbm{1}\{k=\text{tgt}(e)\}
:

\displaystyle\gamma^{\prime}\sum_{e\in G,k\notin e}w(A_{v,e})

\displaystyle=\gamma^{\prime}\left(W_{total}(v)-W_{out}(v,k)-W_{in}(v,k)\right).

Substituting this back:

\displaystyle o(v,G,Z_{v})_{k}

\displaystyle=\alpha^{\prime}W_{out}(v,k)+\beta^{\prime}W_{in}(v,k)+\gamma^{\prime}\left(W_{total}(v)-W_{out}(v,k)-W_{in}(v,k)\right)

\displaystyle=(\alpha^{\prime}-\gamma^{\prime})W_{out}(v,k)+(\beta^{\prime}-\gamma^{\prime})W_{in}(v,k)+\gamma^{\prime}W_{total}(v).

Defining
\Delta_{\alpha}=\alpha^{\prime}-\gamma^{\prime}
,
\Delta_{\beta}=\beta^{\prime}-\gamma^{\prime}
, and
C=\gamma^{\prime}W_{total}(v)
(which is independent of
k
), we get:

o(v,G,Z_{v})_{k}=\Delta_{\alpha}W_{out}(v,k)+\Delta_{\beta}W_{in}(v,k)+C.

We define
\Psi_{k}=\Delta_{\alpha}W_{out}(v,k)+\Delta_{\beta}W_{in}(v,k)
.
It remains to compute
W_{out}(v,k)
and
W_{in}(v,k)
in each of the configurations listed in the lemma statement. Since
G
is the disjoint union of two directed chains, each
k\in(\mathcal{C}_{a}\cup\mathcal{C}_{b})
has at most one outgoing edge
(k,\mathrm{succ}(k))
(absent iff
k\in\{a_{n},b_{n}\}
) and at most one incoming edge
(\mathrm{pred}(k),k)
(absent iff
k\in\{a_{1},b_{1}\}
). For such edges, by the assumed form of
A(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
on the vertex–edge block,

A_{v,(k,\mathrm{succ}(k))}=\begin{cases}\alpha,&v=k,\\
\beta,&v=\mathrm{succ}(k),\\
\gamma,&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}\qquad A_{v,(\mathrm{pred}(k),k)}=\begin{cases}\alpha,&v=\mathrm{pred}(k),\\
\beta,&v=k,\\
\gamma,&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Applying
w(\cdot)
and substituting into
\Psi_{k}=\Delta_{\alpha}W_{out}(v,k)+\Delta_{\beta}W_{in}(v,k)
gives exactly the case-by-case expressions stated in the lemma (with the
\mathcal{C}_{b}
cases obtained by symmetry).
∎

Appendix H
Experimental Details

H.1
Theoretically Inspired Experiments

We provide full details for the experiments described in
Section

7.1
.
We use a single-layer transformer with attention matrix
A\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|\Sigma|\times|\Sigma|}
and value matrix
V\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|\Sigma|\times|\Sigma|}
, as defined in
Section

3.2
.
We initialize both matrices with small random values sampled from a normal distribution with standard deviation
10^{-8}
.
We optimize
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D},\theta)
using the REINFORCE algorithm
(
67
)
with the advantage function computed as the difference between the reward and the average reward across the batch.
We use the Adam optimizer with learning rate
0.01
.
For each chain size, we train on
1{,}000{,}000
samples over
300
epochs with batch size
50{,}000
, and validate on
1{,}000
samples.
During evaluation, we measure test accuracy and chain traversal correctness using greedy decoding (temperature
0
).
For experiments with a single chain size (see
Sections

7.1.1
and
7.1.3
), we set max new tokens to
n+1
where
n
is the chain size; for generalization experiments across multiple chain sizes (see
Section

7.1.2
), we use
\max(\text{test chain sizes})+1
.

H.2
Real World Experiments

We provide full details for the experiments described in
Section

7.2
.
We fine-tune Qwen2.5 3B
(
51
)
(
https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct
) using GRPO
(
58
)
.
We use a KL coefficient
\beta=0.0001
, generate
8
completions per prompt with sampling temperature
0.9
during training, and set max completion length to
1024
tokens.
We optimize with AdamW using learning rate
5\times 10^{-7}
with a cosine learning rate scheduler and warmup ratio of
0.03
.
We use a batch size of
1
per device with
8
gradient accumulation steps, training for
600
steps.
Evaluation is performed over 1000 samples for the 15-Hard evaluations, and 1008 for the 15-Uniform evaluation (this amounts to 72 samples per reasoning-length).
All test evaluations use greedy decoding (temperature
0
).
The prompt used for fine-tuning and evaluation is shown in
Figure

4
.
The code for the real-world experiments is based on https://github.com/philschmid/deep-learning-pytorch-huggingface/blob/main/training/mini-deepseek-r1-aha-grpo.ipynb

[{

"role": "system",

"content": "You are a helpful assistant that can solve arithmetic equations systems."

},

{

"role": "user",

"content": f"Given this equation system: {input_problem}\n\n

Find the value of the target variable. Show

your reasoning in <think> </think> tags,

and provide your final answer in <answer> </answer>

tags, for example <answer>12</answer>."

},

{

"role": "assistant",

"content": "I’ll think about the equation system and solve it.\n<think>"

}]

Figure 4:
Prompt template used for fine-tuning and evaluation in the real-world experiments. The
{input_problem}
placeholder is replaced with the equation system at runtime.

H.2.1
LLM Annotation for Efficient Reasoning

To verify that the LLM learns the efficient step-by-step reasoning algorithm described in
Section

7.2.2
, we employ automated LLM annotation.
We use GPT-5.1 with reasoning effort set to “low” to classify whether each solution follows the target algorithm.
We sample
100
correct solutions from the
15
-Uniform evaluation set and apply binary classification to each.
The judge LLM is provided with the system prompt shown in
Figure

5
.
See
Section

H.2.2
for examples of solutions that follow this algorithm.

You are a strict binary classifier.

Given:

(A) a system of equations and a request to find a target variable, and

(B) a solution writeup produced by a model,

Output True iff the writeup’s solving strategy is:

1) backtrace from the target variable through dependencies until

reaching an explicit constant assignment,

2) then compute forward along that dependency chain to obtain the target.

Otherwise output False. Judge the described procedure (not correctness).

Output exactly one word: True or False.

Figure 5:
System prompt provided to GPT-5.1 for classifying whether solutions follow the efficient step-by-step reasoning algorithm.

H.2.2
Example Inputs and Solutions

We provide two randomly generated examples from the
15
-Uniform distribution (see
Section

7.2
) to illustrate the Transformer’s reasoning process.
Each input consists of a shuffled list of affine equations of the form
x_{i}=x_{j}+h_{i}
(where
h_{i}
is uniformly distributed between
-3
and
3
) with one constant assignment (shown in
blue
) and a query for a target variable (shown in
red
).
The Transformer generates its reasoning within
<think>
tags, where it identifies the dependency chain by backtracking from the target to the constant, then calculates values forward step-by-step, and finally outputs the answer within
<answer>
tags.
See
Figure

6
for complete examples.

Example 1:

Input:

x_15 = x_5 + 3. x_5 = x_27 + 2. x_1 = x_24 + 2. x_20 = x_2 + 3. x_2 = x_22 + 3. x_30 = x_3 + 3. x_11 = x_13 + 1. x_26 = x_18 - 3. x_14 = x_20 + 2. x_28 = x_30 - 2. x_16 = x_6 - 3. x_10 = x_23 + 1.
x_8 = 3
. x_4 = x_8 - 2. x_25 = x_7 - 3. x_7 = x_28 + 3. x_23 = x_14 + 2. x_8 = x_17 - 1. x_3 = x_15 - 1. x_21 = x_25 - 2. x_6 = x_4 - 1. x_13 = x_19 + 3. x_29 = x_12 + 3. x_18 = x_16 - 2. x_17 = x_1 + 2. x_24 = x_29 + 1. x_9 = x_11 + 1. x_12 = x_9 - 3. x_27 = x_26 + 1. x_19 = x_10 + 1. Find
x_21
.

Solution:

<think> We need to find x_21. From equation x_21 = x_25 - 2, we need x_25. From x_25 = x_7 - 3, we need x_7. From x_7 = x_28 + 3, we need x_28. From x_28 = x_30 - 2, we need x_30. From x_30 = x_3 + 3, we need x_3. From x_3 = x_15 - 1, we need x_15. From x_15 = x_5 + 3, we need x_5. From x_5 = x_27 + 2, we need x_27. From x_27 = x_26 + 1, we need x_26. From x_26 = x_18 - 3, we need x_18. From x_18 = x_16 - 2, we need x_16. From x_16 = x_6 - 3, we need x_6. From x_6 = x_4 - 1, we need x_4. From x_4 = x_8 - 2, and we know x_8 = 3. Now calculating forward: x_4 = 3 - 2 = 1, x_6 = 1 - 1 = 0, x_16 = 0 - 3 = -3, x_18 = -3 - 2 = -5, x_26 = -5 - 3 = -8, x_27 = -8 + 1 = -7,
…
x_25 = 1 - 3 = -2, x_21 = -2 - 2 = -4. </think> <answer> -4 </answer>

Example 2:

Input:

x_18 = x_8 - 1. x_27 = x_19 + 3. x_7 = x_27 + 2. x_21 = x_24 + 1. x_19 = x_5 - 1. x_13 = x_26 - 3. x_22 = x_9 - 1. x_23 = x_2 + 2. x_5 = x_10 - 1. x_12 = x_20 + 3. x_15 = x_12 - 3. x_10 = x_21 - 1. x_14 = x_7 + 3. x_8 = x_30 - 1. x_20 = x_13 + 3. x_3 = x_28 - 1. x_26 = x_3 - 1. x_1 = x_23 + 1. x_17 = x_25 - 1. x_29 = x_11 - 3.
x_30 = 8
. x_9 = x_4 - 3. x_24 = x_22 + 2. x_16 = x_1 - 3. x_4 = x_29 + 3. x_6 = x_16 - 2. x_28 = x_6 + 2. x_2 = x_18 + 2. x_11 = x_17 + 2. Find
x_15
.

Solution:

<think> First, let’s identify the dependency chain. We need to find x_15, which depends on x_12. From the equations: x_15 = x_12 - 3, x_12 = x_20 + 3, x_20 = x_13 + 3, x_13 = x_26 - 3, x_26 = x_3 - 1, x_3 = x_28 - 1, x_28 = x_6 + 2, x_6 = x_16 - 2, x_16 = x_1 - 3, x_1 = x_23 + 1, x_23 = x_2 + 2, x_2 = x_18 + 2, x_18 = x_8 - 1, x_8 = x_30 - 1. We know x_30 = 8, so we can calculate: x_8 = 8 - 1 = 7, x_18 = 7 - 1 = 6, x_2 = 6 + 2 = 8, x_23 = 8 + 2 = 10,
…
x_12 = 18 + 3 = 21, x_15 = 21 - 3 = 18. </think> <answer> 18 </answer>

Figure 6:
Randomly generated examples from the test set showing input-solution pairs for the mathematical reasoning task. The input contains a shuffled list of affine equations with one constant assignment (shown in
blue
) and asks for the value of a target variable (shown in
red
). The Transformer generates its reasoning process within
<think>
tags, identifying the dependency chain by backtracking from the target to the constant, then calculates values forward step-by-step, and finally outputs the answer within
<answer>
tags.
